Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-Up: Session 1 Retrieval

10 min
Timing
4mFive questions W1 to W5 in the workbook, no notes, then fingers up for each answer
3mMachines that were NOT READY in Session 1: the environment check again, last table on the projector
3mHomework check: hands up for the bookstore sketch and the health check; one learner reads their 1.2 journal error and its fix
Warm-up answers

Answers: W1 B) No: MySQL is the server (a DBMS); a database such as bookstore lives inside it; W2 C) ERROR 1045 (28000): Access denied; W3 A) Rows 1 Rex and 3 Fido; W4 C) DELETE FROM customers WHERE id = 3;; W5 D) Nothing: it describes the client; ask the server with SELECT VERSION();.

  • W1 and W5 are Session 1's two big ideas (server, client, database; ask the server). If more than a few miss W1, draw the three boxes again before the 1.3 frame; it is the frame anyway.
  • W2 is the triage line. Misses: write command not found = PATH · 1045 = answered and refused · 2002/2003 = nothing answered on the board again.
  • W3 and W4 check CRUD from 1.1. A wrong W4 (DROP TABLE or DELETE without WHERE) is the most dangerous misconception in the course: say "WHERE picks the rows" once more.
  • Still NOT READY? Pair the learner with a neighbor for today and book ten minutes at the break. Do not fix installs during the demo.

Lesson Plans

1.3

MySQL CLI & phpMyAdmin

70 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Log in to and out of the mysql client, and read its prompts (mysql>, ->, '>)
  • Explore a server with SHOW DATABASES, USE, SHOW TABLES, DESCRIBE and SELECT DATABASE()
  • Tell SQL statements apart from client commands such as \G, \c, SOURCE and help
  • Save SQL in a .sql file and run it with SOURCE and with mysql < file.sql, knowing what MySQL 9.x's client does differently in scripts
  • Find the Structure, SQL, Import and Export tabs in phpMyAdmin and choose the right tool for a task
Timing in the room
10mFrame: one server, many clients; SQL statements versus client commands; the prompts
25mLive demo: find your way around, \G, two errors on purpose, SOURCE and the shell, phpMyAdmin
20mGuided lab: find_your_way_starter.sql in pairs
15mStart the lesson project: explore.sql, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Point at the three boxes from Session 1: "A client is any program that sends SQL to the server and shows you the answer: the mysql command, phpMyAdmin in a browser, Workbench. The server does not care which one you use: same statement, same result."

Then the two columns on the board: "Most of what you type is SQL, sent to the server: SHOW, USE, DESCRIBE, SELECT. A few things only the mysql program understands: backslash G, SOURCE, help, exit. Those are client commands; phpMyAdmin has never heard of them."

Then the prompts: "Nothing runs until the semicolon. The prompt tells you why the client is waiting: -> it wants the end of the statement, '> a quote is open. Backslash c gives up." Tie it to Session 1's missing-semicolon mistake.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Log in on the projector (mysql -u root -p); learners log in too and type along. A fresh session has no current database. Ask for a prediction before every statement that prints something.

  1. A fresh server (2m). "Logged in, no database chosen yet. What is on this server?" Point at the four system databases: look, never change. 
    SHOW DATABASES;
    +--------------------+
| Database           |
+--------------------+
| information_schema |
| mysql              |
| performance_schema |
| sys                |
+--------------------+
  2. A database that is not there yet (1m). "Which database will we build today? Watch what happens if I go there now." Read the error from the left. 
    USE bookstore;
    ERROR 1049 (42000): Unknown database 'bookstore'
    1049 = no database by that name on this server: a typo, the wrong server, or (here) one you have not created. Lesson 2.1 fixes this at 1:40.
  3. USE, and where am I? (2m). "USE picks the drawer. SELECT DATABASE() answers 'where am I' at any time; before the first USE it says NULL." 
    USE mysql;
SELECT DATABASE();
    Database changed
+------------+
| DATABASE() |
+------------+
| mysql      |
+------------+
  4. What is in this drawer? (2m). "SHOW TABLES on its own lists 38 tables here; LIKE filters the names, % means anything." 
    SHOW TABLES LIKE 'help%';
    +-------------------------+
| Tables_in_mysql (help%) |
+-------------------------+
| help_category           |
| help_keyword            |
| help_relation           |
| help_topic              |
+-------------------------+
  5. What does one table look like? (2m). "DESCRIBE: one row per column. Read the Null, Key and Default columns aloud: NO means required, PRI is the primary key, UNI unique." 
    DESCRIBE help_category;
    +--------------------+-------------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| Field              | Type              | Null | Key | Default | Extra |
+--------------------+-------------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| help_category_id   | smallint unsigned | NO   | PRI | NULL    |       |
| name               | char(64)          | NO   | UNI | NULL    |       |
| parent_category_id | smallint unsigned | YES  |     | NULL    |       |
| url                | text              | NO   |     | NULL    |       |
+--------------------+-------------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
  6. Read some rows (2m). "Type it over several lines on purpose: nothing runs until the semicolon. Watch the prompt change to ->." 
    SELECT User, Host
FROM user
ORDER BY User, Host;
    +------------------+-----------+
| User             | Host      |
+------------------+-----------+
| mysql.infoschema | localhost |
| mysql.session    | localhost |
| mysql.sys        | localhost |
| root             | localhost |
+------------------+-----------+
    The three mysql.* accounts are internal and locked. Docker shows a second root with host %.
  7. A wide row, vertically (2m). "End with backslash G instead of the semicolon: one line per column. That is a client command, not SQL." 
    SELECT User, Host, plugin, account_locked
FROM user
WHERE User = 'root' AND Host = 'localhost'\G
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
          User: root
          Host: localhost
        plugin: caching_sha2_password
account_locked: N
  8. Looking in the wrong drawer (2m). "The error names the database it looked in. That is the hint: where am I?" 
    DESCRIBE customers;
    ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'mysql.customers' doesn't exist
  9. A reserved word as a name (2m). "1064 is the general 'I could not read this'; near '...' points at the spot. CURRENT_TIME is a built-in function." 
    SELECT NOW() AS current_time;
    ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'current_time' at line 1
  10. The fix: another name (1m). "Pick a name that is not a keyword (or use backticks)." 
    SELECT NOW() AS now_time, CURRENT_USER() AS logged_in_as;
    +---------------------+----------------+
| now_time            | logged_in_as   |
+---------------------+----------------+
| 2026-10-16 01:26:20 | root@localhost |
+---------------------+----------------+
    Your time differs, of course.
  11. Run a file: SOURCE and the shell (4m). Open hello.sql (the lesson's three-line script) in your editor next to the terminal. "At the prompt, SOURCE runs every statement in the file. From the shell, the file goes into mysql with <, in batch mode: plain tab-separated text, -t brings the boxes back." Show SOURCE ~/mysql-course/hello.sql, then exit and the two shell lines. 
    SOURCE ~/mysql-course/hello.sql
exit
mysql -u root -p < ~/mysql-course/hello.sql
mysql -t -u root -p < ~/mysql-course/hello.sql
    The lesson shows the outputs. Say the 9.x rule once: in a redirected file the client ignores its own commands (\G, SOURCE) unless started with --commands. PowerShell has no <: cmd /c "...".
  12. phpMyAdmin in two minutes (2m). If you have it (Docker http://localhost:8080): log in with the same root account, expand mysql > user in the navigation panel, show Browse (and the SELECT it ran above the grid), Structure, the SQL tab with the demo's SELECT, Import and Export. Point at the red Drop and Empty buttons. No phpMyAdmin: show the lesson's tour section instead.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Typing the password after -p with a space. mysql -u root -p mypassword asks for the password and then fails with 1049 Unknown database 'mypassword'. Coach: "-p means 'ask me'. The next word is read as a database." (Self-check Q4.)
  • Looking for a table in the wrong database. 1146 names the database it looked in. Coach: "Read the name in the message. Where are you? SELECT DATABASE()."
  • SOURCE with the wrong path. Failed to open file almost always means the client was started in another folder, or Windows backslashes. Coach: forward slashes, and start the client in the course folder (or type the full path).
Guided lab

File: learners open labs/module-02/l1_3-mysql-cli-phpmyadmin/find_your_way_starter.sql; your reference is labs/module-02/l1_3-mysql-cli-phpmyadmin/find_your_way_solution.sql. The setup part builds kit_s2_cafe, the bookstore café's database, the way a coworker might leave it. Learners do not read the setup: they find their way with the statements from the demo.

  • SOURCE it as shipped (2m): SOURCE labs/module-02/l1_3-mysql-cli-phpmyadmin/find_your_way_starter.sql. Everyone sees current_db kit_s2_cafe and six TODO lines.
  • TODO 1 together (2m), TODOs 2 to 6 in pairs (14m). Each TODO replaces one placeholder SELECT with one statement; save, SOURCE again. One learner types, the other writes the answers in the workbook; swap at TODO 4.
  • Expected: three tables (cafe_orders, menu_items, staff_notes); id is the primary key of menu_items and name, price are required (Null NO); ordered_at and paid_at; five time_zone tables; item 3 is Oat Latte, 4.10, vegan 1; staff_notes is InnoDB with utf8mb4_unicode_ci.
  • Circulate for: TODO 3 written as LIKE '%_at' (it still works here, but _ means "any one character": say why \_ is exact); TODO 5 ended with ; instead of \G (one wide horizontal row: ask which is easier to read); TODO 4 answered with USE mysql; (it works, but the file then runs the rest in the wrong database: SHOW TABLES FROM avoids moving).
  • Debrief (2m): "You learned four things about a database you had never seen without reading a line of its code: that is what DESCRIBE and SHOW are for."
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-02/l1_3-mysql-cli-phpmyadmin/system_tour_starter.sql (information_schema questions about the mysql database), then labs/module-02/l1_3-mysql-cli-phpmyadmin/debug_lost.sql (errors 1049, 1146 and two 1064s).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is A Server Report Script: explore.sql, a short script that describes the server, run three ways. In the room: Steps 1 to 3 (15 minutes).

  • Step 1: the orientation query (USER, CURRENT_USER, DATABASE) at the prompt.
  • Step 2: the information_schema count per system database. Read GROUP BY as "count the rows for each database" (Lesson 3.3 teaches it).
  • Step 3: save the file in mysql-course with a header comment, every statement ending in ; and no \G.
  • Done in the room = explore.sql saved and run once with SOURCE without errors.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (run it from the shell, plain and with -t, and explain the difference) and Step 5 (phpMyAdmin's SQL tab, or DESCRIBE in the client).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Is SHOW TABLES; SQL or a client command? Answer: SQL: phpMyAdmin can send it too.
  • The prompt shows ->. What do you type? Answer: ; and Enter (or \c to give up).
  • ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'mysql.customers' doesn't exist: what is the first thing you check? Answer: which database you are in (SELECT DATABASE();): the message says mysql.
  • Why does \G fail in mysql -u root -p < report.sql on 9.7 but not with SOURCE? Answer: a redirected file runs in batch mode, where the 9.x client ignores its own commands unless started with --commands.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q1 C) SOURCE ~/mysql-course/hello.sql; Q2 B) A single quote is still open: close it, then type ;; Q3 5 (time_zone, time_zone_leap_second, time_zone_name, time_zone_transition, time_zone_transition_type); Q4 D) It asks for the password, then fails with ERROR 1049 (42000): Unknown database 'secret123'; Q5 A) mysql -u root -p < report.sql; Q6 B) SHOW COLUMNS FROM mysql.user LIKE '%lock%';. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: a card with the five exploring statements (SHOW DATABASES, USE, SELECT DATABASE(), SHOW TABLES, DESCRIBE) and the four prompts. Learners new to terminals keep SOURCE labs/module-02/... on the board and use tab completion.

Stretch: system_tour_starter.sql and debug_lost.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (a script that uses \G, run with --commands) and the Bonus Challenge (a custom prompt in an option file).

2.1

Creating Databases & Tables

80 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Create, select and drop a database, with the utf8mb4 character set and an explicit collation
  • Write a CREATE TABLE statement with column names, data types and basic constraints
  • Inspect a table with SHOW TABLES, DESCRIBE and SHOW CREATE TABLE
  • Add, rename, change and remove columns with ALTER TABLE, without losing a constraint by accident
  • Rename, empty and drop tables, and use IF EXISTS / IF NOT EXISTS to write scripts you can rerun
  • Read and fix errors 1007, 1046, 1050, 1064 and 1146
Timing in the room
10mFrame: cabinet, drawer, folder; utf8mb4 and a named collation; naming conventions
30mLive demo: build bookstore and books, inspect, IF NOT EXISTS, ALTER and the MODIFY trap, three errors, drop it all
20mGuided lab: events_build_starter.sql
20mBuild the lesson project: create_bookstore.sql, Steps 1 to 5, run twice
Mental model to say

Draw the filing cabinet: "The server is the cabinet. Each database is a drawer: mostly a name and a few settings. Each table is a folder in the drawer, and the data lives in the tables." Point at the four system drawers from the 1.3 demo: "Today we add our own: bookstore."

Then the two settings: "The character set decides which characters can be stored: utf8mb4 is all of Unicode, emoji included. Never write utf8: in MySQL that means a three-byte subset. The collation decides comparing and sorting: is dune equal to Dune? We name utf8mb4_unicode_ci so MySQL 9.7, 8.4 and MariaDB sort the same way."

Then the habit that the whole lesson builds to: "Real databases are built from script files that start clean (DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS) and can run any number of times. If an experiment goes wrong in the next two sessions, your script puts everything back in a second." And the naming rules on one line: lowercase, snake_case, plural tables, id, customer_id, no reserved words.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Type the lesson's statements live; learners type along. This builds a throwaway bookstore on the projector and drops it at the end (Step 13), so the project script after the lab starts from nothing for everyone.

  1. Create the database, named settings (3m). "A database is a drawer: a name and a few settings. utf8mb4 stores every character; the collation decides comparing and sorting. We name it so every server gives the same answers." 
    CREATE DATABASE bookstore
    CHARACTER SET utf8mb4
    COLLATE utf8mb4_unicode_ci;
SHOW CREATE DATABASE bookstore;
    +-----------+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Database  | Create Database                                                                                                                     |
+-----------+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| bookstore | CREATE DATABASE `bookstore` /*!40100 DEFAULT CHARACTER SET utf8mb4 COLLATE utf8mb4_unicode_ci */ /*!80016 DEFAULT ENCRYPTION='N' */ |
+-----------+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
    Point at utf8mb4_unicode_ci inside the versioned comment /*!40100 ... */.
  2. The same name again (1m). "Run it again." 
    CREATE DATABASE bookstore;
    ERROR 1007 (HY000): Can't create database 'bookstore'; database exists
    Fix: IF NOT EXISTS, or drop it first if you meant to start over.
  3. Go there (1m). "Where am I now?" 
    USE bookstore;
SELECT DATABASE();
    Database changed
+------------+
| DATABASE() |
+------------+
| bookstore  |
+------------+
  4. The first table (4m). Type it slowly; read each line as "name, type, rule". "Commas between the columns, none after the last. Types today on trust; Session 3 explains each one." 
    CREATE TABLE books (
    id          INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    title       VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
    author      VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
    price       DECIMAL(8, 2) NOT NULL,
    pages       INT,
    published   DATE,
    in_stock    BOOLEAN DEFAULT TRUE
);
    Success prints only Query OK (0 rows affected): the table exists and is empty.
  5. Look at what MySQL stored (3m). "DESCRIBE is how you check your own work. Where did BOOLEAN go?" (tinyint(1), default 1.) 
    DESCRIBE books;
    +-----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| Field     | Type         | Null | Key | Default | Extra          |
+-----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| id        | int          | NO   | PRI | NULL    | auto_increment |
| title     | varchar(200) | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| author    | varchar(100) | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| price     | decimal(8,2) | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| pages     | int          | YES  |     | NULL    |                |
| published | date         | YES  |     | NULL    |                |
| in_stock  | tinyint(1)   | YES  |     | 1       |                |
+-----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
  6. IF NOT EXISTS keeps the old table (2m). "A smaller definition, IF NOT EXISTS. Did books lose five columns?" Ask for a prediction. 
    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS books (
    id INT PRIMARY KEY
);
SHOW WARNINGS;
    +-------+------+------------------------------+
| Level | Code | Message                      |
+-------+------+------------------------------+
| Note  | 1050 | Table 'books' already exists |
+-------+------+------------------------------+
    No: a Note, and books is untouched. IF NOT EXISTS never updates a table; ALTER TABLE does.
  7. ALTER TABLE: two new columns (3m). "ALTER changes the structure and keeps the rows. AFTER places a column; a DEFAULT fills existing rows." 
    ALTER TABLE books
    ADD COLUMN isbn CHAR(13) AFTER id,
    ADD COLUMN language VARCHAR(30) DEFAULT 'English';
DESCRIBE books;
    +-----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| Field     | Type         | Null | Key | Default | Extra          |
+-----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| id        | int          | NO   | PRI | NULL    | auto_increment |
| isbn      | char(13)     | YES  |     | NULL    |                |
| title     | varchar(200) | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| author    | varchar(100) | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| price     | decimal(8,2) | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| pages     | int          | YES  |     | NULL    |                |
| published | date         | YES  |     | NULL    |                |
| in_stock  | tinyint(1)   | YES  |     | 1       |                |
| language  | varchar(30)  | YES  |     | English |                |
+-----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
  8. The silent trap (3m). "I only want longer titles." Type the MODIFY without NOT NULL; ask the room what DESCRIBE will say before you run the check. 
    ALTER TABLE books MODIFY COLUMN title VARCHAR(250);
SHOW COLUMNS FROM books LIKE 'title';
    +-------+--------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| Field | Type         | Null | Key | Default | Extra |
+-------+--------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| title | varchar(250) | YES  |     | NULL    |       |
+-------+--------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
    No error, no warning, and title now allows NULL. MODIFY (and CHANGE) replace the WHOLE definition: repeat every rule.
  9. Put it back, in one statement (2m). "Several changes, commas between them; MySQL rebuilds the table once." 
    ALTER TABLE books
    MODIFY COLUMN title VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
    DROP COLUMN isbn,
    DROP COLUMN language;
SHOW COLUMNS FROM books LIKE 'title';
    +-------+--------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| Field | Type         | Null | Key | Default | Extra |
+-------+--------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
| title | varchar(200) | NO   |     | NULL    |       |
+-------+--------------+------+-----+---------+-------+
  10. A reserved word as a table name (2m). "A table for orders, named order." 
    CREATE TABLE order (
    id INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY
);
    ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'order (
    id INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY
)' at line 1
    ORDER is a keyword (ORDER BY). The naming rule: tables are plural nouns, so orders.
  11. A misspelled table (1m). "The table is books, plural." 
    DESCRIBE book;
    ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'bookstore.book' doesn't exist
    The message names the database too: bookstore.book.
  12. Copy, rename, look (2m). "LIKE copies the structure and none of the rows: the safe way to practice destructive commands." 
    CREATE TABLE books_copy LIKE books;
RENAME TABLE books_copy TO old_books;
SHOW TABLES;
    +---------------------+
| Tables_in_bookstore |
+---------------------+
| books               |
| old_books           |
+---------------------+
  13. Drop it all (3m). "DROP has no undo. Read the name twice. And after dropping the database you were in, where are you?" 
    DROP TABLE old_books;
DROP DATABASE bookstore;
SELECT DATABASE();
    +------------+
| DATABASE() |
+------------+
| NULL       |
+------------+
    NULL: no current database, so the next CREATE TABLE would fail with 1046. Now everybody builds the real bookstore from a script.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • A comma after the last column, or a missing one between two. Both give 1064, near ) or near the next column's name. Coach: "Read the near '...' part: the mistake is just before it."
  • MODIFY (or CHANGE) without the old rules. No error; NOT NULL or a DEFAULT silently disappears. Coach: "After every ALTER, DESCRIBE. Is Null still NO?" (Self-check Q11, exit ticket 3.)
  • 1046 after DROP DATABASE. Learners drop the database they were in and the next CREATE TABLE fails. Coach: "Where are you? SELECT DATABASE() says NULL: USE one, or write database.table."
Guided lab

File: labs/module-02/l2_1-creating-databases-tables/events_build_starter.sql; reference labs/module-02/l2_1-creating-databases-tables/events_build_solution.sql. The bookstore's events team wants a small database for author readings: learners write the build script from a written spec (two tables), then make it safe to rerun.

  • Step 1 is given (DROP IF EXISTS, CREATE DATABASE with utf8mb4 and the collation, USE). TODO 1 is a skeleton with three gaps; TODO 2 is written from the spec alone; TODO 3 turns on CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS + SHOW WARNINGS.
  • Expected: TODO 3 prints Note 1050 Table 'rooms' already exists; the self-check lists readings and rooms, both utf8mb4_unicode_ci, with 7 and 3 columns (the solution also DESCRIBEs readings). Running the file twice gives the same result: that is the test of a build script.
  • Circulate for: a trailing comma before ) (1064 near )); starts_at DATE instead of DATETIME (no error: ask "where does the 7:30 pm go?"); BOOLEAN DEFAULT 'false' (an error 1067 Invalid default value: FALSE without quotes).
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-02/l2_1-creating-databases-tables/change_request_starter.sql (ALTER TABLE on a table with rows, the MODIFY trap, TRUNCATE resetting the id), then labs/module-02/l2_1-creating-databases-tables/debug_build_script.sql (errors 1007, 1046, 1064, 1050, 1146).
  • Debrief (1m): "Your spec became a script anyone can rerun. The project is the same thing for the bookstore."
Build the lesson project

The lesson project is The Bookstore Build Script: create_bookstore.sql, which rebuilds the empty bookstore from nothing. All five steps happen in the room (20 minutes): Session 3 starts from these three tables.

  • Steps 1 to 3 (12m): the header, DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS bookstore;, the database with utf8mb4 and utf8mb4_unicode_ci, USE, then books, customers and categories from the lesson page, each with a comment. Run each CREATE at the prompt as it is added, so a typo shows up now.
  • Step 4 (3m): the information_schema self-check at the end of the file.
  • Step 5 (5m): SOURCE ~/mysql-course/create_bookstore.sql, then once more. Done = the same self-check table twice: books, categories, customers, all utf8mb4_unicode_ci. Walk the room and look at every screen.
  • Not done by 2:50? The learner finishes at home and emails you the self-check table before Session 3. The kit's labs/_states/schema.sql builds exactly the same three tables, as a fallback at the start of Session 3.
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Why name the collation if utf8mb4 is already the default? Answer: the default collation differs between servers (9.7/8.4 utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci, MariaDB utf8mb4_uca1400_ai_ci); naming one makes every server sort the same way.
  • CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS books (id INT PRIMARY KEY); on the existing seven-column books: what happens? Answer: nothing changes; a Note 1050.
  • TRUNCATE or DELETE: which one starts the ids at 1 again? Answer: TRUNCATE.
  • Why does create_bookstore.sql start with DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS bookstore;? Answer: so it can run any number of times from any state; only on a practice database.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q7 D) The database is created with utf8mb3 (no emoji), and warning 3719 says to use utf8mb4; Q8 email (then id and name); Q9 A) There is no current database: run USE first, or write database.table; Q10 C) book_reviews; Q11 B) The column is now book_title, VARCHAR(250), and allows NULL; Q12 TRUNCATE TABLE customers;. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: learners who fall behind in the project copy the three CREATE TABLE statements from the lesson page (typing them is the goal, but having them matters more today). Pair a fast typist with them for Step 5.

Stretch: change_request_starter.sql and debug_build_script.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (work without USE, with database.table names) and the Bonus Challenge (table and column comments that survive a rebuild).

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Put the three questions on the slide; learners answer in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Sort them before Session 3: question 3's "not yet" pile gets a minute of DESCRIBE in the Session 3 warm-up.

  1. Name two statements that work in phpMyAdmin's SQL tab and one command that only the mysql client understands. Answer: SQL (any client): SHOW TABLES;, DESCRIBE books;, USE bookstore;, SELECT DATABASE();, CREATE TABLE .... Client only: \G, SOURCE file.sql, \c, help, exit, status.
  2. A script stops with ERROR 1046 (3D000): No database selected. Give two ways to fix it. Answer: Put USE bookstore; (the right database) before the statement, or write the full name bookstore.books. It happens in a fresh session and after dropping the database you were in.
  3. title is VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL. You run ALTER TABLE books MODIFY COLUMN title VARCHAR(250);. What did you lose, and which statement shows it? Answer: The NOT NULL rule: MODIFY replaces the whole definition. DESCRIBE books; (or SHOW COLUMNS FROM books LIKE 'title';) shows YES under Null. Fix: repeat it, VARCHAR(250) NOT NULL.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish explore.sql (Lesson 1.3 project Steps 4 and 5: run it with SOURCE, from the shell plain and with -t; phpMyAdmin's SQL tab or DESCRIBE in the client).
  • create_bookstore.sql: if it was not finished in class, finish it and run it twice. Everyone: run it once more from a fresh session (2.1's "Before the Next Lesson") so bookstore holds exactly three empty tables.
  • Lesson exercises. 1.3: Exercises 1 to 3 (privilege tables, one column's details, vertical status); Exercise 4 is a stretch. 2.1: Exercises 1 to 3 (ALTER round trip, a publishers table, fix the broken table); Exercise 4 is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes on the lesson pages (answers are in the Answer Key).
  • Journal: both entries. The 2.1 prompt (a sketch of tables for something they know) comes back in Lesson 2.2's journal, so keep it.
  • Optional: labs/module-02/l1_3-mysql-cli-phpmyadmin/system_tour_starter.sql and labs/module-02/l2_1-creating-databases-tables/change_request_starter.sql for anyone who did not reach them; the two debug handouts.
Bring list for Session 3
  • A laptop on which USE bookstore; SHOW TABLES; lists books, categories and customers (empty), and the root password.
  • create_bookstore.sql in the mysql-course folder (Session 3 reruns it whenever an experiment goes wrong).
  • The 2.1 journal sketch (tables for something you know): Lesson 2.2 asks you to revise its types.
  • The course folder with labs/ (Session 3's starters are in labs/module-03/), the workbook and the journal.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: create_bookstore.sql run twice by everyone; 2.1 demo Steps 1 to 9; the find-your-way lab through TODO 5.
  • Cut first (saves 2m): the phpMyAdmin tour: "the lesson's tour section shows every tab; try it at home".
  • Cut second (saves 6m): 2.1 demo Steps 10 to 12: hand out debug_build_script.sql instead.
  • Cut third (saves 15m): the 1.3 project start: all five steps of explore.sql go home.
Running short: what to add
  • Run debug_build_script.sql for everyone and fix the five errors together, one volunteer per error number.
  • Lesson 2.1 Exercise 3 live: the coworker's broken CREATE TABLE Order Items (...) with three problems.
  • The 1.3 Bonus Challenge together: prompt \u@\d>\_ and watch the prompt follow USE.
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 3: Data Types, Constraints and CRUD

How to teach the third session of MySQL Foundations. Learners see why every column has a type and why rules belong in the database (MySQL refusing bad numbers, money, text, dates and duplicates, each with its own error number), then fill the bookstore with data and change it safely: INSERT, SELECT, UPDATE and DELETE with the preview habit, upserts, safe-updates mode, and a reload script for the data Module 3 queries.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 2.2–2.3 Session 3 of 14 · Part 1

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame → live demo (you type, they follow) → guided lab (a kit lab, new practice) → the lesson's own project. The lesson exercises, quiz and journal are homework.
  • Every SQL block below was run to produce the output under it, on MySQL 9.7.2, inside bookstore as Lesson 2.1's build script leaves it. Ask for a prediction before every statement, especially the ones that fail: predicting the error number is the skill of the day.
  • Keep lesson_05.html and lesson_06.html open for the type tables and the DELETE/TRUNCATE/DROP table. Labs live in labs/module-03/ and run inside bookstore (learners USE bookstore; once).
  • This session has many error numbers. Do not ask learners to memorize them: put the lesson's error table on the board at the start and point at it each time.
  • Session 4 rebuilds bookstore with Lesson 3.1's setup script, so nothing here can leave a learner stuck; the lesson projects (employees.sql, load_bookstore_data.sql) are still worth finishing.
The #1 risk of Session 3: a learner's bookstore is not what the lessons expect

Everything today assumes bookstore with exactly three empty tables. Learners who did not finish create_bookstore.sql, or who left demo tables behind, get different outputs from the first statement on. The warm-up checks it on every laptop (USE bookstore; SHOW TABLES;), and the fix takes ten seconds: rerun create_bookstore.sql, or SOURCE labs/_states/schema.sql (the same three empty tables). The second risk is the 2.2 demo's length: twelve small steps; keep each to its minutes and cut Step 5 (text) first.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Sort the Session 2 exit tickets. Question 3's "not yet" pile (the MODIFY trap) gets W4 in today's warm-up.
  • ☐ Files to hand out: the labs/module-03/ starters and the two debug files (no *_solution.sql). Remind learners to bring create_bookstore.sql.
  • ☐ Run the kit check once: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-03. Every line should say PASS (10 files).
  • ☐ Projector machine: SOURCE labs/_states/schema.sql (bookstore with three empty tables, exactly what learners have), then USE bookstore;. A terminal at 18 pt or larger; browser tabs lesson_05.html, lesson_06.html.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: the error table (1048 NULL given · 1364 left out · 1062 duplicate · 1264 out of range · 1265 not on the list / rounded · 1292 bad date · 1406 too long · 3819 CHECK); and, for 2.3, "SELECT first, same WHERE, read Rows matched".
  • ☐ Print or share: the workbook (warm-up, guided labs, self-checks, exit ticket).
Known environment traps
SymptomCauseFix
ERROR 1046 No database selected in a labThe learner did not USE bookstore; firstUSE bookstore; once per session; the labs never say USE themselves.
ERROR 1146 Table 'bookstore.books' doesn't existbookstore was dropped and not rebuiltRerun create_bookstore.sql or SOURCE labs/_states/schema.sql.
Bad values are stored with only a warningStrict mode is off (some hosts, old XAMPP configs)SELECT @@sql_mode; should list STRICT_TRANS_TABLES; for the session: the lesson's SET SESSION sql_mode = CONCAT(...) line.
AS new gives ERROR 1064MariaDB (XAMPP)ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE description = VALUES(description) (the lesson's version note).
CHECK errors read ERROR 4025 ... CONSTRAINT ... failedMariaDBSame rule, different number: say so and move on.
An UPDATE in the 2.3 demo is refused with 1175The learner started the client with --safe-updates (homework from Lesson 2.3) or forgot SET SQL_SAFE_UPDATES = 0;SET SQL_SAFE_UPDATES = 0; for the session.

In the room. The lesson pages estimate 100 + 105 = 205 minutes for a solo learner; the room has 150. Lesson 2.2 gets 70 minutes: its long reference tables are read at home, the room demos one example of each refusal. Lesson 2.3 gets 80, including its whole project.

Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-Up: Session 2 Retrieval

10 min
Timing
4mFive questions W1 to W5 in the workbook, no notes, then fingers up
3mEvery laptop: USE bookstore; SHOW TABLES; lists books, categories, customers. Anyone else reruns create_bookstore.sql (or SOURCE labs/_states/schema.sql) now
3mHomework check: hands up for explore.sql run from the shell; one learner reads a 2.1 journal sketch (it comes back in the 2.2 journal)
Warm-up answers

Answers: W1 B) ; and Enter; W2 C) Which database you are in: the message says mysql, not bookstore; W3 A) DESCRIBE books;; W4 B) YES: MODIFY replaced the whole definition; W5 D) No database selected (1046): USE one, or write database.table.

  • W1 and W3 are the prompt and the SQL-versus-client split from 1.3: quick, and nearly everyone gets them.
  • W2 is reading an error message: the database it names. If it is missed, point at the message and ask "where did MySQL look?"
  • W4 is the MODIFY trap, and it is today's theme in miniature: the database keeps exactly the rules you write.
  • W5 (1046) catches the cabinet picture: the session is not inside any drawer.

Lesson Plans

2.2

Data Types & Constraints

70 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Pick an integer type and decide when UNSIGNED makes sense; explain why money is DECIMAL, never FLOAT
  • Choose between CHAR, VARCHAR and TEXT, and between DATE, DATETIME and TIMESTAMP
  • Use BOOLEAN and ENUM correctly, knowing what MySQL really stores
  • Apply PRIMARY KEY, AUTO_INCREMENT, NOT NULL, UNIQUE, DEFAULT and named CHECK constraints
  • Read the errors strict mode raises for bad values (1048, 1062, 1264, 1265, 1292, 1364, 1406, 3819)
Timing in the room
10mFrame: a type is a promise, a constraint a rule; strict mode refuses instead of guessing
25mLive demo: numbers, money, text, dates, ENUM; the coupons table; the BOOLEAN trap
20mGuided lab: types_matter_starter.sql in pairs
15mStart the lesson project: employees.sql Steps 1 to 4, then Step 6's cleanup
Mental model to say

Draw the lesson's flowchart: a value arrives → does it fit the column's type? → does it pass the constraints? → stored, or the row is rejected with a number. "Every program and every person that writes to this table goes through this door. That is far safer than hoping each one checks."

Then strict mode in one breath: "MySQL 9.7 starts in strict mode: a value that does not fit is an error. Without it MySQL would quietly cut, round or clamp and only warn. Old tutorials show that; some hosts still do it. Keep it on."

Then the rule of thumb on the board: choose the type that says what the value is, then the smallest size that comfortably fits the biggest value you can imagine, then NOT NULL on every column a row cannot do without. Money is DECIMAL; phone numbers and ZIP codes are text.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Inside bookstore (USE bookstore;). The demo creates six small demo tables next to the three real ones and drops them in the last step. Ask for the error number before every refused statement.

  1. One column of each kind of number (3m). "A type is a promise about the values; MySQL keeps it for you." Read the four types aloud. 
    CREATE TABLE number_demo (
    stars     TINYINT,
    copies    SMALLINT UNSIGNED,
    price     DECIMAL(8, 2),
    weight_kg FLOAT
);
INSERT INTO number_demo (stars, copies, price, weight_kg)
VALUES (5, 120, 14.99, 0.1);
SELECT * FROM number_demo;
    +-------+--------+-------+-----------+
| stars | copies | price | weight_kg |
+-------+--------+-------+-----------+
|     5 |    120 | 14.99 |       0.1 |
+-------+--------+-------+-----------+
  2. A number that does not fit (1m). "A TINYINT stops at 127. Predict: what happens to 200?" 
    INSERT INTO number_demo (stars) VALUES (200);
    ERROR 1264 (22003): Out of range value for column 'stars' at row 1
    Strict mode (the default) refuses instead of quietly storing 127.
  3. Too many decimals is different (2m). "Three decimals into a two-decimal column." 
    INSERT INTO number_demo (price) VALUES (12.345);
SHOW WARNINGS;
SELECT price FROM number_demo WHERE stars IS NULL;
    +-------+------+--------------------------------------------+
| Level | Code | Message                                    |
+-------+------+--------------------------------------------+
| Note  | 1265 | Data truncated for column 'price' at row 1 |
+-------+------+--------------------------------------------+
+-------+
| price |
+-------+
| 12.35 |
+-------+
    Rounded, with a Note. Rounding to the column's precision is not an error.
  4. Why money is never FLOAT (3m). "Multiply both by 3. And is the stored 0.1 even equal to 0.1?" 
    SELECT
    price * 3        AS decimal_times_3,
    weight_kg * 3    AS float_times_3,
    weight_kg = 0.1  AS float_equals_0_1
FROM number_demo
WHERE stars = 5;
    +-----------------+---------------------+------------------+
| decimal_times_3 | float_times_3       | float_equals_0_1 |
+-----------------+---------------------+------------------+
|           44.97 | 0.30000000447034836 |                0 |
+-----------------+---------------------+------------------+
    The DECIMAL result is exact; FLOAT stores binary fractions. Over thousands of orders the crumbs become real money.
  5. Text that is too long (1m). "Fourteen characters into VARCHAR(5)." 
    CREATE TABLE text_demo (
    code  CHAR(5),
    label VARCHAR(5)
);
INSERT INTO text_demo (label) VALUES ('Fahrenheit 451');
    ERROR 1406 (22001): Data too long for column 'label' at row 1
    Strict mode refuses instead of storing Fahre.
  6. Dates are checked (2m). "February 30th. Then the date the way an American writes it." 
    CREATE TABLE date_demo (d DATE, dt DATETIME);
INSERT INTO date_demo (d) VALUES ('2026-02-30');
INSERT INTO date_demo (d) VALUES ('03/10/2026');
    ERROR 1292 (22007): Incorrect date value: '2026-02-30' for column 'd' at row 1
ERROR 1292 (22007): Incorrect date value: '03/10/2026' for column 'd' at row 1
    Dates are year first: '2026-03-10'. Spreadsheet dates go in with STR_TO_DATE (the lab uses it).
  7. ENUM: one value from a list (2m). "A default, and a value that is not on the list." 
    CREATE TABLE format_demo (
    title  VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL,
    format ENUM('paperback', 'hardcover', 'ebook') NOT NULL DEFAULT 'paperback'
);
INSERT INTO format_demo (title, format) VALUES ('Dune', 'hardcover');
INSERT INTO format_demo (title) VALUES ('Emma');
INSERT INTO format_demo (title, format) VALUES ('1984', 'audiobook');
SELECT title, format FROM format_demo;
    ERROR 1265 (01000): Data truncated for column 'format' at row 1
+-------+-----------+
| title | format    |
+-------+-----------+
| Dune  | hardcover |
| Emma  | paperback |
+-------+-----------+
    1265 "Data truncated" is how MySQL says the value is not one of the options.
  8. A table with every kind of rule (3m). Read the CREATE line by line: "required, never repeated, a default, and a named CHECK." 
    CREATE TABLE coupons (
    id           INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    code         VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    percent_off  TINYINT UNSIGNED NOT NULL,
    min_order    DECIMAL(8, 2) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0.00,
    expires      DATE,
    CONSTRAINT chk_coupon_percent CHECK (percent_off BETWEEN 5 AND 50)
);
INSERT INTO coupons (code, percent_off, expires) VALUES ('WELCOME10', 10, '2026-12-31');
INSERT INTO coupons (code, percent_off, min_order) VALUES ('BIGBASKET', 20, 50.00);
SELECT * FROM coupons;
    +----+-----------+-------------+-----------+------------+
| id | code      | percent_off | min_order | expires    |
+----+-----------+-------------+-----------+------------+
|  1 | WELCOME10 |          10 |      0.00 | 2026-12-31 |
|  2 | BIGBASKET |          20 |     50.00 | NULL       |
+----+-----------+-------------+-----------+------------+
  9. Break every rule (3m). Ask for the four error numbers BEFORE you run it; write predictions on the board. 
    INSERT INTO coupons (code, percent_off) VALUES (NULL, 15);
INSERT INTO coupons (code) VALUES ('SPRING15');
INSERT INTO coupons (code, percent_off) VALUES ('WELCOME10', 15);
INSERT INTO coupons (code, percent_off) VALUES ('HALFPRICE', 75);
    ERROR 1048 (23000): Column 'code' cannot be null
ERROR 1364 (HY000): Field 'percent_off' doesn't have a default value
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry 'WELCOME10' for key 'coupons.code'
ERROR 3819 (HY000): Check constraint 'chk_coupon_percent' is violated.
    Explicit NULL 1048, left out 1364, duplicate 1062, CHECK 3819 (named, so you know which rule).
  10. The id after failed inserts (2m). "Which id does SUMMER25 get?" 
    INSERT INTO coupons (code, percent_off) VALUES ('SUMMER25', 25);
SELECT LAST_INSERT_ID();
SELECT id, code FROM coupons ORDER BY id;
    +------------------+
| LAST_INSERT_ID() |
+------------------+
|                4 |
+------------------+
+----+-----------+
| id | code      |
+----+-----------+
|  1 | WELCOME10 |
|  2 | BIGBASKET |
|  4 | SUMMER25  |
+----+-----------+
    4, not 3: a failed insert used up an id. Gaps are normal; an id is a label, not a count.
  11. The silent BOOLEAN trap (2m). "BOOLEAN is a TINYINT, so 5 goes in. Which books are in stock?" 
    CREATE TABLE flag_demo (title VARCHAR(50), in_stock BOOLEAN);
INSERT INTO flag_demo VALUES ('Dune', TRUE), ('Emma', FALSE), ('1984', 5);
SELECT title, in_stock FROM flag_demo WHERE in_stock = TRUE;
SELECT title, in_stock FROM flag_demo WHERE in_stock;
    +-------+----------+
| title | in_stock |
+-------+----------+
| Dune  |        1 |
+-------+----------+
+-------+----------+
| title | in_stock |
+-------+----------+
| Dune  |        1 |
| 1984  |        5 |
+-------+----------+
    = TRUE means = 1; 1984 disappears. Test truth with WHERE in_stock.
  12. Clean up (1m). "Back to the three tables Lesson 2.3 expects." 
    DROP TABLE IF EXISTS number_demo, text_demo, date_demo, format_demo, coupons, flag_demo;
SHOW TABLES;
    +---------------------+
| Tables_in_bookstore |
+---------------------+
| books               |
| categories          |
| customers           |
+---------------------+
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Numbers that are really identifiers stored as numbers. A ZIP code or phone as INT loses leading zeros. Coach: "Will you ever add two of these together? No? Then it is text."
  • Constraints forgotten for the obvious columns. Learners write email VARCHAR(100) and stop. Coach with the question: "Does a row make sense without this value? Can two rows share it?"
  • Reading 1265 as a rounding message only. It also means "not on the ENUM list". Coach: "Read the column name in the message: price (rounded) or format (not an option)?"
Guided lab

File: labs/module-03/l2_2-data-types-constraints/types_matter_starter.sql; reference labs/module-03/l2_2-data-types-constraints/types_matter_solution.sql. A delivery list pasted from a spreadsheet with copies as text, cost as FLOAT and dates as MM/DD/YYYY text. As shipped, three of its four answers are wrong and MySQL never complains.

  • Run it as shipped first (3m) and ask pairs which answers are wrong and why: copies sort 9, 15, 120 (text order); the total is 556.9499866962433; the dates sort by month text; the "last" delivery is 12/01/2026.
  • TODOs 1 to 3 in pairs (12m): INT UNSIGNED, DECIMAL(6, 2), DATE, and the dates converted with STR_TO_DATE('03/10/2026', '%m/%d/%Y') (or retyped). A DATE column refuses the US format with 1292: let them hit it once.
  • Expected: Dune 120, Emma 15, 1984 9; total exactly 556.95; 2026-03-10, 2026-12-01, 2027-01-15; last delivery 2027-01-15.
  • Debrief (3m): "Nothing in the first run was an error. Wrong types give wrong answers silently; the right types make MySQL do the right thing."
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-03/l2_2-data-types-constraints/membership_rules_starter.sql (five written rules become constraints), then labs/module-03/l2_2-data-types-constraints/debug_bad_rows.sql (six sign-ups refused with 1048, 1062, 1265, 1292, 1364 and 3819).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is The Staff Table: employees.sql, designed on paper, then proven by trying to break every rule. In the room: Steps 1 to 4 (13 minutes), then Step 6's DROP TABLE (2 minutes).

  • Step 1 on paper in pairs (3m): a type and the rules for each requirement. Compare with Step 2 before typing.
  • Steps 2 and 3: the table and the three staff members; nobody types id or is_active.
  • Step 4: the four INSERTs that must fail; predict each error number first (3819, 1062, 1265, 1364).
  • Before the break: Step 6's DROP TABLE IF EXISTS employees, ...; so bookstore is back to three tables for Lesson 2.3. employees.sql is saved, so nothing is lost.
  • Goes home: Step 5 (COUNT and SHOW CREATE TABLE) and a rerun of the whole file from a fresh session (then Step 6 again).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Why is a phone number VARCHAR and not INT? Answer: it can start with 0 or +, has dashes, can be longer than INT allows, and nobody adds phone numbers.
  • 1048 or 1364: which one is an explicit NULL? Answer: 1048; 1364 is a required column left out.
  • Why name a CHECK constraint? Answer: the error then says which rule failed (chk_salary), instead of employees_chk_1.
  • WHERE in_stock = TRUE misses a row holding 5. What should you write? Answer: WHERE in_stock (any non-zero), and a CHECK if only 0/1 is allowed.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6.

Answers: Q1 A) TINYINT UNSIGNED; Q2 7.01; Q3 C) ERROR 1292 (22007): Incorrect date value: '2026-04-31' for column 'due' at row 1; Q4 D) DATETIME; Q5 B) ERROR 1048: Column 'email' cannot be null; Q6 small, medium, large. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: the lesson's "Choosing the Right Type" table printed as a card; learners pick from it instead of from memory.

Stretch: membership_rules_starter.sql and debug_bad_rows.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (created/updated timestamps) and the Bonus Challenge (a CHECK comparing two columns).

2.3

CRUD Operations

80 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Create rows with INSERT: one row, many rows, with defaults, and read back the new id
  • Read rows with SELECT: chosen columns, aliases, calculated columns and DISTINCT
  • Update and delete exactly the rows you mean, using the "SELECT first" habit and safe-updates mode
  • Write an upsert with INSERT ... AS new ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE, and explain how it differs from REPLACE and INSERT IGNORE
  • Read and fix errors 1054, 1062, 1136 and 1175
Timing in the room
10mFrame: CRUD; WHERE picks the rows; the preview habit
25mLive demo: INSERT, SELECT, UPDATE and DELETE with previews, an upsert, safe-updates, the AND trap
20mGuided lab: new_arrivals_starter.sql
25mBuild the lesson project: load_bookstore_data.sql, Steps 1 to 5, run twice
Mental model to say

Write CRUD on the board with its four statements. Then the lesson's key sentence: "INSERT only ever adds. UPDATE and DELETE act on every row their WHERE matches, and on every row in the table if there is no WHERE at all. There is no undo."

Then the habit, as a three-step recipe: 1 SELECT with the WHERE you mean · 2 the UPDATE or DELETE with exactly the same WHERE · 3 compare the counts (the preview's rows and Rows matched). "Safe-updates mode is the seat belt; the preview is looking before you pull out."

And the upsert in one line: "Insert it, or update it if it is already there": the nightly-import problem every shop has.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Inside bookstore with three empty tables. These blocks keep MySQL's Query OK and Rows matched lines on purpose: read them aloud every time. The demo empties books and categories at the end, so the project starts clean.

  1. Create: one row (2m). "List the columns, then the values in the same order. id and in_stock are left out on purpose." 
    INSERT INTO books (title, author, price, pages, published)
VALUES ('Dune', 'Frank Herbert', 12.99, 412, '1965-08-01');
    Query OK, 1 row affected
  2. Read it back, and the new id (1m). "LAST_INSERT_ID: what a program uses to show the new book's page." 
    SELECT * FROM books;
SELECT LAST_INSERT_ID();
    +----+-------+---------------+-------+-------+------------+----------+
| id | title | author        | price | pages | published  | in_stock |
+----+-------+---------------+-------+-------+------------+----------+
|  1 | Dune  | Frank Herbert | 12.99 |   412 | 1965-08-01 |        1 |
+----+-------+---------------+-------+-------+------------+----------+
+------------------+
| LAST_INSERT_ID() |
+------------------+
|                1 |
+------------------+
  3. Many rows in one statement (2m). "Commas between the value lists. Read the Records line." 
    INSERT INTO books (title, author, price, pages, published, in_stock) VALUES
    ('1984', 'George Orwell', 9.99, 328, '1949-06-08', TRUE),
    ('To Kill a Mockingbird', 'Harper Lee', 11.99, 281, '1960-07-11', TRUE),
    ('The Great Gatsby', 'F. Scott Fitzgerald', 8.99, 180, '1925-04-10', TRUE),
    ('Pride and Prejudice', 'Jane Austen', 7.99, 432, '1813-01-28', TRUE),
    ('The Hobbit', 'J.R.R. Tolkien', 10.99, 310, '1937-09-21', TRUE),
    ('Brave New World', 'Aldous Huxley', 9.49, 311, '1932-01-01', TRUE),
    ('The Catcher in the Rye', 'J.D. Salinger', 8.99, 234, '1951-07-16', FALSE),
    ('Lord of the Rings', 'J.R.R. Tolkien', 15.99, 1178, '1954-07-29', TRUE),
    ('Fahrenheit 451', 'Ray Bradbury', 10.49, 158, '1953-10-19', TRUE);
    Query OK, 9 rows affected
Records: 9  Duplicates: 0  Warnings: 0
  4. Aliases and a calculated column (2m). "A calculation never changes the table." 
    SELECT
    title,
    price,
    ROUND(price * 1.08, 2) AS price_with_tax
FROM books
WHERE price > 11;
    +-----------------------+-------+----------------+
| title                 | price | price_with_tax |
+-----------------------+-------+----------------+
| Dune                  | 12.99 |          14.03 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird | 11.99 |          12.95 |
| Lord of the Rings     | 15.99 |          17.27 |
+-----------------------+-------+----------------+
  5. DISTINCT (1m). "Ten books, how many authors?" 
    SELECT DISTINCT author
FROM books
ORDER BY author;
    +---------------------+
| author              |
+---------------------+
| Aldous Huxley       |
| F. Scott Fitzgerald |
| Frank Herbert       |
| George Orwell       |
| Harper Lee          |
| J.D. Salinger       |
| J.R.R. Tolkien      |
| Jane Austen         |
| Ray Bradbury        |
+---------------------+
  6. Update one row by its id (2m). "Read the second line every time: matched, then changed." 
    UPDATE books
SET price = 14.99
WHERE id = 1;
    Query OK, 1 row affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 1  Warnings: 0
  7. The same update again (1m). "Predict both numbers." 
    UPDATE books
SET price = 14.99
WHERE id = 1;
    Query OK, 0 rows affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 0  Warnings: 0
    Matched 1, changed 0: the price was already 14.99. Matched 0 would mean a typo in the WHERE.
  8. Preview first (2m). "A sale on everything over $10. Before changing anything: which rows?" 
    SELECT id, title, price
FROM books
WHERE price > 10
ORDER BY id;
    +----+-----------------------+-------+
| id | title                 | price |
+----+-----------------------+-------+
|  1 | Dune                  | 14.99 |
|  3 | To Kill a Mockingbird | 11.99 |
|  6 | The Hobbit            | 10.99 |
|  9 | Lord of the Rings     | 15.99 |
| 10 | Fahrenheit 451        | 10.49 |
+----+-----------------------+-------+
  9. Then the same WHERE (1m). "Five previewed, five matched: that is the habit." 
    UPDATE books
SET price = ROUND(price * 0.90, 2)
WHERE price > 10;
    Query OK, 5 rows affected
Rows matched: 5  Changed: 5  Warnings: 0
  10. Delete, and the id is not reused (2m). "Fahrenheit 451 goes and comes back. Which id?" 
    DELETE FROM books WHERE id = 10;
INSERT INTO books (title, author, price, pages, published)
VALUES ('Fahrenheit 451', 'Ray Bradbury', 10.49, 158, '1953-10-19');
SELECT id, title FROM books WHERE title = 'Fahrenheit 451';
    +----+----------------+
| id | title          |
+----+----------------+
| 11 | Fahrenheit 451 |
+----+----------------+
    11, never 10 again: another table, an email or a receipt may still mention old id 10.
  11. Categories, then an upsert (3m). Insert the five categories, then: "Fiction exists. Insert it, or update it if it is there." 
    INSERT INTO categories (name, description) VALUES
    ('Fiction', 'Novels, short stories, and other imaginative works'),
    ('Science Fiction', 'Stories exploring futuristic or scientific concepts'),
    ('Classic', 'Timeless literary works recognized across generations'),
    ('Fantasy', 'Stories set in imaginary worlds with magical elements'),
    ('Dystopian', 'Stories about oppressive societies and dark futures');

INSERT INTO categories (name, description)
VALUES ('Fiction', 'Imaginative prose of every kind') AS new
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE description = new.description;
    Query OK, 2 rows affected
    2 rows affected = the update branch ran (1 = inserted, 0 = unchanged). MariaDB has no AS new: VALUES(description) there.
  12. The safety switch (2m). "The classic slip, with safe-updates on." 
    SET SQL_SAFE_UPDATES = 1;
UPDATE books SET price = 0;
    ERROR 1175 (HY000): You are using safe update mode and you tried to update a table without a WHERE that uses a KEY column.
    Then SET SQL_SAFE_UPDATES = 0; again for the rest of the demo (its WHEREs use non-key columns).
  13. AND instead of a comma (2m). "Dune: 15.99 and out of stock. Predict." 
    UPDATE books
SET price = 15.99 AND in_stock = FALSE
WHERE id = 1;
SELECT title, price, in_stock FROM books WHERE id = 1;
    Query OK, 1 row affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 1  Warnings: 0
+-------+-------+----------+
| title | price | in_stock |
+-------+-------+----------+
| Dune  |  0.00 |        1 |
+-------+-------+----------+
    No error, one row changed, price 0.00 and still in stock: price = (15.99 AND (in_stock = FALSE)). With TEXT values the same slip is refused (1292), as the debug handout shows.
  14. Empty the tables (1m). "The project loads the official data from nothing." 
    TRUNCATE TABLE books;
TRUNCATE TABLE categories;
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Text without quotes (VALUES (Emma, ...) → 1054 Unknown column 'Emma'). Coach: "Without quotes MySQL thinks Emma is a column." A quote inside text is doubled: 'Charlotte''s Web'.
  • Not reading the second line. Learners see Query OK and move on. Coach: "How many rows did the WHERE match? Is that the number your preview showed?"
  • AND in SET. No error with numbers (price becomes 0.00), ERROR 1292 with text. Coach: "Assignments are separated by commas." Then look at the changed row.
Guided lab

File: labs/module-03/l2_3-crud-operations/new_arrivals_starter.sql; reference labs/module-03/l2_3-crud-operations/new_arrivals_solution.sql. Six new books arrive; then a price rise, a sold-out book, a clear-out and one more arrival, each change previewed and counted with ROW_COUNT(). The file empties books at the end (TRUNCATE), so it can run any number of times.

  • Steps 1 and 2 run as shipped: predict LAST_INSERT_ID() after a six-row INSERT first (it is 1: the first row of the batch, not 6).
  • TODOs 3 to 6 (15m): each turned-off statement has one gap. TODO 4's gap is the comma between two assignments: the AND trap in disguise.
  • Expected: preview Moby Dick 12.40 and Little Women 10.20; rows_changed 2; Frankenstein 6.99, in_stock 0; Emma and Frankenstein previewed, rows_deleted 2; Middlemarch gets id 7; final prices 13.02 and 10.71.
  • Circulate for: TODO 3's WHERE different from the preview's (the counts disagree: good, ask why); TODO 5 run before its preview; learners running the lab after their project (its TRUNCATE empties books: rerun load_bookstore_data.sql).
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-03/l2_3-crud-operations/category_feed_starter.sql (a multi-row upsert with ROW_COUNT 3, INSERT IGNORE, REPLACE), then labs/module-03/l2_3-crud-operations/debug_crud_slips.sql (1136, 1054, 1062, 1175, 1292).
Build the lesson project

The lesson project is Stock the Bookstore: load_bookstore_data.sql, which empties the three tables and loads the official 10 books, 8 customers and 5 categories. All five steps in the room (25 minutes).

  • Step 1: header, USE bookstore;, three TRUNCATEs.
  • Steps 2 and 3: the three INSERTs from the lesson page (typing them is fine practice; copying is acceptable if time is short, then check each row).
  • Step 4: the check queries. Done = books ends with id 10 Fahrenheit 451, Gatsby and Catcher show in_stock 0, 8 customers, 5 categories.
  • Step 5: SOURCE it twice; the same result both times. Walk the room and look at every screen.
  • Session 4 starts with Lesson 3.1's setup script, which rebuilds the same data from nothing: a learner who does not finish here is not stuck, but the habit of a reload script is the point.
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • UPDATE books SET price = 0;: what happens in a normal session? Answer: every price becomes 0.00, no undo.
  • You deleted id 10 and inserted a book. Which id? Answer: 11: AUTO_INCREMENT never reuses a number.
  • The upsert says 2 rows affected. Which branch ran? Answer: the update (1 = inserted, 0 = unchanged).
  • Why avoid REPLACE on a row other tables point to? Answer: it deletes and re-inserts: a new id, and the unlisted columns get their defaults.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12.

Answers: Q7 D) It depends on the table's exact column order: an added column breaks it (1136) or shifts values silently; Q8 2; Q9 A) The WHERE found no rows: often a typo in the value or the wrong column; Q10 C) UPDATE books SET price = 9.99 WHERE id = 2;; Q11 B) INSERT ... AS new ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE description = new.description;; Q12 3. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: the three-step recipe (preview, same WHERE, compare counts) on a card next to the keyboard.

Stretch: category_feed_starter.sql and debug_crud_slips.sql; the lesson's Exercise 5 (an idempotent upsert) and the Bonus Challenge (an upsert that adds to a count; note the Answer Key's warning about qualifying the column).

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Put the three questions on the slide; learners answer in the workbook or on a sticky note. Question 3's answers tell you whether the preview habit stuck.

  1. Why is money stored as DECIMAL and never as FLOAT? One sentence and one example. Answer: DECIMAL is exact; FLOAT stores binary fractions, so most decimal values are slightly off and the crumbs add up: 0.1 stored as FLOAT times 3 printed 0.30000000447034836 in the demo, and the lab's FLOAT total was 556.9499866962433 instead of 556.95.
  2. An UPDATE says Rows matched: 3 Changed: 2. What happened to the third row? Answer: The WHERE found it, but it already had the new value, so nothing changed in it. (Matched 0 would mean the WHERE found nothing.)
  3. What do you run before every UPDATE or DELETE that touches more than one row, and why? Answer: A SELECT with exactly the same WHERE: it shows which rows will be touched, and its row count must match the UPDATE's Rows matched. Safe-updates mode (SET SQL_SAFE_UPDATES = 1; or mysql --safe-updates) is the second net.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish employees.sql (Lesson 2.2 project Step 5, then rerun the whole file and Step 6's cleanup so bookstore is back to three tables).
  • load_bookstore_data.sql: if it was not finished in class, finish it; everyone runs it once more (2.3's "Before the Next Lesson").
  • Lesson exercises. 2.2: Exercises 1 to 3 (pick the type, prove DECIMAL rounds, fix the bad table); Exercise 4 is a stretch. 2.3: Exercises 1 to 4 (add, read, update and remove, each putting the data back); Exercise 5 is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes on the lesson pages (answers in the Answer Key).
  • Journal: both entries; the 2.2 prompt revises the 2.1 sketch.
  • Try starting the client with --safe-updates for a week.
  • Optional: labs/module-03/l2_2-data-types-constraints/membership_rules_starter.sql, labs/module-03/l2_3-crud-operations/category_feed_starter.sql and the two debug handouts.
Bring list for Session 4
  • A laptop with bookstore loaded (10 books, 8 customers, 5 categories), the root password.
  • create_bookstore.sql and load_bookstore_data.sql in the mysql-course folder.
  • The course folder with labs/ (Session 4's starters are in labs/module-04/), the workbook and the journal.
  • Your notes from Sessions 1 to 3: Session 4 ends with Checkpoint 1 on Modules 1 and 2 (30 minutes, open notes).

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: the warm-up's bookstore check; 2.2 demo Steps 4, 8 and 9; 2.3 demo Steps 8, 9 and 14; the load_bookstore_data project.
  • Cut first (saves 1m): 2.2 demo Step 5 (text too long): the lesson page shows it.
  • Cut second (saves 3m): 2.3 demo Step 11 (the upsert): "the lesson's upsert section, and the fast finishers' lab".
  • Cut third (saves 15m): the 2.2 project start: all six steps of employees.sql go home.
Running short: what to add

  • Lesson 2.2 Exercise 3 live: the coworker's Orders table with almost every column wrong.
  • Lesson 2.3 Exercise 5 live: run the Horror upsert three times and read the three counts.
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 4: Filtering, Sorting and Limiting

How to teach the fourth session of MySQL Foundations. Learners filter rows precisely (comparisons, AND/OR with parentheses, IN, BETWEEN, LIKE and the rules for NULL), sort and page results so they come out the same every time, and close Modules 1 and 2 with Checkpoint 1, a scored 30-minute task: build, fill and read a small database from nothing.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 3.1–3.2 Checkpoint 1 Session 4 of 14 · Part 1

How to Use This Guide

  • Two lessons in shorter blocks (65 and 55 minutes) because the 30 minutes before the wrap-up are Checkpoint 1, a scored open-notes task on Modules 1 and 2 (Lessons 1.1 to 2.3).
  • Every SQL block below was run to produce the output under it, on MySQL 9.7.2, on the Module 3 data (Lesson 3.1's setup script = labs/_states/flat.sql). Ask for a row count before every query: counting first is how silent filter mistakes get caught.
  • Keep lesson_07.html and lesson_08.html open. Labs live in labs/module-04/; the lesson labs are read-only and run inside bookstore.
  • Part 1 (Sessions 1 to 7) can run on its own. Teal blocks are hands-on, amber blocks are watch-outs.
The #1 risk of Session 4: running out of time for the checkpoint

Both lessons are dense, and a room of laptops is slower than your demo. Protect the 2:20 start of Checkpoint 1. If 3.1 runs over, cut its project start (send all seven steps home); if 3.2 runs over, cut the lab to TODOs 1 to 4 and the project start. The second risk is data: anyone whose bookstore is not the Module 3 data gets different rows all session. The warm-up loads it on every laptop.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Sort the Session 3 exit tickets. Question 3 (the preview habit) tells you who needs Part B's preview in the checkpoint pointed out.
  • ☐ Files to hand out: the labs/module-04/ starters and debug files; hold back checkpoint-1/checkpoint_starter.sql until 2:20 (print the workbook's Checkpoint 1 page or share it at that moment). Keep checkpoint_solution.sql and the rubric (Answer Key) to yourself.
  • ☐ Run the kit check once: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-04. Every line should say PASS (12 files).
  • ☐ Projector machine: SOURCE labs/_states/flat.sql (it ends in bookstore). Browser tabs lesson_07.html, lesson_08.html.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: the evaluation order (comparisons → NOT → AND → OR), "NULL: IS NULL, never = NULL", and the clause order SELECT · FROM · WHERE · ORDER BY · LIMIT.
  • ☐ Checkpoint logistics: decide how learners hand in (their checkpoint_starter.sql by email or USB, plus the workbook page for Part D). Open notes means workbook, scripts and lesson pages; no neighbors, no AI assistants.
