Lesson Plans

3.1

WHERE Clauses, Operators & Filtering

65 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Filter rows by numbers, text and dates with the comparison operators (=, !=, <, >, <=, >=)
  • Combine conditions with AND, OR and NOT, and use parentheses to say exactly what you mean
  • Match lists, ranges and text patterns with IN, BETWEEN and LIKE
  • Find missing values with IS NULL and explain why = NULL finds nothing
  • Spot the silent filtering mistakes (missing NULL rows, AND/OR mix-ups) and fix error 1054
  • Save a set of reusable searches in a .sql script
Timing in the room
10mFrame: a test on every row; true, false, unknown
25mLive demo: comparisons to precedence, two mistakes
20mGuided lab: staff_requests_starter.sql in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: saved_searches.sql, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Draw the lesson's diagram: each row of books → the test → kept (true) or dropped (false, or unknown). "WHERE is a test MySQL runs on every row. The third answer, unknown, appears whenever a NULL is involved, and unknown is dropped like false."

Then the counting habit: "Before you run a filter, say how many rows you expect. Three of today's mistakes give no error at all; the count is how you catch them."

Then precedence as arithmetic: "Multiplication before addition; AND before OR. When you mix them, type the parentheses."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Inside bookstore with the Module 3 data. Ask for the row count before each query.

  1. WHERE as a test on every row (2m). "Five of ten pass. The ORDER BY makes the result read the same everywhere: Lesson 3.2." 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
WHERE price < 10
ORDER BY price, title;
    +------------------------+-------+
| title                  | price |
+------------------------+-------+
| Pride and Prejudice    |  7.99 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |  8.99 |
| The Great Gatsby       |  8.99 |
| Brave New World        |  9.49 |
| 1984                   |  9.99 |
+------------------------+-------+
  2. See the test itself (2m). "A condition is a value: 1, 0 or NULL. Put it in the SELECT list to debug a WHERE." 
    SELECT title, price, price < 10 AS passes
FROM books
ORDER BY id
LIMIT 4;
    +-----------------------+-------+--------+
| title                 | price | passes |
+-----------------------+-------+--------+
| Dune                  | 14.99 |      0 |
| 1984                  |  9.99 |      1 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird | 11.99 |      0 |
| The Great Gatsby      |  8.99 |      1 |
+-----------------------+-------+--------+
  3. Text ignores case (1m). "The collation is _ci: case-insensitive." 
    SELECT title
FROM books
WHERE author = 'j.r.r. tolkien'
ORDER BY title;
    +-------------------+
| title             |
+-------------------+
| Lord of the Rings |
| The Hobbit        |
+-------------------+
  4. Not equal, and two missing customers (2m). "Eight customers, two in New York. Count the rows." 
    SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
WHERE city != 'New York'
ORDER BY first_name;
    +------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Bob        | Chicago     |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
| Dave       | Houston     |
| Grace      | Chicago     |
+------------+-------------+
    Four. Eve and Hank are gone: an unknown city is not "different from New York". Leave the question open until Step 12.
  5. AND narrows (1m). "Cheap AND long." 
    SELECT title, price, pages
FROM books
WHERE price < 10 AND pages > 300
ORDER BY price;
    +---------------------+-------+-------+
| title               | price | pages |
+---------------------+-------+-------+
| Pride and Prejudice |  7.99 |   432 |
| Brave New World     |  9.49 |   311 |
| 1984                |  9.99 |   328 |
+---------------------+-------+-------+
  6. OR widens (1m). "Cheap OR short. Gatsby passes both: does it appear twice?" 
    SELECT title, price, pages
FROM books
WHERE price < 9 OR pages < 200
ORDER BY title;
    +------------------------+-------+-------+
| title                  | price | pages |
+------------------------+-------+-------+
| Fahrenheit 451         | 10.49 |   158 |
| Pride and Prejudice    |  7.99 |   432 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |  8.99 |   234 |
| The Great Gatsby       |  8.99 |   180 |
+------------------------+-------+-------+
  7. IN: a list (1m). "Three ORs on one column, said once." 
    SELECT title, author
FROM books
WHERE author IN ('George Orwell', 'Aldous Huxley', 'Ray Bradbury')
ORDER BY author;
    +-----------------+---------------+
| title           | author        |
+-----------------+---------------+
| Brave New World | Aldous Huxley |
| 1984            | George Orwell |
| Fahrenheit 451  | Ray Bradbury  |
+-----------------+---------------+
  8. BETWEEN includes both ends (1m). "Are 9.49 and 10.99 in?" 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
WHERE price BETWEEN 9.49 AND 10.99
ORDER BY price;
    +-----------------+-------+
| title           | price |
+-----------------+-------+
| Brave New World |  9.49 |
| 1984            |  9.99 |
| Fahrenheit 451  | 10.49 |
| The Hobbit      | 10.99 |
+-----------------+-------+
  9. A month of timestamps (2m). "created_at has a time. Half-open: on or after the first, before the first of next month." 
    SELECT first_name, created_at
FROM customers
WHERE created_at >= '2026-01-01'
  AND created_at <  '2026-02-01'
ORDER BY created_at;
    +------------+---------------------+
| first_name | created_at          |
+------------+---------------------+
| Alice      | 2026-01-04 10:15:00 |
| Bob        | 2026-01-06 14:02:00 |
| Carol      | 2026-01-09 09:40:00 |
| Dave       | 2026-01-12 16:25:00 |
| Eve        | 2026-01-20 11:08:00 |
| Frank      | 2026-01-27 13:30:00 |
+------------+---------------------+
  10. LIKE: contains (2m). "% is any number of characters. Lowercase 'the' finds The too." 
    SELECT title
FROM books
WHERE title LIKE '%the%'
ORDER BY title;
    +------------------------+
| title                  |
+------------------------+
| Lord of the Rings      |
| The Catcher in the Rye |
| The Great Gatsby       |
| The Hobbit             |
+------------------------+
  11. NULL is unknown (2m). "Is NULL equal to NULL?" Let them guess before you run it. 
    SELECT
    NULL = NULL   AS equals,
    NULL != 'x'   AS not_equals,
    NULL IS NULL  AS is_null;
    +--------+------------+---------+
| equals | not_equals | is_null |
+--------+------------+---------+
|   NULL |       NULL |       1 |
+--------+------------+---------+
  12. Keep the NULL rows on purpose (2m). "Back to Step 4: say what the empty rows should do." 
    SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
WHERE city != 'New York' OR city IS NULL
ORDER BY first_name;
    +------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Bob        | Chicago     |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
| Dave       | Houston     |
| Eve        | NULL        |
| Grace      | Chicago     |
| Hank       | NULL        |
+------------+-------------+
  13. AND before OR (2m). "Tolkien or Orwell books under $12, written the way we say it." 
    SELECT title, author, price
FROM books
WHERE author = 'J.R.R. Tolkien' OR author = 'George Orwell' AND price < 12
ORDER BY price;
    +-------------------+----------------+-------+
| title             | author         | price |
+-------------------+----------------+-------+
| 1984              | George Orwell  |  9.99 |
| The Hobbit        | J.R.R. Tolkien | 10.99 |
| Lord of the Rings | J.R.R. Tolkien | 15.99 |
+-------------------+----------------+-------+
    Lord of the Rings at 15.99 is in it: MySQL read A OR (B AND C).
  14. Parentheses say what you meant (1m). 
    SELECT title, author, price
FROM books
WHERE (author = 'J.R.R. Tolkien' OR author = 'George Orwell')
  AND price < 12
ORDER BY price;
    +------------+----------------+-------+
| title      | author         | price |
+------------+----------------+-------+
| 1984       | George Orwell  |  9.99 |
| The Hobbit | J.R.R. Tolkien | 10.99 |
+------------+----------------+-------+
  15. Two mistakes (2m). "Text without quotes; and = NULL." 
    SELECT title FROM books WHERE author = Tolkien;
SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
WHERE city = NULL;
    ERROR 1054 (42S22): Unknown column 'Tolkien' in 'where clause'
Empty set
    The second is silent: no error, no rows. IS NULL.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • = NULL or != that drops the NULL rows. Coach: "What happens to Eve and Hank in this test?" Show the condition as a column.
  • AND/OR without parentheses. Coach: "Read it the way MySQL does: AND first. Where would you put the brackets?"
  • Text without quotes (1054) and wildcards with = (silent). Coach: "Quotes for text; % only means something to LIKE."
Guided lab

File: labs/module-04/l3_1-where-clauses-operators-filtering/staff_requests_starter.sql; reference labs/module-04/l3_1-where-clauses-operators-filtering/staff_requests_solution.sql. Seven requests from the staff, each a ready SELECT whose WHERE says FALSE (Empty set).

  • Request 1 together (3m), 2 to 7 in pairs (14m): count the expected rows from the lesson's two tables first, then write the WHERE.
  • Expected: 3 · 3 · 2 (Johnson, Wilson) · 2 (1984, Dune) · 6 · 3 · 2 (Grace, Hank). Request 5 is the NULL trap (4 without OR city IS NULL); request 7 the half-open range.
  • Circulate for: request 2 with '1950' (works by accident: compare with a full date); request 4 with LIKE '%____%' (every title); request 6 with OR instead of AND before the Tolkien test.
  • Debrief (3m): which request would have been wrong without an error? (5, and 6 if mis-bracketed.)
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-04/l3_1-where-clauses-operators-filtering/null_detective_starter.sql, then labs/module-04/l3_1-where-clauses-operators-filtering/debug_filters.sql.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is Saved Searches: saved_searches.sql, six commented searches the staff can rerun. In the room: Steps 1 to 3 (10 minutes).

  • Steps 1 to 3: gift ideas, long reads, mid-century shelf, each run as it is added.
  • Done in the room = three searches saved and run with SOURCE.
  • Goes home: Steps 4 to 7 (title search, follow-up list, flyer, run the whole file).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud:

  • NULL = NULL: true, false or unknown? Answer: unknown (NULL).
  • BETWEEN 9.49 AND 10.99: are the ends in? Answer: yes, both.
  • Why a half-open range for created_at? Answer: BETWEEN '...-01' AND '...-31' stops at midnight of the 31st and misses that day.
  • 'The%' or 'The %'? Answer: the space keeps out words like Theory.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6.

Answers: Q1 B) 4; Q2 4; Q3 A) WHERE author NOT IN ('J.R.R. Tolkien', 'George Orwell'); Q4 C) WHERE created_at >= '2026-01-27' AND created_at < '2026-01-28'; Q5 Alice, Frank, Grace; Q6 D) WHERE city NOT IN ('Reno', NULL). Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: a card with the operator table and "NULL → IS NULL".

Stretch: null_detective_starter.sql and debug_filters.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (the picky customer) and the REGEXP Bonus Challenge.

3.2

Sorting & Limiting Results

55 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Sort results with ORDER BY on one or more columns or expressions, each ascending or descending
  • Predict where NULL values land in a sort and move them to the end
  • Take the top N rows with LIMIT, and add a tie-breaker so the result is always the same
  • Split a result into pages with LIMIT ... OFFSET, and explain when keyset pagination is better
  • Read and fix the errors MySQL raises for misplaced or malformed ORDER BY and LIMIT clauses
Timing in the room
10mFrame: a table has no order; sort keys and tie-breakers; LIMIT runs last
20mLive demo: ties, directions, alias, CASE, NULLs, top N, pages, keyset, three mistakes
15mGuided lab: bestseller_board_starter.sql
10mStart the lesson project: storefront.sql, Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

"A table is a set of rows, not a list. Without ORDER BY you get whatever is cheapest for the server, which usually looks right until one day it is not." Then the phone book: sorted by last name, and by first name only among people with the same last name.

Then the run order on the board: FROM → WHERE → SELECT → ORDER BY → LIMIT. "That is why an alias works in ORDER BY, and why LIMIT cuts the sorted result." And the rule: every ORDER BY that feeds a LIMIT ends with a unique column.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Same data; read-only.

  1. Ties without a tie-breaker (2m). "Cheapest first. Two books cost 8.99: which comes first, and will it always?" 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
ORDER BY price;
    +------------------------+-------+
| title                  | price |
+------------------------+-------+
| Pride and Prejudice    |  7.99 |
| The Great Gatsby       |  8.99 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |  8.99 |
| Brave New World        |  9.49 |
| 1984                   |  9.99 |
| Fahrenheit 451         | 10.49 |
| The Hobbit             | 10.99 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird  | 11.99 |
| Dune                   | 14.99 |
| Lord of the Rings      | 15.99 |
+------------------------+-------+
    Here Gatsby came first; nothing promises it. Lesson 3.2's card says the same.
  2. A tie-breaker (1m). 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
ORDER BY price, title;
    +------------------------+-------+
| title                  | price |
+------------------------+-------+
| Pride and Prejudice    |  7.99 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |  8.99 |
| The Great Gatsby       |  8.99 |
| Brave New World        |  9.49 |
| 1984                   |  9.99 |
| Fahrenheit 451         | 10.49 |
| The Hobbit             | 10.99 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird  | 11.99 |
| Dune                   | 14.99 |
| Lord of the Rings      | 15.99 |
+------------------------+-------+
  3. A direction per column (2m). "In stock first (1 before 0, so DESC), then cheapest." 
    SELECT title, in_stock, price
FROM books
ORDER BY in_stock DESC, price, title;
    +------------------------+----------+-------+
| title                  | in_stock | price |
+------------------------+----------+-------+
| Pride and Prejudice    |        1 |  7.99 |
| Brave New World        |        1 |  9.49 |
| 1984                   |        1 |  9.99 |
| Fahrenheit 451         |        1 | 10.49 |
| The Hobbit             |        1 | 10.99 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird  |        1 | 11.99 |
| Dune                   |        1 | 14.99 |
| Lord of the Rings      |        1 | 15.99 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |        0 |  8.99 |
| The Great Gatsby       |        0 |  8.99 |
+------------------------+----------+-------+
  4. Sort by an alias (2m). "Best value per page; the alias works because ORDER BY runs after SELECT." 
    SELECT title, price, pages,
       ROUND(pages / price, 1) AS pages_per_dollar
FROM books
ORDER BY pages_per_dollar DESC, title
LIMIT 5;
    +---------------------+-------+-------+------------------+
| title               | price | pages | pages_per_dollar |
+---------------------+-------+-------+------------------+
| Lord of the Rings   | 15.99 |  1178 |             73.7 |
| Pride and Prejudice |  7.99 |   432 |             54.1 |
| 1984                |  9.99 |   328 |             32.8 |
| Brave New World     |  9.49 |   311 |             32.8 |
| The Hobbit          | 10.99 |   310 |             28.2 |
+---------------------+-------+-------+------------------+
  5. A custom order with CASE (2m). "The Chicago team wants their customers first." 
    SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
ORDER BY
    CASE city
        WHEN 'Chicago'  THEN 1
        WHEN 'New York' THEN 2
        ELSE 3
    END,
    first_name;
    +------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Bob        | Chicago     |
| Grace      | Chicago     |
| Alice      | New York    |
| Frank      | New York    |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
| Dave       | Houston     |
| Eve        | NULL        |
| Hank       | NULL        |
+------------+-------------+
  6. Where NULLs go (2m). "Ascending: NULL first." Then push them down. 
    SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
ORDER BY city, first_name;

SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
ORDER BY city IS NULL, city, first_name;
    +------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Eve        | NULL        |
| Hank       | NULL        |
| Bob        | Chicago     |
| Grace      | Chicago     |
| Dave       | Houston     |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
| Alice      | New York    |
| Frank      | New York    |
+------------+-------------+
+------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Bob        | Chicago     |
| Grace      | Chicago     |
| Dave       | Houston     |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
| Alice      | New York    |
| Frank      | New York    |
| Eve        | NULL        |
| Hank       | NULL        |
+------------+-------------+
  7. Top N (1m). 
    SELECT title, pages
FROM books
ORDER BY pages DESC
LIMIT 3;
    +---------------------+-------+
| title               | pages |
+---------------------+-------+
| Lord of the Rings   |  1178 |
| Pride and Prejudice |   432 |
| Dune                |   412 |
+---------------------+-------+
  8. OFFSET (1m). "Rows 4 to 6 by price." 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
ORDER BY price, title
LIMIT 3 OFFSET 3;
    +-----------------+-------+
| title           | price |
+-----------------+-------+
| Brave New World |  9.49 |
| 1984            |  9.99 |
| Fahrenheit 451  | 10.49 |
+-----------------+-------+
  9. A page and the page count (2m). "Page 2 of the catalog, four per page, and how many pages." 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
ORDER BY title
LIMIT 4 OFFSET 4;

SELECT COUNT(*)              AS total_books,
       CEILING(COUNT(*) / 4) AS pages_needed
FROM books;
    +------------------------+-------+
| title                  | price |
+------------------------+-------+
| Lord of the Rings      | 15.99 |
| Pride and Prejudice    |  7.99 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |  8.99 |
| The Great Gatsby       |  8.99 |
+------------------------+-------+
+-------------+--------------+
| total_books | pages_needed |
+-------------+--------------+
|          10 |            3 |
+-------------+--------------+
  10. Keyset: the page after id 4 (1m). 
    SELECT id, title
FROM books
WHERE id > 4
ORDER BY id
LIMIT 4;
    +----+------------------------+
| id | title                  |
+----+------------------------+
|  5 | Pride and Prejudice    |
|  6 | The Hobbit             |
|  7 | Brave New World        |
|  8 | The Catcher in the Rye |
+----+------------------------+
  11. Three mistakes (3m). "Clauses in the wrong order; a formula in LIMIT; a column name in quotes." 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
ORDER BY price
WHERE price > 10;

SELECT title
FROM books
ORDER BY title
LIMIT 4 OFFSET (3 - 1) * 4;

SELECT title, price
FROM books
ORDER BY 'price' DESC
LIMIT 3;
    ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'WHERE price > 10' at line 4
ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near '(3 - 1) * 4' at line 4
+-----------------------+-------+
| title                 | price |
+-----------------------+-------+
| Dune                  | 14.99 |
| 1984                  |  9.99 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird | 11.99 |
+-----------------------+-------+
    The third is silent: 'price' is text, the same for every row. Not the three most expensive.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • ORDER BY a, b DESC expecting both descending. Coach: "DESC belongs to the column before it."
  • A top N with ties at the cut. Coach: "Could two rows have the same value here? Then add a unique column."
  • Clauses out of order or a formula in LIMIT (1064). Coach with the clause order on the board; compute the offset first.
Guided lab

File: labs/module-04/l3_2-sorting-limiting-results/bestseller_board_starter.sql; reference labs/module-04/l3_2-sorting-limiting-results/bestseller_board_solution.sql. Six lists for the shop's display board; each starts as ORDER BY id.

  • Lists 1 to 4 in pairs (10m), 5 and 6 for the quick (3m), debrief (2m).
  • Expected: Hank first; The Catcher in the Rye (22 characters) first, 1984 before Dune; authors A to Z, Tolkien's Lord of the Rings before The Hobbit; Fahrenheit 451, To Kill a Mockingbird, The Hobbit; Classic, Fiction, Dystopian, Fantasy, Science Fiction; Eve and Hank on top.
  • Circulate for: list 4 without WHERE (out-of-stock Gatsby appears); list 6 with city IS NULL (NULLs at the bottom: which way does 1 sort?).
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-04/l3_2-sorting-limiting-results/paging_starter.sql, then labs/module-04/l3_2-sorting-limiting-results/debug_sorting.sql.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is The Storefront Queries: storefront.sql. In the room: Steps 1 and 2 (10 minutes).

  • Step 1: three newest in-stock books (add , title as the tie-breaker the lesson suggests). Step 2: the bargain shelf.
  • Goes home: Steps 3 to 6 (catalog pages, page count, customer directory, review).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud:

  • Where do NULLs go in an ascending sort? Answer: first.
  • LIMIT 3 OFFSET 6 returns which rows? Answer: rows 7 to 9.
  • Why does the alias work in ORDER BY but not in WHERE? Answer: WHERE runs before SELECT computes it; ORDER BY after.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12.

Answers: Q7 A) in_stock ascending (0 first), then price descending within each group; Q8 Dune (412 pages: the third longest); Q9 C) No error, but the rows come in no particular order: 'price' is a fixed piece of text; Q10 D) Last; Q11 B) ... WHERE id > 57 ORDER BY id LIMIT 20; Q12 4. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: the clause order as a card.

Stretch: paging_starter.sql and debug_sorting.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (Tolkien week) and the keyset Bonus Challenge.

Checkpoint 1

2:20

Checkpoint 1: Modules 1 and 2

30 min
Timing
2mHand out labs/module-04/checkpoint-1/checkpoint_starter.sql and the workbook's Checkpoint 1 page; read the four parts aloud
24mLearners work alone: Parts A to C in the file, Part D on the page; SOURCE after each part
4mHand in the file and the page; learners mark their own confidence (1 to 3) next to each part
What it checks

Everything up to Lesson 2.3: a rerunnable build script with a named collation (2.1), types and constraints (2.2), INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE with a preview and an upsert (2.3), reading MySQL's results and error numbers (all of Module 1 and 2). The domain is new (a used-book trade-in desk), so nobody can copy a lesson project. Filtering and sorting (Module 3) are not scored; a learner who uses them is welcome to.

Running it
  • Open notes: the workbook, their own scripts (create_bookstore.sql, employees.sql, load_bookstore_data.sql) and the lesson pages. Alone; no AI assistants.
  • Answer questions about the task, not about SQL. "Is paid required?" (yes, default no) is fine; "How do I write the CHECK?" gets "look at Lesson 2.2's coupons table".
  • Stuck on Part A for more than 10 minutes? Hand them the model answer's two CREATE TABLE statements (from the Answer Key) so Parts B and C can still be scored; record that Part A is 0.
  • Scoring: the 5-row rubric in the Answer Key, 0 to 2 per row (10 points). 8 to 10: on track. 5 to 7: review the weakest row before Session 6. Under 5: a one-to-one before Lesson 4.1 (relationships build on all of it). Return the scores at the start of Session 5.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

After the checkpoint, keep it short: three questions on the slide, sticky notes on the way out.

  1. Why does WHERE city != 'Chicago' skip Eve and Hank, and how do you include them? Answer: Their city is NULL, so the comparison is unknown, not true, and only true rows are kept. Add OR city IS NULL.
  2. Write the WHERE clause for: books from $9 to $11, both ends included, that are not by Tolkien. Answer: WHERE price BETWEEN 9 AND 11 AND author != 'J.R.R. Tolkien' (on the course data: Brave New World, 1984, Fahrenheit 451).
  3. Why should every ORDER BY that feeds a LIMIT end with a unique column? Answer: If rows tie at the cut-off, the server may return either one, and the choice can change between runs: page 1 and page 2 can repeat or skip a row. A unique column (id, title here) makes the order repeatable.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both projects: saved_searches.sql (Steps 4 to 7) and storefront.sql (Steps 3 to 6).
  • Lesson exercises. 3.1: Exercises 1 to 3; Exercise 4 is a stretch. 3.2: Exercises 1 to 3; Exercise 4 is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes on the lesson pages.
  • Journal: both entries.
  • Optional: labs/module-04/l3_1-where-clauses-operators-filtering/null_detective_starter.sql, labs/module-04/l3_2-sorting-limiting-results/paging_starter.sql and the two debug handouts.
Bring list for Session 5
  • A laptop with the Module 3 data in bookstore (rerun labs/_states/flat.sql if in doubt), the root password.
  • saved_searches.sql and storefront.sql: Lesson 3.3's project builds on SOURCE-ing script files.
  • The course folder with labs/ (Session 5's starters are in labs/module-05/), the workbook and the journal.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: the checkpoint's 30 minutes, starting at 2:20; the data load; 3.1 demo Steps 4 and 11 to 14; 3.2 demo Steps 1, 2 and 11.
  • Cut first (saves 10m): the 3.1 project start.
  • Cut second (saves 4m): 3.2 demo Steps 4 and 5.
  • Cut third (saves 10m): the 3.2 project start; then shorten the 3.2 lab to lists 1 to 4.
Running short: what to add
  • Run debug_filters.sql together: which three bugs give no error?
  • Lesson 3.2's keyset Bonus Challenge with the row-comparison form (price, id) > (8.99, 8).
  • Give back the checkpoint model answer immediately and let learners compare their Part D.
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 5: Aggregates and Subqueries

How to teach the fifth session of MySQL Foundations: from rows to numbers. Aggregates and GROUP BY turn the books and customers into counts, totals and averages per group (with WHERE before the piles and HAVING after), then subqueries answer two-step questions in one statement: above average, the row behind the maximum, a list for IN, the NOT IN / NULL trap, correlated lookups, NOT EXISTS and derived tables.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 3.3–3.4 Session 5 of 14 · Part 1

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame (one mental model) → live demo (you type, they follow) → guided lab (a kit lab: new practice on the lesson's ideas, never the lesson project) → start the lesson's own project. The lesson exercises, quiz and journal are homework.
  • Type, do not paste. Every SQL block below was run on MySQL 9.7.2 in the flat bookstore of Module 3 (Lesson 3.1's setup script, labs/_states/flat.sql) to produce the output printed under it. Ask for a prediction before every statement that prints something; read every error number aloud.
  • The lessons are the reference: keep lesson_09.html and lesson_10.html open for the diagrams (piles, the run order, inner and outer query). Labs live in labs/module-05/: _starter.sql files are handouts, _solution.sql files are yours.
  • This is the session where SQL turns from "show me rows" into "tell me something about the rows". Two ideas carry it: the piles (WHERE before, HAVING after) and inside out (the inner query answers first). Draw both and leave them on the board all session.
  • Part 1 (Sessions 1 to 7) can run on its own as a short course; nothing here depends on Part 2. Teal blocks are hands-on, amber blocks are watch-outs.
The #1 risk of Session 5: correlated subqueries eat the second half

Lesson 3.4 is 100 minutes for a solo learner and the most abstract hour of Part 1. Learners who follow the scalar subquery and the IN list often stall at the correlated one, and a room can lose twenty minutes on why author = author is always true. Protect the scalar subquery, the NOT IN trap and the walk-through of one correlated row (Steps 2, 5 to 8): those are what Session 6 and 7 lean on (Lesson 4.1 uses correlated lookups before joins exist). ANY/ALL is deliberately not in the demo (it is in the lesson and the lab); keep the derived table to one example, and send the price review's Steps 3 to 6 home. If the room is still lost at 2:00, do the correlated step of the project (Step 2) together on the projector, inner query first, instead of the lab's TODO 2.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Checkpoint 1: scored with the 5-row rubric in Session 4's Answer Key, one sentence of advice per learner; you return it in the warm-up. Learners under 5 points get a one-to-one before Lesson 4.1 (Session 6).
  • ☐ Data: every machine has the flat bookstore of Module 3 (10 books with a text author column, 8 customers). A learner whose data drifted runs SOURCE labs/_states/flat.sql (it drops and rebuilds bookstore). Do NOT let anyone load bookstore.sql yet: that is the relational database Lesson 4.1 builds in Session 6, and every count today would change (11 books, an authors table).
  • ☐ Files to hand out: labs/module-05/ without the *_solution.sql files. Check the folder once: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-05 (10 files, a few seconds, every line PASS).
  • ☐ Projector machine: MySQL 9.7 with the flat bookstore loaded, a terminal in the course folder at 18 pt or larger, already in the mysql> client with USE bookstore;. Browser tabs: lesson_09.html, lesson_10.html.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: the run order as a column of seven boxes (FROM → WHERE → GROUP BY → HAVING → SELECT → ORDER BY → LIMIT) with "rows in" beside WHERE and "piles out" beside HAVING; a second area headed "inside out" with WHERE price > ( ___ ) and an arrow from a small box labeled "inner query" into the blank.
  • ☐ Print or share: the workbook (warm-up, worked examples, predict-then-run, self-check), the two debug handouts for the error clinic, sticky notes for the exit ticket.
Known traps today
SymptomCauseFix
Counts differ from the slides (11 books, author column missing)The learner loaded bookstore.sql (the Module 4 database) earlySOURCE labs/_states/flat.sql, then rerun.
ERROR 1055 on a query that "works on my friend's laptop"The friend runs MariaDB (XAMPP), which has no ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BYFix the query; never SET sql_mode = ''. The lesson's Common Mistake 2 shows the MariaDB output.
Groups come out in a different order than the lessonNo ORDER BY (MySQL 8.0+ does not sort groups); or MariaDB, which doesAdd ORDER BY. Every grouped query in a report ends with one.
ERROR 1064 ... near '' with a subquery in FROMMariaDB's wording for a derived table without an alias (MySQL says 1248)Add AS name after the closing parenthesis.
A correlated query returns the same number on every rowNo aliases, so author = author compares the inner row with itselfAlias both copies and qualify every column: b2.author = b.author.
Failed to open file after SOURCEThe client was started in another folderexit, cd into the course folder (the one with labs/), log in again.

In the room. The lesson pages estimate 90 + 100 = 190 minutes for a solo learner; after the warm-up, the break, the error clinic and the wrap-up the room has 140. Both lessons keep their full shape (model, demo, lab, project start). Lesson 3.4 gets the larger share because correlated subqueries are where learners need a person in the room.

Goes home (about 2 to 3 hours). The inventory report Steps 3 to 6 and the price review Steps 3 to 6; each lesson's Hands-on Exercises; both Quick Quizzes; both journal entries; the labs not finished in class (page_report_starter.sql, city_neighbors_starter.sql).

Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-up: Filtering, Sorting and Limiting

10 min
Timing
6mFive retrieval questions on Lessons 3.1 and 3.2, answered in the workbook, then on the projector
4mCheckpoint 1 scores returned (the 5-row rubric, one sentence of advice each); homework check: the storefront queries (3.2 project)
Retrieval questions and answers

Put the five questions on the slide; learners answer in the workbook's Warm-up box without running anything, then you run the SQL ones. The real output is below each.

  1. Who comes back, and why? (AND binds before OR.) 
    SELECT first_name, city, id
FROM customers
WHERE city = 'Chicago' OR city = 'Houston' AND id > 5
ORDER BY id;
    +------------+---------+----+
| first_name | city    | id |
+------------+---------+----+
| Bob        | Chicago |  2 |
| Grace      | Chicago |  7 |
+------------+---------+----+
    Answer: Bob and Grace. AND is evaluated first, so the condition reads city = 'Chicago' OR (city = 'Houston' AND id > 5); Dave (Houston) has id 4. Parentheses say what you mean.
  2. Why does this find nobody, although two customers have no city? 
    SELECT first_name FROM customers WHERE city = NULL;
    Empty set
    Answer: nothing is equal to NULL, not even NULL. Write WHERE city IS NULL (Eve and Hank).
  3. In which order must you write WHERE, ORDER BY and LIMIT? Answer: WHERE, then ORDER BY, then LIMIT (LIMIT always last). Today adds GROUP BY and HAVING between WHERE and ORDER BY.
  4. Which three books does this show? 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
ORDER BY price DESC, title
LIMIT 3 OFFSET 2;
    +-----------------------+-------+
| title                 | price |
+-----------------------+-------+
| To Kill a Mockingbird | 11.99 |
| The Hobbit            | 10.99 |
| Fahrenheit 451        | 10.49 |
+-----------------------+-------+
    Answer: the 3rd to 5th most expensive: OFFSET 2 skips Lord of the Rings and Dune. The tie-breaker title makes the order repeatable.
  5. Predict: how many customers outside Chicago? 
    SELECT first_name
FROM customers
WHERE city NOT IN ('Chicago', NULL);
    Empty set
    Answer: none. Lesson 3.1 warned: a NULL inside a NOT IN list makes every row unknown. Keep this one in mind: after the break, the same trap hides inside a subquery.
How to read the warm-up
  • Q1 wrong (Dave included, or Bob only): precedence is shaky. Today's HAVING conditions combine AND and OR (the lesson project's Step 4); say "parentheses" every time you see both.
  • Q2 wrong: spend one minute on NULL now: it is the main character of both lessons today (COUNT(column), AVG skipping NULL, the NOT IN trap).
  • Q5 right from more than half the room: say "you already know the trap; after the break you will meet it where it is harder to see".

Lesson Plans

3.3

Aggregate Functions & GROUP BY

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Summarize a table with COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN and MAX, and predict how each one treats NULL
  • Produce one summary row per group with GROUP BY, sorted with ORDER BY
  • Keep or drop whole groups with HAVING, and explain when to use WHERE instead
  • Read and fix the errors MySQL raises for broken aggregate queries (1140, 1055, 1111)
  • Build a small inventory report as a reusable .sql script
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the cashier's slip; rows into piles, one answer per pile; the run order
25mLive demo: COUNT three ways, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX, GROUP BY, HAVING, errors 1140, 1055, 1111
15mGuided lab: customer_summary_starter.sql in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: the inventory report, Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

Start with the slip: "At the end of the day the cashier does not read every receipt. She reads the totals at the bottom. That is an aggregate: many rows in, one value out. COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX."

Then the piles, drawn on the board: "GROUP BY sorts the rows into piles that share a value, then runs the aggregate once per pile. One pile, one result row. Every column you show must be either the pile's label (the GROUP BY column) or a summary of the pile (an aggregate). Anything else, like one title from a pile of two, is a guess, and MySQL refuses to guess."

Then the run order, pointing at the seven boxes: "You WRITE SELECT first, but the server RUNS it fifth. WHERE runs before the piles exist, so it cannot count them. HAVING runs after, so it can."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

The lesson's own examples, typed live in the flat bookstore. Learners type along. Ask for a prediction before each statement and read every error number aloud.

  1. COUNT three ways (3m). "A plain SELECT gives one result row per table row. An aggregate reads many rows and gives back ONE value." Ask first: eight customers, two without a city, New York and Chicago twice each: what are the three numbers? 
    SELECT
    COUNT(*)             AS customers,
    COUNT(city)          AS with_a_city,
    COUNT(DISTINCT city) AS cities
FROM customers;
    +-----------+-------------+--------+
| customers | with_a_city | cities |
+-----------+-------------+--------+
|         8 |           6 |      4 |
+-----------+-------------+--------+
    Write the rule on the board: every aggregate skips NULL, except COUNT(*).
  2. SUM and AVG (2m). "SUM adds, AVG divides by the number of values. Why six decimals?" Let them guess before you explain: price is DECIMAL(8,2), and MySQL keeps the average exact by adding four places. 
    SELECT
    SUM(price)           AS one_of_each,
    AVG(price)           AS avg_price,
    ROUND(AVG(price), 2) AS avg_rounded
FROM books;
    +-------------+-----------+-------------+
| one_of_each | avg_price | avg_rounded |
+-------------+-----------+-------------+
|      109.90 | 10.990000 |       10.99 |
+-------------+-----------+-------------+
    Habit for the rest of the course: ROUND(AVG(...), 2) whenever a person reads money.
  3. MIN tells the value, not the row (2m). "MIN says the cheapest price is 7.99. Which book is that?" It cannot say. Sort and take one row, as in Lesson 3.2. 
    SELECT MIN(price) AS cheapest, MAX(price) AS priciest
FROM books;
SELECT title, price
FROM books
ORDER BY price
LIMIT 1;
    +----------+----------+
| cheapest | priciest |
+----------+----------+
|     7.99 |    15.99 |
+----------+----------+
+---------------------+-------+
| title               | price |
+---------------------+-------+
| Pride and Prejudice |  7.99 |
+---------------------+-------+
    Leave this open: "what if two books shared the lowest price? LIMIT 1 would hide one." Lesson 3.4 closes the gap after the break.
  4. Aggregates over no rows (2m). "Nothing costs over $100. COUNT says 0. What does SUM say?" 
    SELECT
    COUNT(*)   AS books_over_100,
    SUM(price) AS their_total
FROM books
WHERE price > 100;
    +----------------+-------------+
| books_over_100 | their_total |
+----------------+-------------+
|              0 |        NULL |
+----------------+-------------+
    COUNT answers 0, the others NULL. A report that adds a NULL to something gets NULL: COALESCE(SUM(price), 0).
  5. GROUP BY: one answer per pile (4m). Draw ten books falling into piles by author, then COUNT on each pile. "Nine authors, so nine piles, so nine rows. I show four." 
    SELECT author, COUNT(*) AS book_count
FROM books
GROUP BY author
ORDER BY book_count DESC, author
LIMIT 4;
    +---------------------+------------+
| author              | book_count |
+---------------------+------------+
| J.R.R. Tolkien      |          2 |
| Aldous Huxley       |          1 |
| F. Scott Fitzgerald |          1 |
| Frank Herbert       |          1 |
+---------------------+------------+
    The ORDER BY is not decoration: on MySQL 8.0 and later groups come back in no promised order (the lesson's Common Mistake 5).
  6. Several aggregates per group (2m). "One pile for in stock, one for out of stock, four numbers each." 
    SELECT
    in_stock,
    COUNT(*)             AS books,
    ROUND(AVG(price), 2) AS avg_price,
    MAX(pages)           AS longest
FROM books
GROUP BY in_stock
ORDER BY in_stock DESC;
    +----------+-------+-----------+---------+
| in_stock | books | avg_price | longest |
+----------+-------+-----------+---------+
|        1 |     8 |     11.49 |    1178 |
|        0 |     2 |      8.99 |     234 |
+----------+-------+-----------+---------+
    in_stock is a BOOLEAN stored as 1 and 0. The two out-of-stock books both cost 8.99.
  7. HAVING keeps whole piles (3m). "WHERE picks rows going INTO the piles. HAVING picks piles coming OUT." Point at the run-order diagram on the board. 
    SELECT city, COUNT(*) AS customers
FROM customers
WHERE city IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY city
HAVING customers > 1
ORDER BY city;
    +----------+-----------+
| city     | customers |
+----------+-----------+
| Chicago  |         2 |
| New York |         2 |
+----------+-----------+
  8. WHERE and HAVING together (3m). Read each line's job aloud: drop out-of-stock books, pile by author, keep piles averaging over $10, most expensive first. 
    SELECT author, ROUND(AVG(price), 2) AS avg_price
FROM books
WHERE in_stock = TRUE
GROUP BY author
HAVING AVG(price) > 10
ORDER BY avg_price DESC;
    +----------------+-----------+
| author         | avg_price |
+----------------+-----------+
| Frank Herbert  |     14.99 |
| J.R.R. Tolkien |     13.49 |
| Harper Lee     |     11.99 |
| Ray Bradbury   |     10.49 |
+----------------+-----------+
    Rule of thumb on the board: a condition on one row's column goes in WHERE; a condition on a group's aggregate goes in HAVING.
  9. Error 1140 on purpose (1m). "Ten titles squashed into one row. Which title goes in it?" 
    SELECT title, COUNT(*) FROM books;
    ERROR 1140 (42000): In aggregated query without GROUP BY, expression #1 of SELECT list contains nonaggregated column 'bookstore.books.title'; this is incompatible with sql_mode=only_full_group_by
  10. Error 1055 on purpose (2m). "Tolkien's pile has two titles. Which one?" Read the message from the left: number, then 'not in GROUP BY clause'. 
    SELECT author, title, COUNT(*) AS books
FROM books
GROUP BY author;
    ERROR 1055 (42000): Expression #2 of SELECT list is not in GROUP BY clause and contains nonaggregated column 'bookstore.books.title' which is not functionally dependent on columns in GROUP BY clause; this is incompatible with sql_mode=only_full_group_by
    Fix: aggregate it (MIN(title)), group by it, or drop it. Never switch off ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY to make it go away: MariaDB already does, and silently picks a title.
  11. Error 1111 on purpose (1m). "WHERE runs before the piles exist. There is nothing to count yet." 
    SELECT author, COUNT(*) AS books
FROM books
WHERE COUNT(*) > 1
GROUP BY author;
    ERROR 1111 (HY000): Invalid use of group function
    Fix: HAVING COUNT(*) > 1 after GROUP BY.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • A plain column next to an aggregate. SELECT title, COUNT(*) (1140) or an extra column with GROUP BY (1055). Coach with the piles: "Point at the pile. Which ONE title belongs in its row?" Then the three fixes: aggregate it, group by it, drop it.
  • The aggregate in WHERE. WHERE COUNT(*) > 1 (1111). Coach with the run order: "When WHERE runs, do the piles exist yet?" Then move it to HAVING.
  • Trusting the group order, or forgetting ORDER BY. The result looks sorted on one run and not on the next server. Coach: "Who promised you that order?" Every grouped query in the project ends with ORDER BY (the project's checklist says so).
Guided lab

File: learners open labs/module-05/l3_3-aggregate-functions/customer_summary_starter.sql; your reference is labs/module-05/l3_3-aggregate-functions/customer_summary_solution.sql. The lesson summarized the books; this lab summarizes the customers: four TODOs, each a small change to a query that already runs.

  • SOURCE it as shipped (1m): SOURCE labs/module-05/l3_3-aggregate-functions/customer_summary_starter.sql. They see 8 customers with zero placeholders, a TODO line, every known city, and the cities with a NULL group.
  • TODO 1 together (4m), TODOs 2 to 4 in pairs (8m). TODO 1 replaces five placeholders with COUNT(city), COUNT(*) - COUNT(city), COUNT(DISTINCT city), MIN and MAX of created_at. TODO 2 is written from scratch (GROUP BY a DATE_FORMAT label). TODO 3 adds HAVING. TODO 4 combines WHERE, COALESCE and an ORDER BY on the count.
  • Expected: TODO 1: 8 / 6 / 2 / 4 cities, first signup 2026-01-04 10:15:00, latest 2026-02-10 19:12:00; TODO 2: 2026-01 6, 2026-02 2; TODO 3: New York and Chicago, 2 each; TODO 4: New York 2, then (no city), Chicago, Houston, Los Angeles with 1 each.
  • Circulate for: COALESCE in the SELECT list but not in GROUP BY (it still works on MySQL, since the alias is allowed in GROUP BY; ask them to say why), HAVING customers > 1 written before GROUP BY (1064), and a missing comma between two aggregates.
  • Debrief (2m): "In TODO 4, which line picks the rows, and which line makes the piles?" Then: "why is (no city) a pile at all?" (NULLs land together in one group).
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-05/l3_3-aggregate-functions/page_report_starter.sql (length bands with CASE, pages per author, books per century, WHERE plus HAVING), then labs/module-05/l3_3-aggregate-functions/debug_group_by.sql (four broken summaries: 1140, 1055, 1111, 1064).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is The Inventory Report: inventory_report.sql, a five-part summary the owner reruns every Monday. In the room: Steps 1 and 2 (10 minutes).

  • Step 1: create the file in the course folder with USE bookstore; and the headline query (titles, in stock, total pages, average price). The SUM(in_stock) trick deserves one sentence: true is 1, so adding them counts the true rows.
  • Step 2: stock value by status with IF(in_stock, 'In stock', 'Out of stock') grouped by that label.
  • Done in the room = the file runs with SOURCE and prints the 10 / 8 / 3824 / 10.99 headline and the two status rows.
  • Goes home: Steps 3 to 6 (price bands, display-table authors, customers by city, running the whole file).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • COUNT(*) or COUNT(city): which one counts Eve? Answer: COUNT(*): it counts rows; COUNT(city) skips her NULL.
  • Where does price > 10 go, and where does AVG(price) > 10 go? Answer: WHERE (one row's column) and HAVING (a group's aggregate).
  • Why does SELECT author, title, COUNT(*) ... GROUP BY author fail? Answer: Tolkien's pile holds two titles; MySQL will not pick one (1055).
  • What does SUM return when no row matches? Answer: NULL (COUNT returns 0); COALESCE turns it into 0.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6:

Answers: Q1 B) 2 and 1; Q2 C) In HAVING, after GROUP BY; Q3 One row: n = 0 and top = NULL; Q4 A) ERROR 1055 (42000): first_name is not in GROUP BY and not aggregated; Q5 D) No: since MySQL 8.0 the order of groups is not guaranteed; add ORDER BY; Q6 Two rows: 0 | 2 and 1 | 3. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: learners who freeze at an empty editor get the starter's TODO 3 first (only one line to add). Pair a learner who missed warm-up Q2 (NULL) with one who got it: today's TODO 1 is all about NULL.

Stretch: page_report_starter.sql and debug_group_by.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (books by decade) and the WITH ROLLUP Bonus Challenge (ask: why is the grand total's average not the average of the two group averages?).

3.4

Subqueries & Nested Queries

80 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Compare every row with an aggregate using a scalar subquery ("above average", "equal to the maximum")
  • Filter with IN, NOT IN, ANY and ALL subqueries, and keep NULLs from emptying a NOT IN
  • Write correlated subqueries and EXISTS / NOT EXISTS checks that compare a row with related rows
  • Build derived tables in FROM and computed columns in SELECT
  • Read and fix the subquery errors 1242, 1241, 1248 and 1235
Timing in the room
10mFrame: fill in the blank; inside out; the three shapes of result (one value, a list, a table)
30mLive demo: scalar, the MAX row, IN, the NOT IN trap, correlated, NOT EXISTS, a derived table, errors 1242 and 1248
25mGuided lab: page_checks_starter.sql, then city_neighbors_starter.sql
15mStart the lesson project: the price review, Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

Point at the blank on the board: "WHERE price > ( ___ ). Before the break you would have typed 10.99 there. A subquery is a whole SELECT in that blank: the inner query works out the value, the outer query uses it. Read nested queries from the inside out."

Then the shapes: "What the blank expects depends on where it is. After = or >: ONE value (a scalar). After IN: one column, any number of rows (a list). In FROM: a whole table, and it needs a name. Most subquery errors are a shape mismatch: 1242 is too many rows, 1241 too many columns, 1248 a table without a name."

Then correlation, slowly: "Every subquery so far could run on its own. A correlated one cannot: it mentions the OUTER row. Picture MySQL walking down the books one at a time and asking the inner question again for each one: what is the average of THIS book's author?"

Live demo (you type, they follow)

The lesson's examples, typed live. Run every inner query on its own first (select it and run it), then the whole statement: it is the habit that makes nested queries readable.

  1. Two steps, by hand (2m). "Which books cost more than average? First the average." 
    SELECT AVG(price) FROM books;
    +------------+
| AVG(price) |
+------------+
|  10.990000 |
+------------+
    Type WHERE price > 10.99 once and show it works, then ask: "what happens to that 10.99 next week when a price changes?"
  2. Put the first query where the number was (3m). "Parentheses, a whole SELECT inside. MySQL works out the inner one first, then uses its value." 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
WHERE price > (SELECT AVG(price) FROM books)
ORDER BY price;
    +-----------------------+-------+
| title                 | price |
+-----------------------+-------+
| To Kill a Mockingbird | 11.99 |
| Dune                  | 14.99 |
| Lord of the Rings     | 15.99 |
+-----------------------+-------+
    Read it inside out, aloud. This is the whole idea of the lesson; everything after is a variation.
  3. The row behind the maximum (2m). "Lesson 3.3 could not say which book costs the most. Now:" 
    SELECT title, author, price
FROM books
WHERE price = (SELECT MAX(price) FROM books);
    +-------------------+----------------+-------+
| title             | author         | price |
+-------------------+----------------+-------+
| Lord of the Rings | J.R.R. Tolkien | 15.99 |
+-------------------+----------------+-------+
    Ask: what would ORDER BY price DESC LIMIT 1 do if two books tied? The subquery returns all of them.
  4. A list: IN with a GROUP BY inside (4m). "One column, several rows: that is a list, and lists go with IN." Run the inner query alone first, highlighted. 
    SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
WHERE city IN (
    SELECT city FROM customers
    GROUP BY city
    HAVING COUNT(*) > 1
)
ORDER BY city, first_name;
    +------------+----------+
| first_name | city     |
+------------+----------+
| Bob        | Chicago  |
| Grace      | Chicago  |
| Alice      | New York |
| Frank      | New York |
+------------+----------+
    The inner query also returns NULL (Eve and Hank form a group of two). In a plain IN that does no harm.
  5. NOT IN, and the NULL trap (5m). "Which January customers live in a city where no February customer lives? First, the February cities." 
    SELECT city FROM customers WHERE created_at >= '2026-02-01';
    +---------+
| city    |
+---------+
| Chicago |
| NULL    |
+---------+
  6. The trap springs. "Predict how many rows." Then run it. 
    SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
WHERE created_at < '2026-02-01'
  AND city NOT IN (
      SELECT city FROM customers
      WHERE created_at >= '2026-02-01'
  );
    Empty set
    No error, no rows. city != 'Chicago' AND city != NULL: the second half is unknown for every row. Write the rule: every NOT IN subquery gets an IS NOT NULL.
  7. The fix. Add one line inside the subquery. 
    SELECT first_name, city
FROM customers
WHERE created_at < '2026-02-01'
  AND city NOT IN (
      SELECT city FROM customers
      WHERE created_at >= '2026-02-01'
        AND city IS NOT NULL
  )
ORDER BY first_name;
    +------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Alice      | New York    |
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
| Dave       | Houston     |
| Frank      | New York    |
+------------+-------------+
    Eve is missing too: her own city is NULL, so NOT IN cannot decide about her. NOT EXISTS treats her differently (Step 9).
  8. Correlated: above their OWN author's average (5m). "Now the inner query depends on the outer row. b is the book being tested, b2 the scan of the same table." Walk the Hobbit row, then the Lord of the Rings row, out loud. 
    SELECT b.title, b.author, b.price
FROM books AS b
WHERE b.price > (
    SELECT AVG(b2.price)
    FROM books AS b2
    WHERE b2.author = b.author
);
    +-------------------+----------------+-------+
| title             | author         | price |
+-------------------+----------------+-------+
| Lord of the Rings | J.R.R. Tolkien | 15.99 |
+-------------------+----------------+-------+
    Every single-book author's average is that book's price, and a price is never above itself: only one row survives.
  9. NOT EXISTS (3m). "Is there ANOTHER customer in my city? NOT EXISTS keeps the ones where there is not." SELECT 1 means: the columns do not matter. 
    SELECT c.first_name, c.city
FROM customers AS c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1
    FROM customers AS other
    WHERE other.city = c.city
      AND other.id != c.id
)
ORDER BY c.first_name;
    +------------+-------------+
| first_name | city        |
+------------+-------------+
| Carol      | Los Angeles |
| Dave       | Houston     |
| Eve        | NULL        |
| Hank       | NULL        |
+------------+-------------+
    Eve and Hank appear: nobody can be found in an unknown city. No NULL trap.
  10. A derived table: a summary of a summary (3m). "Count per author first, then average the counts. You cannot write AVG(COUNT(*)) in one level." 
    SELECT ROUND(AVG(book_count), 2) AS avg_books_per_author,
       MAX(book_count)            AS most_books
FROM (
    SELECT author, COUNT(*) AS book_count
    FROM books
    GROUP BY author
) AS author_counts;
    +----------------------+------------+
| avg_books_per_author | most_books |
+----------------------+------------+
|                 1.11 |          2 |
+----------------------+------------+
  11. A whole-table number next to every row (1m). "A subquery in the SELECT list: per-row and whole-table side by side." 
    SELECT
    title,
    price,
    ROUND(price - (SELECT AVG(price) FROM books), 2) AS diff_from_avg
FROM books
ORDER BY diff_from_avg DESC, title
LIMIT 4;
    +-----------------------+-------+---------------+
| title                 | price | diff_from_avg |
+-----------------------+-------+---------------+
| Lord of the Rings     | 15.99 |          5.00 |
| Dune                  | 14.99 |          4.00 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird | 11.99 |          1.00 |
| The Hobbit            | 10.99 |          0.00 |
+-----------------------+-------+---------------+
  12. Error 1242 on purpose (1m). "= needs ONE value. Two books are out of stock." 
    SELECT title, price
FROM books
WHERE price = (SELECT price FROM books WHERE in_stock = FALSE);
    ERROR 1242 (21000): Subquery returns more than 1 row
    Fix: IN, or MIN/MAX. This one works today and breaks the day a second row arrives.
  13. Error 1248 on purpose. "A table in FROM must have a name." 
    SELECT AVG(book_count)
FROM (
    SELECT author, COUNT(*) AS book_count
    FROM books
    GROUP BY author
);
    ERROR 1248 (42000): Every derived table must have its own alias
    Fix: AS author_counts after the closing parenthesis.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • A shape mismatch. = with a list (1242), two columns in an IN list (1241), a derived table without a name (1248). Coach: "Run the inner query alone. How many rows and columns came back? What does the blank expect?"
  • NOT IN with a NULL in the list (silent). An empty result and no error. Coach: "Run the inner query alone. Do you see a NULL?" Then the rule: every NOT IN subquery gets IS NOT NULL, or use NOT EXISTS.
  • A correlated subquery without aliases (silent). WHERE author = author returns the store average. Coach: "Inside the parentheses, which table does author mean?" Then alias both copies and qualify every column inside the subquery.
Guided lab

Files: labs/module-05/l3_4-subqueries/page_checks_starter.sql (scalar, IN and ALL subqueries on dates and page counts), then labs/module-05/l3_4-subqueries/city_neighbors_starter.sql (correlated, a derived table, the NOT IN trap on new data). References: the matching _solution.sql files.

  • page_checks (10m, pairs): each TODO replaces a hand-typed placeholder with a subquery, so the file ends with no typed numbers. Expected: Pride and Prejudice (1813-01-28); five quick reads from Fahrenheit 451 (158 pages) to 1984 (328); The Great Gatsby and The Catcher in the Rye; four books after every out-of-stock book, and same_with_max 4.
  • city_neighbors (15m, pairs): TODO 1 and 2 are correlated (count the neighbors; the first signup per city), TODO 3 is a derived table, TODO 4 is the NOT IN trap as shipped: 4a prints nothing until the IS NOT NULL is added. Expected: neighbors 1, 1, 0, 0, 0, 1, 1, 0; first signups Alice, Bob, Carol, Dave (Eve and Hank never match: NULL = NULL is not true); authors_over_300 5, longest_total 1488; 4a and 4b both Carol and Dave.
  • Coach the build order: inner query first, for ONE example value (WHERE city = 'Chicago'), check it, then replace the example with the outer column (= c.city). Say it every time someone is stuck.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-05/l3_4-subqueries/debug_subqueries.sql: four broken subqueries (1242, 1241, 1248, 1235), one per shape.
  • Debrief (2m): "In 4a, why did nothing come back, with no error?" Someone should say "a NULL in the list". Then: "which version would you trust in a monthly report?"
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is The Price Review: price_review.sql, five queries that recompute themselves after every price change. In the room: Steps 1 and 2 (15 minutes).

  • Step 1 (5m): the books above the store average, with the average and the difference beside each (three subqueries, none correlated).
  • Step 2 (10m): each author's entry-level book, the correlated subquery. Make everyone write the inner query first for WHERE author = 'J.R.R. Tolkien', run it, then correlate it. This is the exact skill the journal prompt asks them to explain.
  • Done in the room = Step 1 prints three books (5.00, 4.00, 1.00 above the 10.99 average) and Step 2 prints nine authors, The Hobbit for Tolkien.
  • Goes home: Steps 3 to 6 (NOT EXISTS for fully-in-stock authors, the top price tier via a derived table, the headline numbers, running it inside a transaction).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • What shape does WHERE price = ( ... ) expect? And IN ( ... )? Answer: one value; one column with any number of rows.
  • Why did the NOT IN query return nothing? Answer: the list held a NULL, and city != NULL is unknown for every row.
  • What makes a subquery correlated? Answer: it refers to a column of the outer query's current row (b2.author = b.author).
  • Why does FROM (SELECT ...) need AS name? Answer: every table in FROM must have a name (1248).

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12:

Answers: Q7 B) 6; Q8 A) ERROR 1242 (21000): Subquery returns more than 1 row; Q9 0 (no customer passes the test); Q10 C) NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM customers AS other WHERE other.city = c.city AND other.id != c.id); Q11 2; Q12 D) Both author names refer to the inner table, so author = author is always true and the subquery returns the store average. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: for learners lost at the correlated step, the workbook's worked example builds one in two runs (one city by hand, then correlated). Let them do page_checks only and leave city_neighbors for home.

Stretch: debug_subqueries.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (cheaper than their author) and the Bonus Challenge (the second-highest price without LIMIT, then the third: watch the nesting grow, a preview of Lesson 5.2's DENSE_RANK).

2:40

Error Clinic: Eight Numbered Errors

10 min
How it runs

Half the room runs labs/module-05/l3_3-aggregate-functions/debug_group_by.sql, the other half labs/module-05/l3_4-subqueries/debug_subqueries.sql. Each pair takes one ERROR line, writes the number, what MySQL could not decide, and the fix; then one sentence per error, aloud, in error-number order.

ErrorWhat MySQL could not decideFix
1140which first name goes in the one row of a whole-table COUNTaggregate everything, or GROUP BY
1055which last name goes in each city's rowMIN(last_name), group by it, or drop it
1111how to count piles that do not exist yet (aggregate in WHERE)HAVING COUNT(*) > 1 after GROUP BY
1064how to read HAVING before GROUP BYGROUP BY, then HAVING
1242which of two dates to compare with =IN (or an aggregate)
1241how to compare one author with a two-column rowselect only the author; the SUM stays in HAVING
1248what to call a table in FROMAS city_counts after the parenthesis
1235how to apply LIMIT inside an IN listwrap the LIMIT query in a derived table

The fixed versions and their real outputs are in the Answer Key.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Learners answer in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Sort the notes before Session 6: question 2's pile tells you whether to reteach NOT IN in the Session 6 warm-up; question 3's pile tells you who to sit with during the 4.1 migration.

  1. Write one query condition that belongs in WHERE and one that belongs in HAVING, for the books table. Answer: for example WHERE in_stock = TRUE (one row's column) and HAVING COUNT(*) > 1 or HAVING AVG(price) > 10 (a group's aggregate).
  2. A NOT IN subquery returns ('Chicago', NULL). What does the outer query return, and what are two fixes? Answer: no rows at all. Add AND city IS NOT NULL inside the subquery, or rewrite with NOT EXISTS.
  3. In your own words: what makes a subquery correlated, and why does it need aliases? Answer: it uses a column of the outer query's current row, so it is re-evaluated per row; with the same table twice, aliases (b, b2) are the only way to say which copy each column belongs to.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both lesson projects: the inventory report (Steps 3 to 6) and the price review (Steps 3 to 6). Both are scripts: each must run start to finish with SOURCE and no error.
  • Lesson exercises. 3.3: Exercises 1 to 3 (Exercise 4 is a stretch). 3.4: Exercises 1 to 3 (Exercise 4 is a stretch).
  • Both Quick Quizzes on the lesson pages (answers in the Answer Key).
  • Journal: both entries. The 3.4 prompt (explain the correlated subquery as the database walking through the books) is the best preparation for Session 6.
  • Labs not finished in class: page_report_starter.sql, city_neighbors_starter.sql; the two debug handouts if the clinic was cut.
  • Before the Next Lesson (from 3.4): keep the flat bookstore and Lesson 3.1's setup script: Lesson 4.1 starts from this same flat bookstore and turns it into related tables. Download bookstore.sql into the course folder too (do not run it yet).
Bring list for Session 6
  • A laptop with the flat bookstore loaded (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM books; says 10 and books still has an author column). If not: SOURCE labs/_states/flat.sql before you arrive.
  • bookstore.sql saved in the course folder (not run yet): Lesson 4.1 ends by comparing with it, and Lesson 4.2 starts by loading it.
  • inventory_report.sql and price_review.sql, finished, and the course folder with labs/.
  • The workbook and the journal; paper for an ER sketch.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: 3.4 demo Steps 2 and 5 to 8 (inside out, the NOT IN trap, one correlated row aloud); 3.3 demo Steps 5, 7 and 8 (piles, HAVING, WHERE plus HAVING); 3.3 lab TODOs 1 and 3.
  • Cut first (saves 10m): the error clinic. Hand out both debug files as homework; their fixes are in the Answer Key.
  • Cut second (saves 10m): the 3.4 lab's second file (city_neighbors) goes home; keep TODO 4a (the trap) as a 2-minute demo instead.
  • Cut third (saves 5m): 3.4 demo Steps 10 and 11 (derived table, SELECT-list subquery): show the slides only.
Running short: what to add
  • Run debug_subqueries.sql with the whole room and fix Bug D together: the derived-table workaround for LIMIT inside IN.
  • The 3.3 Bonus Challenge live: GROUP BY in_stock WITH ROLLUP, then label the total row with GROUPING().
  • The 3.4 Bonus Challenge on the board: the second-highest price without LIMIT. Collect two different correct answers.
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 6: Relationships and Foreign Keys

How to teach the sixth session of MySQL Foundations: from one flat table to a relational database. Learners watch an update anomaly happen, migrate the books' author text into an authors table with a checked, reversible sequence, build a junction table, then put the foreign keys to the test: 1452 and 1451, CASCADE, SET NULL and RESTRICT inside transactions, and keys added to data that already holds an orphan.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 4.1–4.2 Session 6 of 14 · Part 1

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame (one mental model) → live demo → guided lab (new practice, never the lesson project) → start the lesson's own project. Exercises, quiz and journal are homework.
  • Type the short statements; paste the long INSERTs (the 25 links, the authors). Every SQL block below was run on MySQL 9.7.2: Lesson 4.1's demo from the flat bookstore, in one session, through the migration; Lesson 4.2's demo from a fresh bookstore.sql.
  • This session changes the database. Lesson 4.1 turns the flat bookstore into the relational one, and from Lesson 4.2 on every lesson starts by loading bookstore.sql (8 tables: authors 10, books 11, categories 5, links 25, customers 8, orders 12, order items 18, employees 10). After the 4.1 project start, every machine loads it; nobody needs to finish the migration by hand to follow 4.2.
  • The lessons are the reference: keep lesson_11.html (the ER diagrams) and lesson_12.html (the rules diagram) open. Labs live in labs/module-06/.
  • Part 1 (Sessions 1 to 7) can run on its own; Session 7 is the Part 1 assessment. Tell the room today: next session ends with it, and today's tables are in it.
The #1 risk of Session 6: half the room in the flat database, half in the relational one

4.1 migrates live data. Learners who mistype a step (a missing comma in the ALTER, the UPDATE run twice, a DROP COLUMN before the check) end up between the two shapes, and from then on nothing on the projector matches their screen. Prevent it: in the 4.1 demo, ready learners type along in their flat bookstore, but say clearly that the outcome does not matter: at 1:05 EVERYONE loads bookstore.sql (SOURCE bookstore.sql from the course folder, or labs/_states/bookstore.sql), and the counts query proves it (10, 11, 5, 25, 8, 12, 18, 10). Recover: anyone confused before then reloads the flat state (SOURCE labs/_states/flat.sql) and watches. The 4.1 lab works on two practice tables of its own, so it cannot damage either shape.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Data: learners arrive with the flat bookstore (Module 3) loaded and bookstore.sql saved in the course folder (Session 5's bring list). Have labs/_states/flat.sql and labs/_states/bookstore.sql on a USB stick for anyone without them.
  • ☐ Files to hand out: labs/module-06/ without the *_solution.sql files. Check: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-06 (10 files, every line PASS).
  • ☐ Projector machine: MySQL 9.7 with the FLAT bookstore loaded (for the 4.1 demo) and bookstore.sql ready for 1:05; terminal at 18 pt; browser tabs lesson_11.html, lesson_12.html. Keep the long INSERTs (authors, the 25 links) in a text file to paste.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: "Can one A have many Bs? Can one B have many As?" with the three answers (1:M key on the many side · 1:1 key + UNIQUE · M:M junction table); and the rules diagram: "Can the child exist without its parent? no → CASCADE · yes → must it keep naming the parent? yes → RESTRICT, no → SET NULL".
  • ☐ Print or share: the workbook (warm-up, the club worked example, predict-then-run, self-check, the relationship map page), sticky notes for the exit ticket, paper for the crow's foot sketch.
Known traps today
SymptomCauseFix
ERROR 1054 Unknown column 'author' in Lesson 3 queriesThe learner is already in the relational bookstore (the author text column is gone)Expected from 4.1 on; joins (4.3) bring the name back. For 3.x homework: SOURCE labs/_states/flat.sql.
ERROR 1138 Invalid use of NULL value in Step 4 of the migrationA book's author_id is still NULL (a typo in the authors INSERT)Run the IS NULL check, fix the author name or the id, then the ALTER again.
ERROR 1824 Failed to open the referenced tableA child created before its parent, or a misspelled parent (book)Create parents first; check the table name.
ERROR 1452 with a table called #sql-...Adding a key to a table that already holds an orphanFind orphans with NOT IN (SELECT id FROM parent), fix them, add the key again.
MariaDB: ERROR 1005 ... errno: 150MariaDB's single message for a badly formed key (MySQL: 3780, 6125, 1830, 1824)Walk the requirements: same type and sign, parent key exists and is unique, SET NULL needs a nullable column.
A delete "does nothing" after a testA transaction left open (START TRANSACTION without ROLLBACK or COMMIT)Type ROLLBACK; and look again. Every test in the labs ends with ROLLBACK.

In the room. The lesson pages estimate 105 + 100 = 205 minutes for a solo learner; the room has 140. Lesson 4.1's live migration is the heart of the session (it is the one time learners watch a table change shape under its data); the 4.1 project is mostly pasting CREATE TABLE and INSERT statements, so the room reads its design and the typing goes home. Lesson 4.2 keeps its full shape.

Goes home (about 2 to 3 hours). Plus preparation for the Part 1 assessment (see the bring list).
