Cut first: the relationship map (it becomes homework), then 4.1 demo Steps 11 and 12 (one-to-one: show the slide), then 4.2 demo Steps 8 to 10 (adding a key to dirty data: the orphan_audit lab does it).

Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-up: Aggregates and Subqueries

10 min
Timing
6mFive retrieval questions on Lessons 3.3 and 3.4, answered in the workbook, then on the projector
4mHomework check: the inventory report and the price review run end to end with SOURCE?
Retrieval questions and answers

Questions on the slide; learners answer in the workbook's Warm-up box, then you run the SQL ones in the flat bookstore (still loaded from Session 5).

  1. Three numbers: predict them. (Customers 1 to 5 signed up before 25 January; Eve has no city.)
    SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(city), COUNT(DISTINCT city)
FROM customers
WHERE created_at < '2026-01-25';
    +----------+-------------+----------------------+
| COUNT(*) | COUNT(city) | COUNT(DISTINCT city) |
+----------+-------------+----------------------+
|        5 |           4 |                    4 |
+----------+-------------+----------------------+
    Answer: 5, 4, 4. COUNT(*) counts rows; COUNT(city) skips Eve's NULL; the four filled-in cities are all different.
  2. price > 10 and COUNT(*) > 1: which goes in WHERE and which in HAVING? Answer: price > 10 in WHERE (one row's column), COUNT(*) > 1 in HAVING (a group's aggregate).
  3. Which book is it?
    SELECT title
FROM books
WHERE pages = (SELECT MAX(pages) FROM books);
    +-------------------+
| title             |
+-------------------+
| Lord of the Rings |
+-------------------+
    Answer: Lord of the Rings: the row behind MAX(pages). If two books tied, both would come back.
  4. How many customers? (Customer 7 is Grace in Chicago, 8 is Hank with no city.)
    SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM customers
WHERE city NOT IN (SELECT city FROM customers WHERE id > 6);
    +----------+
| COUNT(*) |
+----------+
|        0 |
+----------+
    Answer: 0. The list is ('Chicago', NULL), and one NULL empties a NOT IN. Fix: AND city IS NOT NULL inside, or NOT EXISTS.
  5. What is wrong, and what is the error number?
    SELECT author, title, MAX(price)
FROM books
GROUP BY author;
    ERROR 1055 (42000): Expression #2 of SELECT list is not in GROUP BY clause and contains nonaggregated column 'bookstore.books.title' which is not functionally dependent on columns in GROUP BY clause; this is incompatible with sql_mode=only_full_group_by
    Answer: 1055: title is neither grouped nor aggregated (Tolkien's pile has two titles). Fix: drop title, or MIN(title).
How to read the warm-up
  • Q4 wrong for more than a third of the room: the NOT IN trap comes back today (the lesson's orphan query uses NOT IN (SELECT id FROM parent), safe only because a primary key is never NULL). Say so when you reach it.
  • Q1 or Q5 wrong: pair these learners with someone confident for the 4.1 lab; the migration's IS NULL check leans on COUNT.
  • Projects not running end to end: a 2-minute fix at the break, not now. Both scripts are good revision for the Part 1 assessment next session.

Lesson Plans

4.1

Table Relationships

65 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain the update, insert and delete problems of a flat table, using the bookstore's own data
  • Tell one-to-many, one-to-one and many-to-many relationships apart, and say where the foreign key goes in each
  • Move a repeated text column into its own table with CREATE TABLE, ALTER TABLE and UPDATE ... JOIN
  • Build a junction table with a composite primary key, with and without extra columns
  • Read a crow's foot ER diagram of the finished bookstore database
Timing in the room
10mFrame: one big sales table and its four anomalies; primary and foreign keys; where the key goes
30mLive demo: the update anomaly, then the migration (parent, column, UPDATE ... JOIN, NULL check, lock-in), one-to-one, a junction table
15mGuided lab: publisher_split_starter.sql in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: load bookstore.sql, read the design of orders, order_items and employees
Mental model to say

Draw the one-big-table sales sheet from the lesson (Alice's name and email on every row she bought) and name the four anomalies as you point: "duplication; change her email and miss a row (update); delete Bob's only sale and Bob is gone (delete); a new customer who bought nothing has nowhere to go (insert)."

Then the relational idea: "Store each fact once, in the table it describes, and let other tables point at it by its id. The table with the id is the parent; the table holding the pointer is the child."

Then the one question that places every foreign key, written on the board: "Can one A have many Bs? Can one B have many As?" One yes: the key goes on the many side. Two yeses: a junction table. One-to-one is a one-to-many whose key is UNIQUE.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

The lesson's own migration, in the flat bookstore, in one session. Ready learners type along; the outcome on their machines does not matter, because everyone loads bookstore.sql at 1:05.

  1. The repeated text (2m). "Lesson 3.3's report counted books per author. Look how the author is stored." 
    SELECT id, title, author
FROM books
WHERE author = 'J.R.R. Tolkien';
    +----+-------------------+----------------+
| id | title             | author         |
+----+-------------------+----------------+
|  6 | The Hobbit        | J.R.R. Tolkien |
|  9 | Lord of the Rings | J.R.R. Tolkien |
+----+-------------------+----------------+
    The same 14 characters typed twice.
  2. The update anomaly, on purpose (3m). "A staff member adds spaces to the initials on one row. No rule stops them." Ask: how many Tolkiens does the database believe in now? 
    UPDATE books SET author = 'J. R. R. Tolkien' WHERE id = 9;
SELECT author, COUNT(*) AS books
FROM books
WHERE author LIKE '%Tolkien%'
GROUP BY author
ORDER BY author;
    +------------------+-------+
| author           | books |
+------------------+-------+
| J. R. R. Tolkien |     1 |
| J.R.R. Tolkien   |     1 |
+------------------+-------+
    No error, and every report that groups by author is quietly wrong.
  3. Put it back (1m). 
    -- @status
UPDATE books SET author = 'J.R.R. Tolkien' WHERE id = 9;
    Query OK, 1 row affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 1  Warnings: 0
    Then the relational idea on the board: store each fact once, in the table it describes; point at it by id.
  4. Step 1: the parent table (3m). "Each author once, with an id. Asimov has no books yet: allowed on the 'one' side." Paste the INSERT (the point is the structure, not the typing). 
    CREATE TABLE authors (
    id          INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    name        VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
    birth_year  INT
);
INSERT INTO authors (id, name, birth_year) VALUES
    (1, 'Frank Herbert', 1920), (2, 'George Orwell', 1903),
    (3, 'Harper Lee', 1926), (4, 'F. Scott Fitzgerald', 1896),
    (5, 'Jane Austen', 1775), (6, 'J.R.R. Tolkien', 1892),
    (7, 'Aldous Huxley', 1894), (8, 'J.D. Salinger', 1919),
    (9, 'Ray Bradbury', 1920), (10, 'Isaac Asimov', 1920);
SELECT COUNT(*) AS authors FROM authors;
    +---------+
| authors |
+---------+
|      10 |
+---------+
  5. Step 2: the foreign key column (2m). "Can one author have many books? Yes, so the key goes in books." 
    ALTER TABLE books ADD COLUMN author_id INT AFTER title;
SELECT id, title, author, author_id
FROM books
WHERE id <= 3;
    +----+-----------------------+---------------+-----------+
| id | title                 | author        | author_id |
+----+-----------------------+---------------+-----------+
|  1 | Dune                  | Frank Herbert |      NULL |
|  2 | 1984                  | George Orwell |      NULL |
|  3 | To Kill a Mockingbird | Harper Lee    |      NULL |
+----+-----------------------+---------------+-----------+
    Empty (NULL) on every row: nothing is filled in yet.
  6. Step 3: fill it by matching names (4m). "For every pair of a book and an author whose name equals the book's author text, copy the id." JOIN ... ON pairs rows of two tables; Lesson 4.3 is all about it, today only this one pattern. 
    UPDATE books
JOIN authors ON authors.name = books.author
SET books.author_id = authors.id;
SELECT id, title, author, author_id
FROM books
WHERE id IN (6, 9, 10)
ORDER BY id;
    +----+-------------------+----------------+-----------+
| id | title             | author         | author_id |
+----+-------------------+----------------+-----------+
|  6 | The Hobbit        | J.R.R. Tolkien |         6 |
|  9 | Lord of the Rings | J.R.R. Tolkien |         6 |
| 10 | Fahrenheit 451    | Ray Bradbury   |         9 |
+----+-------------------+----------------+-----------+
    Both Tolkien books carry 6. (The lesson shows all ten rows; three are enough on the projector.)
  7. The check before anything is dropped (2m). "A misspelled name would have matched nothing and left a NULL. Count them BEFORE you drop the text column: DROP COLUMN has no undo." 
    SELECT COUNT(*) AS books_without_author_id
FROM books
WHERE author_id IS NULL;
    +-------------------------+
| books_without_author_id |
+-------------------------+
|                       0 |
+-------------------------+
  8. Step 4: lock it in (4m). "Three changes in one ALTER: required, the old column gone, the key declared and named." Point at MUL in the Key column: MySQL indexed the foreign key. 
    ALTER TABLE books
    MODIFY COLUMN author_id INT NOT NULL,
    DROP COLUMN author,
    ADD CONSTRAINT fk_books_author
        FOREIGN KEY (author_id) REFERENCES authors(id)
        ON DELETE RESTRICT
        ON UPDATE CASCADE;
DESCRIBE books;
    +-----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| Field     | Type         | Null | Key | Default | Extra          |
+-----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
| id        | int          | NO   | PRI | NULL    | auto_increment |
| title     | varchar(200) | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| author_id | int          | NO   | MUL | NULL    |                |
| price     | decimal(8,2) | NO   |     | NULL    |                |
| pages     | int          | YES  |     | NULL    |                |
| published | date         | YES  |     | NULL    |                |
| in_stock  | tinyint(1)   | YES  |     | 1       |                |
+-----------+--------------+------+-----+---------+----------------+
  9. Use the relationship (2m). "A book's author is one lookup away: a correlated subquery from Lesson 3.4." 
    SELECT
    title,
    (SELECT name FROM authors WHERE authors.id = books.author_id) AS author
FROM books
WHERE author_id IN (2, 6)
ORDER BY title;
    +-------------------+----------------+
| title             | author         |
+-------------------+----------------+
| 1984              | George Orwell  |
| Lord of the Rings | J.R.R. Tolkien |
| The Hobbit        | J.R.R. Tolkien |
+-------------------+----------------+
  10. Add a book the relational way (2m). "Animal Farm: Orwell's second book, by id. His name can never be split again." 
    INSERT INTO books (id, title, author_id, price, pages, published, in_stock) VALUES
    (11, 'Animal Farm', 2, 8.49, 112, '1945-08-17', TRUE);
SELECT author_id, COUNT(*) AS books
FROM books
GROUP BY author_id
HAVING COUNT(*) > 1
ORDER BY author_id;
    +-----------+-------+
| author_id | books |
+-----------+-------+
|         2 |     2 |
|         6 |     2 |
+-----------+-------+
  11. One-to-one: a UNIQUE foreign key (2m). "A profile per customer, at most one. The UNIQUE on customer_id is the 'one'." The second INSERT is refused. 
    CREATE TABLE customer_profiles (
    id             INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    customer_id    INT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
    favorite_genre VARCHAR(50),
    CONSTRAINT fk_profiles_customer
        FOREIGN KEY (customer_id) REFERENCES customers(id)
);
INSERT INTO customer_profiles (customer_id, favorite_genre) VALUES (1, 'Science Fiction');
INSERT INTO customer_profiles (customer_id, favorite_genre) VALUES (1, 'Fantasy');
    ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry '1' for key 'customer_profiles.customer_id'
    Without UNIQUE it would succeed, and the relationship would silently become one-to-many. Drop the table: the course database has no profiles.
  12. Clean up the profile table. 
    DROP TABLE customer_profiles;
  13. Many-to-many: a junction table (3m). "1984 is Fiction, Classic and Dystopian. One column cannot hold that; a third table can: one row per link, a composite primary key." Paste the 25 links. 
    CREATE TABLE book_categories (
    book_id     INT NOT NULL,
    category_id INT NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (book_id, category_id),
    CONSTRAINT fk_bc_book
        FOREIGN KEY (book_id) REFERENCES books(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    CONSTRAINT fk_bc_category
        FOREIGN KEY (category_id) REFERENCES categories(id) ON DELETE CASCADE
);
INSERT INTO book_categories (book_id, category_id) VALUES
    (1, 1), (1, 2), (2, 1), (2, 3), (2, 5), (3, 1), (3, 3), (4, 1), (4, 3),
    (5, 1), (5, 3), (6, 1), (6, 4), (7, 1), (7, 2), (7, 5), (8, 1), (8, 3),
    (9, 1), (9, 4), (10, 1), (10, 2), (10, 5), (11, 1), (11, 3);
SELECT COUNT(*) AS links FROM book_categories;
    +-------+
| links |
+-------+
|    25 |
+-------+
  14. Follow the links (1m). "Which categories is 1984 in?" 
    SELECT name
FROM categories
WHERE id IN (SELECT category_id FROM book_categories WHERE book_id = 2)
ORDER BY name;
    +-----------+
| name      |
+-----------+
| Classic   |
| Dystopian |
| Fiction   |
+-----------+
  15. The same link twice (1m). "1984 is already Dystopian." 
    INSERT INTO book_categories (book_id, category_id) VALUES (2, 5);
    ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry '2-5' for key 'book_categories.PRIMARY'
    The composite key doing its job: each pair once. Every learner's database now matches the lesson's catalog half; the project adds orders, order_items and employees, which is why everyone loads bookstore.sql next.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • The key on the wrong side. customers.order_id "so each customer points at their order". Coach with the question: "Can one customer have many orders? Then where can all those pointers live?"
  • Dropping before checking. DROP COLUMN, then discovering a NULL author_id (and the 1138 at NOT NULL). Coach the order: add, fill, COUNT the NULLs, then lock in. The lab has a typo planted to make the check matter.
  • A list in one column. categories = 'Fiction,Fantasy'. Coach with the lesson's Mistake 4: "= 'Fiction' finds nothing; LIKE '%Fiction%' finds Science Fiction too." Many values = many rows in a junction table.
Guided lab

File: labs/module-06/l4_1-table-relationships/publisher_split_starter.sql; reference labs/module-06/l4_1-table-relationships/publisher_split_solution.sql. The same migration on new data: six (fictional) editions that type each publisher's name and city into every row. It works on two practice tables (kit_editions, kit_publishers) and drops them at the end, so it is safe in either shape of the bookstore.

  • As shipped (1m): Step 1 prints the anomalies: Harbor Lane Press in Boston AND Cambridge, and North Star split in two by a typo (North Star Book).
  • Steps 2 to 6 in pairs (12m): each step is turned off with --; learners turn on one at a time and SOURCE again. Step 3 has one gap (kit_publishers.id), Step 4 one gap (row 5).
  • Expected: Step 4 finds exactly one unmatched row (5, Fahrenheit 451, North Star Book) and 0 after the fix; Step 6 shows each publisher with 2 editions. The planted typo is the point: without the check, Step 5's NOT NULL fails with 1138 (or worse, on a real migration, a book loses its publisher).
  • Circulate for: a step turned on with its comment lines too (harmless) or with the -- left on one line of a multi-line statement (1064 near the next line); Step 5 run before the fix (1138: "good, now you know why the check exists").
  • Debrief (2m): "Which city did you give Harbor Lane Press, and how did you decide?" A migration forces a decision about which copy was right; that is the update anomaly's cost.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-06/l4_1-table-relationships/navigate_links_starter.sql (needs the relational bookstore: after 1:05), then labs/module-06/l4_1-table-relationships/debug_relationships.sql (1824, 1062, 1138).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is Orders, Order Items and Staff: three more tables that finish the bookstore every later lesson uses. Its value is the design; its typing is long INSERTs. In the room (10 minutes):

  • Everyone loads the finished database: SOURCE bookstore.sql (from the course folder; or SOURCE labs/_states/bookstore.sql). It drops and rebuilds bookstore, so the flat shape is gone for good.
  • Run Step 5's counts query together (the lesson's eight-subquery SELECT plus SHOW TABLES): 10, 11, 5, 25, 8, 12, 18, 10 and eight tables on every machine.
  • Read the design aloud with SHOW CREATE TABLE orders\G: no total column on purpose (Lesson 6.3), order_items as a junction table with extra facts (quantity, price_at_time: history, not duplication), employees pointing at itself.
  • Goes home: the project's Steps 1 to 4 typed into a practice copy if they want the practice (the lesson's "I made a mistake" FAQ explains how to start over), Step 6 (compare their CREATE TABLEs with bookstore.sql), and the sketch from the lesson's "Before the Next Lesson".
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Where does the key go for authors and books, and why? Answer: books.author_id: one author, many books.
  • Why count the NULL author_ids before DROP COLUMN? Answer: a NULL means a name did not match; dropping the text would lose that book's author for good.
  • What turns a one-to-many into a one-to-one? Answer: UNIQUE on the foreign key column.
  • Why does order_items store price_at_time when books has a price? Answer: it records what the customer paid then; the book's price may change later.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6:

Answers: Q1 B) addresses.customer_id; Q2 C) Students and the courses they take; Q3 Three rows: books 2, 7 and 10, each with 3; Q4 A) An update anomaly: one fact stored twice now disagrees with itself; Q5 D) One-to-many: a customer can now have several profiles; Q6 ERROR 1824 (HY000): Failed to open the referenced table 'book'. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: learners overwhelmed by the live ALTERs watch and do the lab only (it is the same four steps on six rows); the workbook's club example is a complete, small many-to-many to reread at home.

Stretch: navigate_links_starter.sql and debug_relationships.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (a music library schema) and the Bonus Challenge (build the authors list with INSERT ... SELECT: do the ids match?).

4.2

Foreign Keys & Referential Integrity

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain referential integrity and predict when MySQL raises errors 1452 and 1451
  • Choose RESTRICT, CASCADE or SET NULL for ON DELETE and ON UPDATE, and justify the choice
  • Add, drop and change a named foreign key on an existing table, cleaning up orphan rows first
  • List a database's foreign keys and their rules from information_schema
  • Diagnose the errors raised by a badly formed key (3780, 6125, 1830)
Timing in the room
10mFrame: referential integrity; four moments MySQL checks; three rules and the two questions
25mLive demo: 1452, 1451, CASCADE and SET NULL in transactions, ON UPDATE, the rules from information_schema, a key on dirty data
20mGuided lab: delete_rules_starter.sql
20mStart the lesson project: book reviews, Steps 1 to 4
Mental model to say

"Referential integrity means every foreign key value points at a row that really exists. A child without its parent is an orphan, and a declared key makes orphans impossible." Then the four moments, as a two-by-two on the board: child INSERT or UPDATE of the key → does the parent exist? (1452) · parent DELETE or key UPDATE → do children point at it? then apply the rule (1451 under RESTRICT).

Then the rules, with the two questions: "Can the child exist without its parent? No: CASCADE (an order line without its order). Yes, and it must keep naming the parent: RESTRICT (an order must keep its customer: deactivate the customer instead). Yes, and the link is optional: SET NULL (staff stay when a manager leaves)." And the safety net: when in doubt, RESTRICT: a refused delete costs one step, a wrong cascade costs data.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

From a freshly loaded bookstore.sql. Every destructive test runs inside START TRANSACTION ... ROLLBACK; say so every time, and end every block with ROLLBACK before anything else.

  1. An order for nobody (2m). "Customer 999 does not exist." Read the message from the END: the constraint, the table, the column, the parent. 
    INSERT INTO orders (customer_id, order_date) VALUES (999, '2026-06-15');
    ERROR 1452 (23000): Cannot add or update a child row: a foreign key constraint fails (`bookstore`.`orders`, CONSTRAINT `fk_orders_customer` FOREIGN KEY (`customer_id`) REFERENCES `customers` (`id`) ON DELETE RESTRICT ON UPDATE CASCADE)
    "add or update a CHILD row": 1452 is always about the child.
  2. Delete a customer with orders (2m). "Alice has three orders, and fk_orders_customer says RESTRICT." 
    DELETE FROM customers WHERE id = 1;
    ERROR 1451 (23000): Cannot delete or update a parent row: a foreign key constraint fails (`bookstore`.`orders`, CONSTRAINT `fk_orders_customer` FOREIGN KEY (`customer_id`) REFERENCES `customers` (`id`) ON DELETE RESTRICT ON UPDATE CASCADE)
    "delete or update a PARENT row": 1451. Alice and her history stay; RESTRICT makes you decide.
  3. CASCADE, safely (4m). "Order lines mean nothing without their order. I wrap the test in a transaction: START TRANSACTION begins, ROLLBACK undoes everything (Lesson 7.2)." 
    START TRANSACTION;
SELECT id, order_id, book_id FROM order_items WHERE order_id = 1;
DELETE FROM orders WHERE id = 1;
SELECT id, order_id, book_id FROM order_items WHERE order_id = 1;
ROLLBACK;
    +----+----------+---------+
| id | order_id | book_id |
+----+----------+---------+
|  1 |        1 |       1 |
|  2 |        1 |       2 |
+----+----------+---------+
Empty set
    One DELETE on orders removed two rows of order_items. After ROLLBACK they are back.
  4. SET NULL (3m). "Priya manages Tom, Lena and Ben. Priya leaves." 
    START TRANSACTION;
DELETE FROM employees WHERE id = 3;
SELECT id, first_name, manager_id
FROM employees
WHERE id IN (4, 5, 10)
ORDER BY id;
ROLLBACK;
    +----+------------+------------+
| id | first_name | manager_id |
+----+------------+------------+
|  4 | Tom        |       NULL |
|  5 | Lena       |       NULL |
| 10 | Ben        |       NULL |
+----+------------+------------+
    The reports stay, with no manager. The column must allow NULL (error 1830 otherwise; Step 11).
  5. ON UPDATE CASCADE (2m). "Renumber Orwell from 2 to 20: his books follow." 
    START TRANSACTION;
UPDATE authors SET id = 20 WHERE id = 2;
SELECT id, title, author_id
FROM books
WHERE author_id = 20
ORDER BY id;
ROLLBACK;
    +----+-------------+-----------+
| id | title       | author_id |
+----+-------------+-----------+
|  2 | 1984        |        20 |
| 11 | Animal Farm |        20 |
+----+-------------+-----------+
  6. No ON UPDATE rule = NO ACTION (1m). "The employees self-reference has no ON UPDATE." 
    UPDATE employees SET id = 100 WHERE id = 1;
    ERROR 1451 (23000): Cannot delete or update a parent row: a foreign key constraint fails (`bookstore`.`employees`, CONSTRAINT `fk_employees_manager` FOREIGN KEY (`manager_id`) REFERENCES `employees` (`id`) ON DELETE SET NULL)
    NO ACTION behaves exactly like RESTRICT in MySQL.
  7. Every rule in the bookstore (3m). "information_schema describes all the other databases. Read each row as a decision." Walk the two-question diagram: can the child exist without its parent? Must it keep naming it? 
    SELECT TABLE_NAME, CONSTRAINT_NAME, REFERENCED_TABLE_NAME,
       DELETE_RULE, UPDATE_RULE
FROM information_schema.REFERENTIAL_CONSTRAINTS
WHERE CONSTRAINT_SCHEMA = 'bookstore'
ORDER BY TABLE_NAME, CONSTRAINT_NAME;
    +-----------------+----------------------+-----------------------+-------------+-------------+
| TABLE_NAME      | CONSTRAINT_NAME      | REFERENCED_TABLE_NAME | DELETE_RULE | UPDATE_RULE |
+-----------------+----------------------+-----------------------+-------------+-------------+
| book_categories | fk_bc_book           | books                 | CASCADE     | NO ACTION   |
| book_categories | fk_bc_category       | categories            | CASCADE     | NO ACTION   |
| books           | fk_books_author      | authors               | RESTRICT    | CASCADE     |
| employees       | fk_employees_manager | employees             | SET NULL    | NO ACTION   |
| order_items     | fk_items_book        | books                 | RESTRICT    | CASCADE     |
| order_items     | fk_items_order       | orders                | CASCADE     | CASCADE     |
| orders          | fk_orders_customer   | customers             | RESTRICT    | CASCADE     |
+-----------------+----------------------+-----------------------+-------------+-------------+
  8. A table that started without a key (2m). "Most real tables get their keys late. Kim's department 7 does not exist." 
    CREATE TABLE demo_departments (
    id   INT PRIMARY KEY,
    name VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL
);
CREATE TABLE demo_staff (
    id            INT PRIMARY KEY,
    name          VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL,
    department_id INT
);
INSERT INTO demo_departments (id, name) VALUES (1, 'Front desk'), (2, 'Warehouse');
INSERT INTO demo_staff (id, name, department_id) VALUES
    (1, 'Ana', 1), (2, 'Raj', 2), (3, 'Kim', 7);
  9. Adding the key fails (2m). "MySQL copies the table to add the key and finds the orphan." The #sql name differs on every server. 
    ALTER TABLE demo_staff
    ADD CONSTRAINT fk_staff_department
        FOREIGN KEY (department_id) REFERENCES demo_departments(id)
        ON DELETE SET NULL;
    ERROR 1452 (23000): Cannot add or update a child row: a foreign key constraint fails (`bookstore`.`#sql-80218_2020`, CONSTRAINT `fk_staff_department` FOREIGN KEY (`department_id`) REFERENCES `demo_departments` (`id`) ON DELETE SET NULL)
  10. Find the orphan, fix it, add the key (3m). "Find with a subquery, fix with the truth (Kim is not assigned yet: NULL), then the key goes on." 
    SELECT id, name, department_id
FROM demo_staff
WHERE department_id NOT IN (SELECT id FROM demo_departments);
UPDATE demo_staff SET department_id = NULL WHERE id = 3;
ALTER TABLE demo_staff
    ADD CONSTRAINT fk_staff_department
        FOREIGN KEY (department_id) REFERENCES demo_departments(id)
        ON DELETE SET NULL;
    +----+------+---------------+
| id | name | department_id |
+----+------+---------------+
|  3 | Kim  |             7 |
+----+------+---------------+
    Choose the fix that tells the truth about the data; deleting Kim would be the most drastic.
  11. A badly formed key: 1830 (1m). "SET NULL on a column that forbids NULL." 
    CREATE TABLE demo_notes (
    id          INT PRIMARY KEY,
    customer_id INT NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT fk_notes_customer FOREIGN KEY (customer_id)
        REFERENCES customers(id) ON DELETE SET NULL
);
    ERROR 1830 (HY000): Column 'customer_id' cannot be NOT NULL: needed in a foreign key constraint 'fk_notes_customer' SET NULL
  12. Clean up. Children first. 
    DROP TABLE demo_staff;
DROP TABLE demo_departments;
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Reading 1451 and 1452 the wrong way round. Coach: "Read the first words: 'add or update a CHILD row' (1452: the parent is missing) or 'delete or update a PARENT row' (1451: children still point at it)."
  • Cascading everything. "CASCADE makes the error go away." Coach with the lesson's warning: if orders cascaded from customers, one DELETE would erase years of sales without a warning. Ask the two questions out loud for that key.
  • Switching the checks off. SET FOREIGN_KEY_CHECKS = 0 to get past an error. Coach: "Turning it back on does not re-check. The error was telling you about bad data." Never in class; the lesson's Mistake 5 shows the orphan that slips in.
Guided lab

File: labs/module-06/l4_2-foreign-keys/delete_rules_starter.sql; reference labs/module-06/l4_2-foreign-keys/delete_rules_solution.sql. Five tests of the bookstore's rules on rows the lesson did not use; each in a transaction, each ending in ROLLBACK. As shipped, the changing line is turned off, so learners see the "before" picture, predict in the workbook, then turn the line on.

  • Predict first (3m): for each test, which rule fires and what the SELECT will show. Learners look rules up with the demo's information_schema query.
  • Run them (12m), one TODO at a time. Expected: links 25 → 22 (Dystopian's three links cascade); Sofia's manager_id NULL (SET NULL); 7 customers left (Hank has no orders: RESTRICT has nothing to protect); orders 6 and 11 now say customer 70 (ON UPDATE CASCADE); order lines 18 → 16 (order 9 had two). The last row is unchanged after every ROLLBACK: 5, 25, 8, 12, 18 and Sofia's manager 6.
  • Circulate for: a DELETE run at the prompt outside a transaction (if it happened: SOURCE labs/_states/bookstore.sql), and a forgotten ROLLBACK when someone experiments by hand.
  • Debrief (3m): the file's bonus question: "why is 'delete Alice' not in this file?" (1451: her orders RESTRICT it; an error would stop a lab file).
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-06/l4_2-foreign-keys/orphan_audit_starter.sql (find two orphan gift cards, fix them, add the key with SET NULL, prove it), then labs/module-06/l4_2-foreign-keys/debug_fk_errors.sql (1452, 1451, 3780, 1830, 6125).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is Book Reviews with the Right Rules: one table, two foreign keys, two different delete rules, each proved with a test that is undone. In the room: Steps 1 to 4 (20 minutes).

  • Step 1 (5m): decide the rules out loud with the two questions. Review → book: CASCADE (a review of nothing is meaningless). Review → customer: SET NULL (keep the review, "by a former customer"), so customer_id must allow NULL.
  • Step 2 (5m): create the table and insert four reviews (paste).
  • Step 3 (3m): the insert for book 42 is refused with 1452.
  • Step 4 (7m): delete The Great Gatsby (4) inside a transaction; its two reviews go with it; ROLLBACK.
  • Done in the room = the four reviews exist, the 1452 was seen, the CASCADE test was run and rolled back.
  • Goes home: Step 5 (the SET NULL test with Carol) and Step 6 (list the rules from information_schema, then drop the table: the course database has no reviews).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • 1452 or 1451: which one is about a missing parent? Answer: 1452 ("add or update a child row").
  • Which rule does order_items → orders use, and why? Answer: CASCADE: a line has no meaning without its order.
  • What happens when you leave ON DELETE out? Answer: NO ACTION, which MySQL enforces exactly like RESTRICT.
  • Why must a SET NULL column allow NULL? Answer: the rule writes NULL into it; NOT NULL makes the key impossible (1830).

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12:

Answers: Q7 B) ERROR 1452 (23000): Cannot add or update a child row; Q8 A) 16; Q9 NULL (Nina stays, with no manager); Q10 C) go with it: ON DELETE CASCADE; Q11 D) DROP FOREIGN KEY fk_items_order, then ADD CONSTRAINT fk_items_order ... ON DELETE RESTRICT; Q12 SET NULL. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: the workbook's worked example (deleting Eve: refused, then her order, then her) shows RESTRICT and CASCADE working together in one transaction. Learners who struggle do tests 1 to 3 of the lab only.

Stretch: orphan_audit_starter.sql and debug_fk_errors.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (tighten a rule, then restore it) and the Bonus Challenge (a three-level cascade: how many rows disappeared in total?).

2:40

Relationship Map

10 min
How it runs

A consolidation of both lessons, on paper. Learners draw the eight tables as boxes and every foreign key as a crow's foot line (the lesson's ER diagram is the model, but they draw from memory first), then label each line with its delete rule. Then they check against the database:

SELECT TABLE_NAME, CONSTRAINT_NAME, DELETE_RULE, UPDATE_RULE
FROM information_schema.REFERENTIAL_CONSTRAINTS
WHERE CONSTRAINT_SCHEMA = 'bookstore'
ORDER BY TABLE_NAME, CONSTRAINT_NAME;
+-----------------+----------------------+-------------+-------------+
| TABLE_NAME      | CONSTRAINT_NAME      | DELETE_RULE | UPDATE_RULE |
+-----------------+----------------------+-------------+-------------+
| book_categories | fk_bc_book           | CASCADE     | NO ACTION   |
| book_categories | fk_bc_category       | CASCADE     | NO ACTION   |
| books           | fk_books_author      | RESTRICT    | CASCADE     |
| employees       | fk_employees_manager | SET NULL    | NO ACTION   |
| order_items     | fk_items_book        | RESTRICT    | CASCADE     |
| order_items     | fk_items_order       | CASCADE     | CASCADE     |
| orders          | fk_orders_customer   | RESTRICT    | CASCADE     |
+-----------------+----------------------+-------------+-------------+
  • Seven keys: one 1:M per line, plus the employees self-reference. Ask for the two junction tables (book_categories, order_items) and what extra facts order_items carries.
  • For each rule, one learner says the reason in one sentence (the lesson's "Choosing the Rules" table). Every UPDATE_RULE that says NO ACTION was simply not written: it behaves like RESTRICT.
  • This map is the best single revision sheet for the Part 1 assessment: tell them to bring it.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Learners answer in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Sort before Session 7: question 1's misses predict trouble with the assessment's table-design task; question 2's with reading its error messages.

  1. For one-to-many, one-to-one and many-to-many: where does the foreign key go? Answer: on the many side; on either side with UNIQUE (usually the optional table); in a junction table with two foreign keys and a composite primary key.
  2. You get ERROR 1452. Which table were you changing (parent or child), and what is missing? And ERROR 1451? Answer: 1452: inserting or updating a CHILD whose parent does not exist. 1451: deleting or changing a PARENT that children still point at (under RESTRICT or NO ACTION).
  3. Choose an ON DELETE rule for order_items.book_id → books and justify it. Answer: RESTRICT: a book that was sold must stay on file for the sales history (mark it out of stock instead). CASCADE would delete sales lines; SET NULL would forget what was sold.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Lesson projects: 4.1 (compare your design with bookstore.sql; type the three tables into a practice database if you want the practice); 4.2 Steps 5 and 6.
  • Lesson exercises. 4.1: Exercises 1 to 3 (Exercise 4 is a stretch). 4.2: Exercises 1 to 3 (Exercise 4 is a stretch).
  • Both Quick Quizzes on the lesson pages; both journal entries.
  • Labs not finished in class: navigate_links_starter.sql, orphan_audit_starter.sql, the two debug handouts; the relationship map if it was cut.
  • Prepare for the Part 1 assessment (Session 7, second half, 60 minutes, open book): reread your project scripts from Sessions 3 to 6 and your two debug-handout notes; redo one exercise per lesson from Module 2 to 4 without looking; make sure SOURCE works from your course folder. It covers creating tables with keys and constraints, CRUD, filtering and sorting, aggregates, subqueries, foreign keys, and the basic joins taught in the first half of Session 7.
  • Before the Next Lesson (from 4.2): reload bookstore.sql, run the "every rule" query once more, explain each rule aloud.
Bring list for Session 7
  • A laptop with bookstore.sql loaded (8 tables, the counts 10, 11, 5, 25, 8, 12, 18, 10), charged: the assessment needs your own machine for 60 minutes.
  • The course folder with labs/ and every project script so far: the assessment is open book (lesson pages, your notes, your own scripts; no other people and no AI assistants).
  • The workbook with today's relationship map, and the journal.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: 4.1 demo Steps 2 to 8 (anomaly, the four migration steps, the NULL check); everyone loading bookstore.sql; 4.2 demo Steps 1 to 4; the 4.2 lab tests 1 to 3.
  • Cut first (saves 10m): the relationship map; it becomes homework with the query in the workbook.
  • Cut second (saves 4m): 4.1 demo Steps 11 and 12 (one-to-one): show the slide; the self-check Q5 covers it.
  • Cut third (saves 7m): 4.2 demo Steps 8 to 10 (a key added to dirty data): orphan_audit_starter.sql does it at home.
Running short: what to add
  • Run debug_fk_errors.sql together: five errors, five sentences (child or parent, insert or delete or create).
  • The 4.2 Exercise 4 live: tighten fk_items_order to RESTRICT, show the 1451 on order 12, restore CASCADE, check with information_schema.
  • A mini-design on the board: a library (books, members, loans). Where does each key go, and which delete rule for loans → members?
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 7: Joins and the Part 1 Assessment

How to teach the seventh session of MySQL Foundations, the last of Part 1: Lesson 4.3 (INNER and LEFT joins, the anti-join, self-joins, joins with GROUP BY and the three silent join bugs), then the 60-minute Part 1 assessment on a new database (the Riverside Cooking School), a short review, and a close that works whether the course ends here or continues with Part 2.

1 lesson + assessment 3 hrs session Lesson 4.3 · Part 1 assessment Session 7 of 14 · Part 1

How to Use This Guide

  • Two halves. The first half teaches Lesson 4.3 in the usual rhythm (frame, live demo, guided lab, project start); the second half is the Part 1 assessment (60 minutes) and a short review, then a close that works both when Part 1 runs on its own and when Part 2 follows.
  • The assessment lives in two places. Learners work in labs/module-07/part1-assessment/part1_assessment_starter.sql: the file IS the brief (rules, data, tasks, hand-in). You score with the rubric in final-assessment.html at the kit root, Part 1 assessment section, whose instructor answer notes hold the reference output (from labs/module-07/part1-assessment/part1_assessment_solution.sql) and the common wrong answers with their real numbers. The Answer Key repeats the reference output.
  • Every SQL block below was run on MySQL 9.7.2 on a fresh bookstore.sql (Lesson 4.3) or in the assessment's own practice database kit_riverside. Type the short joins; the long ones can be pasted.
  • Keep lesson_13.html open for the join diagrams. Labs live in labs/module-07/.
  • Joins are the most used and most interviewed-about skill in SQL, and learners meet them one hour before an assessment that includes them. The assessment is designed for that: every join task also accepts a subquery answer (Lessons 3.4 and 4.1), and the rubric scores the result and the reasoning, not the technique.
The #1 risk of Session 7: the assessment starts late

Sixty minutes is the minimum for the assessment; every minute lost before 1:35 comes out of it, and learners who rush Part B make the mistakes the rubric is built to catch. Protect the 1:25 start: the 4.3 lab is cut first (it continues at home), then the project start. Have the starter file on every laptop BEFORE the break (put it on the shared folder or USB stick at 0:00; the break slide says "copy it now"). Protect the 60 minutes: no new content after 1:25; questions about the environment only (a client that will not start, a file that will not open), never about the SQL. If the room starts late anyway, keep the 60 minutes and shorten the review to five minutes: the review is the only segment that can go home (the Answer Key and the reference solution can be shared after scoring).

Before the Session

  • ☐ Data: every laptop has bookstore.sql loaded (Session 6's bring list). The assessment builds its own database (kit_riverside) and drops it at the end; it needs nothing else.
  • ☐ Files: labs/module-07/ WITHOUT the two *_solution.sql files and WITHOUT part1-assessment/part1_assessment_solution.sql. Run python3 labs/check_labs.py module-07 once (7 files, every line PASS). Print or open final-assessment.html (Part 1 assessment section): the rubric, the scoring sheet and the answer notes.
  • ☐ Hand-in channel: decide now how files come back (a shared folder, a USB stick passed around, email, the LMS). Learners rename the file part1_assessment_<name>.sql. Test it with your own file.
  • ☐ Projector machine: bookstore.sql loaded; terminal at 18 pt; lesson_13.html open. For the review, the reference solution's output ready to show (the Answer Key has it).
  • ☐ Board: "What does one row of this join stand for?" in large letters (it stays up during the assessment); next to it the join decision: "must every row of one table appear? → put it first, LEFT JOIN; only the unmatched? → WHERE right.id IS NULL".
  • ☐ Room for the assessment: power for every laptop, seats spread out if possible, a visible clock. Learners with extended time agreed in advance (see the accommodations in the assessment section).
  • ☐ If Part 1 runs on its own: certificates or completion letters to fill in after scoring, and the course evaluation link for the close. If Part 2 follows: Session 8's bring list on the last slide.
Known traps today
SymptomCauseFix
ERROR 1052 ... ambiguousA column (usually id) that exists in both joined tablesQualify it: o.id, c.id.
ERROR 1054 Unknown column 'books.title'The table name used after an alias was givenUse the alias everywhere in that query.
ERROR 1066 Not unique table/aliasThe same table joined twice without aliases (a self-join)Give each copy its own alias: employees AS e, employees AS m.
A join returns 110 or 96 rowsA missing ON (a silent cross join)Add the ON condition.
Counts too high after a joinFan-out: one row per order line, not per orderCOUNT(DISTINCT o.id), or count before you join.
Assessment: Failed to open fileThe client was started outside the course folderexit, cd into the folder that holds labs/, log in, SOURCE again.
Assessment: everything after A1 failsThe CREATE TABLE has an error, so the enrollments INSERT fails tooRead the FIRST error; fix A1; SOURCE again (every run rebuilds kit_riverside).

Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-up: Relationships and Foreign Keys

10 min
Timing
6mFive retrieval questions on Lessons 4.1 and 4.2, in the workbook, then on the projector
4mHomework check: book_reviews Steps 5 and 6; is bookstore.sql loaded on every laptop (8 tables)?
Retrieval questions and answers
  1. One order has many lines. Where does the foreign key go? Answer: order_items.order_id (the many side).
  2. Predict the count. (Order 3 has one line; fk_items_order is ON DELETE CASCADE.)
    START TRANSACTION;
DELETE FROM orders WHERE id = 3;
SELECT COUNT(*) AS items FROM order_items;
ROLLBACK;
    +-------+
| items |
+-------+
|    17 |
+-------+
    Answer: 17: the order's one line went with it; ROLLBACK put both back.
  3. What happens, and why?
    DELETE FROM authors WHERE id = 6;
    ERROR 1451 (23000): Cannot delete or update a parent row: a foreign key constraint fails (`bookstore`.`books`, CONSTRAINT `fk_books_author` FOREIGN KEY (`author_id`) REFERENCES `authors` (`id`) ON DELETE RESTRICT ON UPDATE CASCADE)
    Answer: 1451: Tolkien has books and fk_books_author is RESTRICT. "delete or update a PARENT row".
  4. A key is declared with no ON DELETE. What does MySQL do when a parent with children is deleted? Answer: it refuses: NO ACTION behaves exactly like RESTRICT.
  5. How many links?
    SELECT COUNT(*) AS links
FROM book_categories
WHERE book_id IN (SELECT id FROM books WHERE author_id = 6);
    +-------+
| links |
+-------+
|     4 |
+-------+
    Answer: 4: Tolkien's two books are each Fiction and Fantasy. Today the same question becomes one join instead of a nested IN.
How to read the warm-up
  • Q5 slow or wrong: following keys through two tables is exactly what a join does in one step; say "after this hour, you will write that as a join".
  • Q2 or Q3 wrong: the assessment's Part A asks for delete rules with a reason; point those learners to their Session 6 relationship map before the break.

Lesson Plan

4.3

Joins

65 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Combine matching rows from two or more tables with INNER JOIN ... ON, using table aliases
  • Keep unmatched rows with LEFT JOIN, and find rows with no match using the anti-join pattern
  • Explain what RIGHT JOIN and CROSS JOIN return, and when (rarely) to use them
  • Join a table to itself to read a hierarchy such as employees and managers
  • Combine joins with GROUP BY without the two silent traps: COUNT(*) on outer joins and row fan-out
Timing in the room
10mFrame: a join pairs rows; INNER keeps pairs, LEFT keeps every left row; one joined row stands for what?
25mLive demo: INNER, aliases, LEFT, the anti-join, a join without ON, a self-join, four tables, GROUP BY, three traps
20mGuided lab: catalog_joins_starter.sql in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: the sales report, Step 1
Mental model to say

Two short lists on the board, a guest list and the RSVPs (the lesson's journal prompt): "A JOIN pairs each guest with their RSVPs and keeps the pairs: a guest who never replied is not in the result. A LEFT JOIN keeps every guest, and where there is no RSVP it writes NULL. Same lists, same condition, one more word."

Then the question that prevents the silent bugs, pointing at the board: "what does one row of this join stand for?" "Join customers to orders and one row is an order. Add order_items and one row is an order LINE: Alice's first order now appears twice. Count after that and you count lines."

Then where conditions go: "ON says how the tables connect. WHERE filters the joined rows. On a LEFT JOIN, a condition on the right table goes in ON, or the NULL rows you wanted to keep are filtered away."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

The lesson's own examples on a fresh bookstore.sql. Ask for a row count before every join: it is the habit that catches fan-out and missing ON clauses.

  1. INNER JOIN: only the matches (3m). "An order row says customer_id 1; the name lives in customers. A join pairs each row with the rows of another table that meet a condition, usually foreign key equals primary key." Point at the ON line. 
    SELECT books.title, authors.name AS author
FROM books
INNER JOIN authors ON books.author_id = authors.id
ORDER BY books.title;
    +------------------------+---------------------+
| title                  | author              |
+------------------------+---------------------+
| 1984                   | George Orwell       |
| Animal Farm            | George Orwell       |
| Brave New World        | Aldous Huxley       |
| Dune                   | Frank Herbert       |
| Fahrenheit 451         | Ray Bradbury        |
| Lord of the Rings      | J.R.R. Tolkien      |
| Pride and Prejudice    | Jane Austen         |
| The Catcher in the Rye | J.D. Salinger       |
| The Great Gatsby       | F. Scott Fitzgerald |
| The Hobbit             | J.R.R. Tolkien      |
| To Kill a Mockingbird  | Harper Lee          |
+------------------------+---------------------+
    Eleven books, eleven rows. Asimov is absent: an inner join keeps only pairs.
  2. Aliases and WHERE (2m). "b and a are short names. ON says how the tables connect; WHERE says which results you want." 
    SELECT b.title, b.price, a.name AS author
FROM books AS b
JOIN authors AS a ON b.author_id = a.id
WHERE a.name = 'George Orwell'
ORDER BY b.title;
    +-------------+-------+---------------+
| title       | price | author        |
+-------------+-------+---------------+
| 1984        |  9.99 | George Orwell |
| Animal Farm |  8.49 | George Orwell |
+-------------+-------+---------------+
    JOIN on its own means INNER JOIN. Once a table has an alias, use only the alias (Step 11).
  3. LEFT JOIN: keep every left row (3m). "Same tables, same ON, one more word." Ask: how many rows now? Then run it. 
    SELECT a.name AS author, b.title
FROM authors AS a
LEFT JOIN books AS b ON b.author_id = a.id
ORDER BY a.id, b.title;
    +---------------------+------------------------+
| author              | title                  |
+---------------------+------------------------+
| Frank Herbert       | Dune                   |
| George Orwell       | 1984                   |
| George Orwell       | Animal Farm            |
| Harper Lee          | To Kill a Mockingbird  |
| F. Scott Fitzgerald | The Great Gatsby       |
| Jane Austen         | Pride and Prejudice    |
| J.R.R. Tolkien      | Lord of the Rings      |
| J.R.R. Tolkien      | The Hobbit             |
| Aldous Huxley       | Brave New World        |
| J.D. Salinger       | The Catcher in the Rye |
| Ray Bradbury        | Fahrenheit 451         |
| Isaac Asimov        | NULL                   |
+---------------------+------------------------+
    Twelve: the eleven pairs plus Asimov with NULL. The left table is the one before the word JOIN.
  4. The anti-join (3m). "The NULLs are a tool. Keep only the rows where the right side is missing." 
    SELECT c.first_name, c.last_name
FROM customers AS c
LEFT JOIN orders AS o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.id IS NULL
ORDER BY c.id;
    +------------+-----------+
| first_name | last_name |
+------------+-----------+
| Carol      | Williams  |
| Hank       | Taylor    |
+------------+-----------+
    Test the right table's primary key: it can only be NULL when there was no match. NOT EXISTS (Lesson 3.4) answers the same question.
  5. Books never sold (1m). 
    SELECT b.title
FROM books AS b
LEFT JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.book_id = b.id
WHERE oi.id IS NULL
ORDER BY b.title;
    +------------------------+
| title                  |
+------------------------+
| The Catcher in the Rye |
| The Great Gatsby       |
+------------------------+
  6. A join without ON (1m). "Forget the ON and MySQL does not complain." 
    SELECT COUNT(*) AS rows_returned
FROM books
JOIN authors;
    +---------------+
| rows_returned |
+---------------+
|           110 |
+---------------+
    11 × 10 = 110: a cross join. If a join returns far more rows than expected, check the ON first.
  7. Self-join: two roles, one table (3m). "employees.manager_id points at another employee. e plays the employee, m the manager." 
    SELECT
    CONCAT(e.first_name, ' ', e.last_name) AS employee,
    CONCAT(m.first_name, ' ', m.last_name) AS manager
FROM employees AS e
LEFT JOIN employees AS m ON e.manager_id = m.id
ORDER BY e.id;
    +---------------+--------------+
| employee      | manager      |
+---------------+--------------+
| Maria Santos  | NULL         |
| Daniel Reyes  | Maria Santos |
| Priya Nair    | Daniel Reyes |
| Tom Becker    | Priya Nair   |
| Lena Park     | Priya Nair   |
| Marcus Hill   | Daniel Reyes |
| Sofia Alvarez | Marcus Hill  |
| James Okafor  | Maria Santos |
| Nina Kowalski | James Okafor |
| Ben Carter    | Priya Nair   |
+---------------+--------------+
    Maria survives only because this is a LEFT JOIN; an inner join would drop the head of the company. To walk the whole tree: Lesson 5.1 (recursive CTE).
  8. Four tables: who bought what (2m). "Each JOIN adds one table to the row you are building. Walk the keys from orders outward." 
    SELECT o.id AS order_id, o.order_date, b.title, oi.quantity
FROM orders AS o
JOIN customers AS c    ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
JOIN books AS b        ON oi.book_id = b.id
WHERE c.first_name = 'Alice'
ORDER BY o.order_date, b.title;
    +----------+------------+---------------------+----------+
| order_id | order_date | title               | quantity |
+----------+------------+---------------------+----------+
|        1 | 2026-01-15 | 1984                |        2 |
|        1 | 2026-01-15 | Dune                |        1 |
|        2 | 2026-02-20 | Pride and Prejudice |        1 |
|        8 | 2026-04-05 | Lord of the Rings   |        1 |
+----------+------------+---------------------+----------+
    Order 1 appears twice: one row per order LINE. Remember that before adding things up.
  9. Joins with GROUP BY: zeros included (2m). "Books per author, Asimov included. COUNT the right table's id." 
    SELECT a.name AS author, COUNT(b.id) AS books
FROM authors AS a
LEFT JOIN books AS b ON b.author_id = a.id
GROUP BY a.id, a.name
ORDER BY books DESC, author;
    +---------------------+-------+
| author              | books |
+---------------------+-------+
| George Orwell       |     2 |
| J.R.R. Tolkien      |     2 |
| Aldous Huxley       |     1 |
| F. Scott Fitzgerald |     1 |
| Frank Herbert       |     1 |
| Harper Lee          |     1 |
| J.D. Salinger       |     1 |
| Jane Austen         |     1 |
| Ray Bradbury        |     1 |
| Isaac Asimov        |     0 |
+---------------------+-------+
  10. What each customer spent (2m). "Both joins LEFT so Carol and Hank stay; COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) counts orders, not lines; COALESCE turns their NULL sums into 0." 
    SELECT
    CONCAT(c.first_name, ' ', c.last_name)           AS customer,
    COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)                             AS orders,
    COALESCE(SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time), 0) AS spent
FROM customers AS c
LEFT JOIN orders AS o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.first_name, c.last_name
ORDER BY spent DESC, customer;
    +----------------+--------+-------+
| customer       | orders | spent |
+----------------+--------+-------+
| Alice Johnson  |      3 | 58.95 |
| Dave Brown     |      2 | 45.96 |
| Frank Miller   |      2 | 41.96 |
| Grace Wilson   |      2 | 33.97 |
| Bob Smith      |      2 | 33.47 |
| Eve Davis      |      1 | 22.98 |
| Carol Williams |      0 |  0.00 |
| Hank Taylor    |      0 |  0.00 |
+----------------+--------+-------+
  11. Trap 1: an ambiguous column. 
    SELECT id, title, name
FROM books
JOIN authors ON books.author_id = authors.id;
    ERROR 1052 (23000): Column 'id' in field list is ambiguous
    Qualify it: books.id or b.id.
  12. Trap 2: WHERE on the right table (silent) (2m). "Every customer, with their orders from May on." Predict how many customers come back. 
    SELECT c.first_name, o.order_date
FROM customers AS c
LEFT JOIN orders AS o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.order_date >= '2026-05-01'
ORDER BY c.first_name;
    +------------+------------+
| first_name | order_date |
+------------+------------+
| Dave       | 2026-06-07 |
| Frank      | 2026-05-02 |
| Grace      | 2026-05-19 |
+------------+------------+
    Three, not eight: for customers without a May order the date is NULL and WHERE drops them. The LEFT JOIN has quietly become an inner join.
  13. The fix: the condition goes in ON (1m). 
    SELECT c.first_name, o.order_date
FROM customers AS c
LEFT JOIN orders AS o
       ON o.customer_id = c.id
      AND o.order_date >= '2026-05-01'
ORDER BY c.first_name;
    +------------+------------+
| first_name | order_date |
+------------+------------+
| Alice      | NULL       |
| Bob        | NULL       |
| Carol      | NULL       |
| Dave       | 2026-06-07 |
| Eve        | NULL       |
| Frank      | 2026-05-02 |
| Grace      | 2026-05-19 |
| Hank       | NULL       |
+------------+------------+
    Eight customers; the condition decides which orders match without removing anyone.
  14. Trap 3: COUNT(*) after a LEFT JOIN (silent). "Asimov has no books." 
    SELECT a.name, COUNT(*) AS books
FROM authors AS a
LEFT JOIN books AS b ON b.author_id = a.id
WHERE a.id IN (2, 10)
GROUP BY a.id, a.name
ORDER BY a.id;
    +---------------+-------+
| name          | books |
+---------------+-------+
| George Orwell |     2 |
| Isaac Asimov  |     1 |
+---------------+-------+
    COUNT(*) counted his padded row. Count the right table's column: COUNT(b.id).
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Ambiguous or alias-mismatched columns (1052, 1054). Coach: "Which table does this column come from? Write it in front, with the alias." Many developers qualify every column in a join.
  • A LEFT JOIN that silently became an inner join (a right-table condition in WHERE). Coach: "Who disappeared? Carol and Hank? Then your WHERE removed the NULL rows: move the condition into ON."
  • Counting after a fan-out. Coach with the board question: "What does one row stand for here?" Then COUNT(DISTINCT ...), or count the right table's id after a LEFT JOIN.
Guided lab

File: labs/module-07/l4_3-joins/catalog_joins_starter.sql; reference labs/module-07/l4_3-joins/catalog_joins_solution.sql. Four questions, each missing its join: an INNER JOIN through three tables, a LEFT JOIN with GROUP BY (Asimov's 0), an anti-join on the self-reference (Session 6 did it with NOT EXISTS), and a self-join with a computed column.

  • TODO 1 together (5m), TODOs 2 to 4 in pairs (13m). Expected: order 7 = 1984 and Animal Farm (Orwell), order 12 = Animal Farm and Fahrenheit 451; authors Orwell 2 / 8.49, Tolkien 2 / 10.99 ... Asimov 0 / NULL; Tom, Lena, Sofia, Nina, Ben manage nobody; Sofia was hired 5 years after Marcus, Tom 3 after Priya.
  • Circulate for: COUNT(*) in TODO 2 (Asimov gets 1: ask them to read his row), the anti-join testing e.id IS NULL instead of report.id IS NULL (nothing comes back), and a self-join without aliases (1066).
  • Debrief (2m): "TODO 3 answered a Session 6 question. Which version, NOT EXISTS or the anti-join, would you rather read next month?" Either is right.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-07/l4_3-joins/city_sales_starter.sql (revenue per city with the NULL group, DISTINCT after a fan-out, the WHERE-versus-ON trap on a new question), then labs/module-07/l4_3-joins/debug_joins.sql (1052, 1054, 1066 and one silent count).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is The Sales Report: sales_report.sql, six joined queries for the owner. In the room: Step 1 only (10 minutes).

  • Step 1: one row per order with the customer's name, the number of books and the total, grouped by the order. Twelve rows; order 1 totals 34.97.
  • Done in the room = Step 1 runs from the file with SOURCE.
  • Goes home: Steps 2 to 6 (revenue by month, best sellers, customers to welcome with the anti-join, sales by category, the staff directory with a self-join). The checklist's "the six monthly revenues add up to the sum of all order totals" is a good self-test: 237.29 both ways.
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Customers JOIN orders returns 12 rows; LEFT JOIN returns 14. Who are the two extra rows? Answer: Carol and Hank, with NULL order columns.
  • How do you find the customers who never ordered? Answer: LEFT JOIN orders, WHERE o.id IS NULL (or NOT EXISTS).
  • Why COUNT(b.id) and not COUNT(*) after a LEFT JOIN? Answer: COUNT(*) counts the NULL-padded row; COUNT(b.id) skips it.
  • Why does a self-join need aliases? Answer: the same table plays two roles; without aliases every column is ambiguous (1066).

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6 (4.3):

Answers: Q1 C) 14; Q2 A) FROM books b LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.book_id = b.id WHERE oi.id IS NULL; Q3 4 (one row per order line, not per order); Q4 B) ERROR 1052 (23000): Column 'id' in field list is ambiguous; Q5 Carol | 0 and Dave | 2; Q6 D) The WHERE runs after the join: for the padded rows b.in_stock is NULL, so the LEFT JOIN behaves like an inner join. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: the workbook's worked example (guests and RSVPs, four guests, three replies) shows JOIN, LEFT JOIN, the anti-join and COUNT(*) against COUNT(r.id) on data small enough to check by eye.

Stretch: city_sales_starter.sql and debug_joins.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (skip-level managers) and the Bonus Challenge (a full outer join with UNION).

Part 1 Assessment

Part 1 Assessment: The Riverside Cooking School

70 min
Timing
10mBrief and setup: the rules, the data, the hand-in; everyone SOURCEs the starter once and sees its placeholder output
20mPart A (suggested): build the enrollments table, load it, two refused statements, one UPDATE, one DELETE
35mPart B (suggested): eight queries on the data after Part A
5mFinal run: SOURCE the whole file, fix the first error, hand in
What to say at 1:25 (brief and setup)
  • "For the next hour you show what you can do with Part 1, alone. It is open book: the lesson pages, your notes, your workbook and your own scripts are all allowed. Other people and AI assistants are not. Nobody can finish everything perfectly in an hour; do what you can do well."
  • "Open labs/module-07/part1-assessment/part1_assessment_starter.sql in your editor. The file is the brief: read its first block with me." Walk through the header: the cooking school, the three given tables, Part A, Part B, the hand-in. Everyone runs it once: SOURCE labs/module-07/part1-assessment/part1_assessment_starter.sql. They should see the three tables built, TODO lines and no ERROR.
  • "Write every answer in the file, under its TODO. After each answer, save and SOURCE the file again: it rebuilds the school from scratch every time, so you cannot break anything. Part B runs on the data AFTER Part A: if you skip a Part A step, later numbers change, and that is fine; just keep going."
  • "You may answer with joins or with subqueries: either is fine if the result is right and the query reads clearly. Every query that returns several rows needs an ORDER BY."
  • "At 2:30 I say 'five minutes': SOURCE the whole file once more, fix the FIRST error if there is one, rename the file part1_assessment_<yourname>.sql and hand it in." Write the hand-in channel and 2:35 on the board.
During the assessment
  • Answer environment questions only: a client that will not start, Failed to open file, a forgotten password. For SQL questions: "Look at the error number, then the lesson page for it."
  • Time calls on the board: 1:55 "Part A should be done", 2:15 "halfway through Part B", 2:30 "five minutes: run the whole file, hand in".
  • Watch, do not grade yet: note learners who are stuck on A1 for more than 15 minutes; at 1:50 tell them privately to paste the A2 INSERT under a minimal table (two INT columns and a date) so Part B can run (they lose points on A1 only).
  • A learner finishes early: ask them to reread every ORDER BY and every typed number, then the stretch: "what would change in B8 if class prices changed later?" (a price_at_time column).
Scoring (after the session)

Score each file with the five-row rubric in final-assessment.html (Part 1 assessment section): table design and constraints; data changes and integrity; filtering, sorting and aggregates; subqueries and joins; script quality. Run each file first (SOURCE it, or python3 labs/check_labs.py --file <file> after adding a -- state: none line if it is missing); a file that stops with an error is scored on what it printed and the code you can read. The answer notes list the reference numbers and what each common wrong answer prints.

The reference output (instructor copy, never handed out before scoring):

+---------------+-------------+---------+
| name          | enrollments | revenue |
+---------------+-------------+---------+
| Marco Rossi   |           5 |  330.00 |
| Aiko Tanaka   |           4 |  260.00 |
| Lena Fischer  |           4 |  240.00 |
| Diego Morales |           2 |  100.00 |
| Nadia Haddad  |           0 |    0.00 |
+---------------+-------------+---------+

Above: B8, the hardest task (five instructors, Nadia Haddad with 0 enrollments and 0.00). The Answer Key prints the whole reference run.

2:35

Part 1 Review

15 min
How it runs

Files are in; scores come later. This is about how to think, not about marks. On the projector, the reference solution's approaches (not its numbers, if your program returns scored work before sharing answers):

  • A1 (3m): the composite primary key (PRIMARY KEY (student_id, class_id)) is the "only once" rule; the CHECK is the 1 to 5 rule; two named keys. Ask two learners for their delete rules and reasons: different choices with good reasons both score.
  • B2 and B4 (3m): WHERE before the piles, HAVING after; COUNT(rating) and AVG(rating) skip the blanks, COALESCE(rating, 0) would turn "not yet rated" into a zero star and drag the averages down.
  • B5 (1m): a typed 61.43 gives the right rows today and the wrong ones after the next price change: a subquery computes it fresh.
  • B6 to B8 (5m): one row of B6's join is one enrollment; B7 is the anti-join (or NOT EXISTS); B8 needs every instructor (LEFT JOIN from instructors) and a sum that ignores the padded rows.
  • The map of Part 1 (3m): the workbook's Part 1 map, one line per module: Module 1 server, client, database; Module 2 tables, types, constraints, CRUD; Module 3 filter, sort, summarize, nest; Module 4 relate, protect, join. "Every task in the assessment came from one of these lines."

The workbook's Part 1 review self-check (Q7 to Q12, one question per lesson group; homework or a Session 8 refresher):

Answers: Q7 A) DECIMAL(8, 2); Q8 ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry 'alice@example.com' for key 'customers.email'; Q9 B) 3; Q10 D) SELECT, FROM, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING, ORDER BY, LIMIT; Q11 Pride and Prejudice (one row); Q12 C) A junction table linking orders and books (many-to-many), carrying facts about each link: quantity and price_at_time. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, 4 minutes)

A reflective exit ticket after the assessment; collect it with the files. Question 2 tells you what to reteach first if Part 2 follows.

  1. In one sentence each, with the customers and orders tables: what does JOIN return, and what does LEFT JOIN add? Answer: JOIN returns one row per customer-order pair (12 rows, no Carol or Hank); LEFT JOIN also keeps every customer without an order, with NULLs (14 rows).
  2. Which assessment task was hardest for you, and what would you look up first? Answer: a reflection; group the answers by task (A1, B4, B7 and B8 are the usual ones).
  3. Name one thing from Part 1 you will use in a project or at work, and one you want to practice. Answer: a reflection.
Version A: Part 1 runs on its own (the course ends today)
  • Close the course (5m). Thank the room. Show the map of Part 1 once more: "Seven sessions ago you installed a server. Today you designed a table with keys and rules, changed data safely, and answered eight questions about a database you had never seen."
  • Completion: say how and when results come back (within a week is a good promise). A learner completes Part 1 when the assessment is met on the rubric in final-assessment.html (or met after one revision) and the sessions were attended or made up.
  • Next steps: Part 2 if your program offers it (CTEs and window functions, indexes and views, design and normalization, stored programs, transactions and JSON, users and backups, a final project); otherwise the course site's Lessons 5.1 to 8.3 are a self-study path in the same style. Practice suggestion: one real question a week about data they care about, answered in SQL.
  • Course evaluation before they leave (link or paper).
Version B: Part 2 follows (Session 8 is next)
  • Homework (about 2 to 3 hours): the sales report Steps 2 to 6; 4.3 Hands-on Exercises 1 to 3; the 4.3 Quick Quiz and journal; the workbook's Part 1 review self-check (Q7 to Q12); the 4.3 labs not finished.
  • Bring list for Session 8: a laptop with bookstore.sql loaded (reload it: today's labs change nothing, but the 4.3 lesson's "Before the Next Lesson" asks for a fresh copy); sales_report.sql, finished; the course folder with labs/; the workbook and the journal.
  • Bridge (1m): "Your sales report reads inside out in places, and the staff hierarchy needed one join per level. Session 8 fixes both: CTEs name the steps of a long query, and window functions rank and total without collapsing rows."
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish the lesson project: sales_report.sql Steps 2 to 6 (optional practice if Part 1 ends today).
  • Lesson exercises: 4.3 Exercises 1 to 3 (Exercise 4 is a stretch).
  • The 4.3 Quick Quiz on the lesson page and the 4.3 journal entry (the guest list and RSVPs).
  • Part 1 review: the workbook's self-check Q7 to Q12, then the matching lesson sections for any you missed.
  • Labs not finished in class: catalog_joins_starter.sql, city_sales_starter.sql, debug_joins.sql.
  • Before the Next Lesson (from 4.3, if Part 2 follows): run sales_report.sql from a fresh session; rewrite one anti-join with NOT EXISTS; reload bookstore.sql.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: the 60 assessment minutes and the 1:25 start; 4.3 demo Steps 1 to 4 and 12 to 14.
  • Cut first (saves up to 20m): the 4.3 guided lab. Learners take catalog_joins_starter.sql home; its expected output is in the file.
  • Cut second (saves 10m): the project start: Step 1 goes home with the rest.
  • Cut third (saves 7m): demo Steps 5 to 8 (books never sold, the cross join, the self-join, four tables): show the slides only.
  • If the assessment starts late anyway: keep the full 60 minutes and shorten the review to five minutes (A1 and B8 only).
Running short: what to add
  • Before the break: debug_joins.sql with the whole room (three errors and the silent fan-out).
  • After the assessment: let learners compare their B8 with a neighbor's and explain any difference in one sentence (the most instructive task to discuss).
  • For Version A: ten minutes of "ask me anything" about where SQL goes next (the jobs, the tools, the other databases).

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 8: CTEs and Window Functions

How to teach the first session of Part 2. Check that every machine has the full course database, refresh Part 1's joins and grouping in five questions, then turn inside-out queries into named steps, follow the bookstore's chain of managers with recursive CTEs, and put every row next to a total, a rank or its neighbor with window functions.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 5.1–5.2 Session 8 of 14 · Part 2

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm as Part 1: frame (one mental model) → live demo (you type, they type along) → guided lab (a kit lab, new practice) → start the lesson's own project. Exercises, quizzes, journals and the rest of each project go home.
  • Every SQL block below was run to produce the output printed under it, on a freshly loaded bookstore on MySQL 9.7 with the default settings. Ask for a prediction before every statement that prints something; read error numbers aloud.
  • Keep lesson_ctes.html and lesson_window_functions.html open for the round-by-round diagrams and the version notes. Labs live in labs/module-08/: _starter.sql files are handouts, _solution.sql files are yours.
  • Session 8 opens Part 2. The warm-up is 20 minutes instead of 10: an environment check that requires the full bookstore, a five-question Part 1 refresher, and the map of Part 2. Both lessons start from a freshly loaded bookstore.sql and only read, so nobody needs to reload between the two lessons.
  • Teal blocks are hands-on, amber blocks are watch-outs. Lesson codes (5.1, 5.2) match the course site.
Starting with Part 2? (programs that skip Part 1)

Part 2 can be sold and run on its own for learners who already write SQL. It assumes everything Part 1 teaches: a working MySQL 9.7 (or 8.4) and the mysql client, SOURCE, CREATE TABLE with keys and constraints, CRUD, WHERE/ORDER BY/LIMIT, GROUP BY with HAVING, subqueries and derived tables, foreign keys, and all four joins including self-joins. Before: send Part 1's setup guide (Lesson 1.2's install section for their platform), the course download bookstore.sql, and two files from this session: labs/module-08/l5_0-part2-start/check_environment.sh (or .ps1) and labs/module-08/l5_0-part2-start/refresher_starter.sql. Ask for the check's verdict and the refresher's four answers by email. In the room: the warm-up below runs unchanged; learners who struggled with the refresher sit with someone who did not. Gaps to expect: self-joins and LEFT JOIN counting (W2 to W4); give those learners Lesson 4.3 as reading before Session 9. The Session 1 environment check is not needed: this session's version checks the same things and also requires the bookstore.

For a cohort coming straight from Part 1 (Session 7), the note does not apply: they ran the Part 1 assessment last time, and the refresher doubles as a quick look at what stuck.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Files to hand out: labs/module-08/ without the *_solution.sql files; the workbook. Learners keep labs/ in their course folder, next to bookstore.sql, and open their terminal there.
  • ☐ Run the kit check once: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-08 from this kit's folder (13 files, a few seconds, all PASS). Run the environment check on the projector machine so you can show READY for Part 2.
  • ☐ Projector machine: MySQL 9.7 with a freshly loaded bookstore (SOURCE bookstore.sql), the mysql> client at 18 pt or larger, and the course folder as the current folder. Browser tabs: lesson_ctes.html, lesson_window_functions.html.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: the Part 2 map (Sessions 8 to 14: queries → design → programs → operations → final project); a three-box "recipe" (customer_totals → average → keep above); and two columns headed "GROUP BY: one row per group" and "OVER: every row, plus the group's number".
  • ☐ Print or share: the workbook (its Start Here page has the Part 2 check and the refresher), sticky notes for the exit ticket.
Known traps today
SymptomCauseFix
ERROR 1146 ... Table 'bookstore.org' doesn't existWITH without RECURSIVE (or a CTE used in the next statement)WITH RECURSIVE; repeat the WITH in each statement that needs it.
ERROR 3636 ... Recursive query aborted after 1001 iterationsNo stop condition, or one that never becomes falseAdd or fix the WHERE in the recursive member. Do not raise the limit to "fix" it.
ERROR 1406 ... Data too long for column 'path'The anchor fixed a narrow column (a literal like 'Maria')Widen it in the anchor: CAST(... AS CHAR(200)).
ERROR 3593 ... cannot use the window functionA window function in WHERE, HAVING or GROUP BYCompute it in a CTE, filter in the outer query.
ERROR 1064 ... near 'rankrank, row_number, groups, window are reserved wordsName columns rank_num, pos; or use backticks.
A learner on MariaDB sees no error for a runaway recursionMariaDB stops at max_recursive_iterations with a warning, not an errorSHOW WARNINGS; and always write the stop condition. LAG(x, 2, 0) is a 1064 there: wrap in COALESCE.
The check says NOT READY ... bookstore has 9 tablesA leftover table from Part 1 work (or a lesson in progress)SOURCE bookstore.sql rebuilds the database from scratch.
