In the room. The lesson pages estimate 100 + 100 = 200 minutes for a solo learner; after the 20-minute Part 2 opening, the break, the debug clinic and the wrap-up, the room has 135. Lesson 5.1 gets 55 minutes and 5.2 gets 70: window functions have more moving parts (partition, order, frame), and recursion is easier to finish at home once the rounds are clear.

Goes home (about 2 to 3 hours). org_report.sql Steps 2, 4 and 5; sales_dashboard.sql Steps 3 to 5; each lesson's Exercises 1 to 3 (4 is a stretch); both Quick Quizzes; both journal entries; the lab files not finished in class (category_steps_starter.sql, order_trends_starter.sql).

Protect, in this order: the environment check (Part 2 needs the full bookstore every session); 5.1 demo steps 6 to 8 (recursion in rounds: the idea people cannot get from reading alone); the 5.2 ranking step with a prediction; top N per group with the CTE pattern; the staff-tree lab through TODO 2. Cut first: the debug clinic (hand out both debug files as homework), then the 5.1 project start (send Steps 1 to 5 home), then 5.2 demo step 9 (the RANGE trap is in the self-check).

Opening Segment

0:00

Welcome to Part 2: Environment Check and Part 1 Refresher

20 min
Timing
5mReload bookstore.sql; run the Part 2 environment check on every machine
12mPart 1 refresher: five retrieval questions (W1 to W5), answered from memory, then shown
3mThe map of Part 2 and today's two lessons
Sequence and talking points
  • Environment check (5m). Say: "Part 2 starts from the full course database, every session." On the projector, then theirs: SOURCE bookstore.sql (it prints the eight row counts), then SOURCE labs/module-08/l5_0-part2-start/check_environment.sql. Done = READY for Part 2 (READY WITH NOTES on 8.4 or MariaDB). The check fails on purpose when bookstore is missing or has a ninth table left from Part 1 work: reload and rerun. Machines that are not ready pair up; you fix them during the 5.1 lab, not now. The .sh / .ps1 helpers in the same folder also check the client version.
  • Refresher (12m). Say: "Five questions from Part 1, no notes. Today's lessons stand on joins, grouping and derived tables." Learners write answers to W1 to W5 on the workbook's Start Here page (5 minutes), then you take them one by one: a volunteer answers, you show the query or the fix. Tally misses on a sticky note: W3 and W4 (counting after joins) predict trouble with today's CTEs that total orders.
  • Part 2 map (3m). Point at the board: "Sessions 8 to 14: better queries today (CTEs and window functions), then design for speed and safety (indexes, views, normalization), programs inside the database (procedures, transactions, JSON), operations (users, backups), and your own database as the final project." Today: "5.1 names the steps of a long query and follows chains of rows; 5.2 puts each row next to a total, a rank or its neighbor."
Refresher answers (W1 to W5)
  1. W1 Which customers have never placed an order? Say the query shape, then the names. Answer: A LEFT JOIN from customers to orders, keeping the customers whose orders side is NULL (or HAVING COUNT(o.id) = 0): Carol and Hank. Lesson 4.3, the anti-join.
  2. W2 employees has a manager_id. How do you show each employee next to their manager's name? Who is Sofia's manager? Answer: A self-join: the table twice with two aliases, ON m.id = e.manager_id. Sofia (id 7) reports to Marcus.
  3. W3 SELECT a.name, COUNT(*) FROM authors a LEFT JOIN books b ON b.author_id = a.id GROUP BY a.id, a.name: what does it say for Isaac Asimov, who has no books, and how do you fix it? Answer: 1, because COUNT(*) counts the NULL-padded row the LEFT JOIN made. Count a column of the right table, COUNT(b.id), which skips NULL: 0. (Lesson 4.3, Common Mistake 4.)
  4. W4 Books per author and copies sold in one query: Orwell shows 5 books, but he wrote 2. What happened? Answer: Fan-out: joining order_items repeats each book once per order line. COUNT(DISTINCT b.id) fixes it. Ask what one row of the join stands for. (Lesson 4.3, Common Mistake 5.)
  5. W5 A subquery in FROM (a derived table) fails with error 1248. What is missing? Answer: An alias: every derived table needs its own name, ( ... ) AS t (Lesson 3.4). Today's CTEs give such steps a name up front, and read top to bottom.

The key shows W1 and W2's real output. labs/module-08/l5_0-part2-start/refresher_starter.sql practices the same four moves (an inner join, LEFT JOIN with COUNT, a derived table, a self-join): hand it to anyone who missed two or more.

Watch-outs
  • Do not reteach Part 1. Five minutes of answers, not a lecture: Lesson 4.3 and the refresher lab are the place for gaps.
  • A machine with no bookstore. SOURCE needs the path to bookstore.sql; Failed to open file means the client was started in another folder (exit, cd, log in again), or give the full path with forward slashes on Windows.
  • New learners in a Part 2-only program. Pair them with each other only if both passed the refresher; otherwise with someone from the strongest pile.

Lesson Plans

5.1

CTEs & Recursive Queries

55 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Rewrite a nested subquery as a WITH query and name each step
  • Chain several CTEs in one statement, letting later steps read earlier ones
  • Choose between a CTE, a derived table and a view for a given job
  • Write a recursive CTE with an anchor, a recursive member and a stop condition, and generate number and month series with it
  • Walk the employee hierarchy down (an org chart) and up (a chain of command)
  • Read and fix errors 1146, 3636 and 1406 from broken CTEs
Timing in the room
10mFrame: one question, three steps; WITH names a step; recursion in rounds
20mLive demo: nested query → CTE; two CTEs; self-joined CTE; series, calendar, org chart, chain of command; 1146 and 3636
15mGuided lab: staff_tree_starter.sql in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: org_report.sql Steps 1 and 3
Mental model to say

Point at the three boxes on the board: "A real question takes steps. Total each customer's orders. Average those totals. Keep the customers above it. Until now you nested the steps inside each other and read them inside out. A CTE writes each step once, gives it a name, and lists the steps top to bottom, like a recipe. The name lives for one statement."

Then recursion, with the number series drawn as rounds: "A recursive CTE has an anchor that runs once and makes the starting rows, and a recursive member that runs again and again, each time reading only the rows the last round made. When a round makes no rows, it stops: that is the stop condition. It is how you follow a chain whose length you do not know: managers above managers, months until July."

The two directions of a hierarchy, as two arrows on the board: down = e.manager_id = cte.id ("whose manager is this?"), up = e.id = cte.manager_id ("who is this person's manager?").

Live demo (you type, they follow)

The lesson's own examples, typed live on the freshly loaded bookstore. Learners type along on their own machines; the long queries can be pasted from the lesson page by anyone who falls behind.

  1. The question, inside out (2m). "Which customers spent more than the average customer? Three steps: total each customer, average the totals, keep the ones above. With Lesson 3.4's derived tables it looks like this." Scroll it slowly; ask where to start reading (the middle) and point at the total-per-customer step written twice. 
    SELECT t.customer, t.total
FROM (
    SELECT c.first_name AS customer,
           SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time) AS total
    FROM customers c
    JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
    JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
    GROUP BY c.id, c.first_name
) AS t
WHERE t.total > (
    SELECT AVG(t2.total)
    FROM (
        SELECT SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time) AS total
        FROM orders o
        JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
        GROUP BY o.customer_id
    ) AS t2
)
ORDER BY t.total DESC;
    +----------+-------+
| customer | total |
+----------+-------+
| Alice    | 58.95 |
| Dave     | 45.96 |
| Frank    | 41.96 |
+----------+-------+
  2. The same question, top to bottom (3m). "Name the step once, up front, with WITH. Now it reads like a recipe: here is customer_totals; show the ones above its average." Same three rows: Alice, Dave, Frank. 
    WITH customer_totals AS (
    SELECT c.first_name AS customer,
           SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time) AS total
    FROM customers c
    JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
    JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
    GROUP BY c.id, c.first_name
)
SELECT customer, total
FROM customer_totals
WHERE total > (SELECT AVG(total) FROM customer_totals)
ORDER BY total DESC;
    +----------+-------+
| customer | total |
+----------+-------+
| Alice    | 58.95 |
| Dave     | 45.96 |
| Frank    | 41.96 |
+----------+-------+
    Average of the six customers who ordered: about 39.55 (Carol and Hank never ordered, so the inner joins leave them out).
  3. Two steps, one WITH (2m). "One WITH, several CTEs, separated by commas. The second reads the first. average has one row, so CROSS JOIN attaches it to every customer." 
    WITH customer_totals AS (
    SELECT o.customer_id,
           SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time) AS total
    FROM orders o
    JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
    GROUP BY o.customer_id
),
average AS (
    SELECT AVG(total) AS avg_total
    FROM customer_totals
)
SELECT c.first_name, c.last_name, t.total,
       ROUND(t.total - a.avg_total, 2) AS above_average
FROM customer_totals t
JOIN customers c ON c.id = t.customer_id
CROSS JOIN average a
WHERE t.total > a.avg_total
ORDER BY t.total DESC;
    +------------+-----------+-------+---------------+
| first_name | last_name | total | above_average |
+------------+-----------+-------+---------------+
| Alice      | Johnson   | 58.95 |         19.40 |
| Dave       | Brown     | 45.96 |          6.41 |
| Frank      | Miller    | 41.96 |          2.41 |
+------------+-----------+-------+---------------+
  4. A CTE joined to itself (2m). "A CTE is read like a table, so it can be read twice, under two aliases: this month next to last month." Point at January's NULLs: the LEFT JOIN keeps a month with no previous month. Say: "after the break, LAG does this in one line". 
    WITH monthly AS (
    SELECT MONTH(o.order_date)                 AS month_num,
           SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time) AS revenue
    FROM orders o
    JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
    GROUP BY MONTH(o.order_date)
)
SELECT cur.month_num,
       cur.revenue,
       prev.revenue                AS previous_month,
       cur.revenue - prev.revenue  AS change_amount
FROM monthly cur
LEFT JOIN monthly prev ON prev.month_num = cur.month_num - 1
ORDER BY cur.month_num;
    +-----------+---------+----------------+---------------+
| month_num | revenue | previous_month | change_amount |
+-----------+---------+----------------+---------------+
|         1 |   61.95 |           NULL |          NULL |
|         2 |   51.95 |          61.95 |        -10.00 |
|         3 |   33.47 |          51.95 |        -18.48 |
|         4 |   38.97 |          33.47 |          5.50 |
|         5 |   31.97 |          38.97 |         -7.00 |
|         6 |   18.98 |          31.97 |        -12.99 |
+-----------+---------+----------------+---------------+
  5. Gone after the semicolon (1m). "The CTE belongs to its statement. The next statement cannot see it." 
    WITH monthly AS (
    SELECT MONTH(order_date) AS month_num, COUNT(*) AS orders
    FROM orders
    GROUP BY MONTH(order_date)
)
SELECT MAX(orders) AS busiest FROM monthly;
SELECT * FROM monthly;
    +---------+
| busiest |
+---------+
|       3 |
+---------+
ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'bookstore.monthly' doesn't exist
    Fix: repeat the WITH, or, if many scripts need it, make it a view (Session 9).
  6. Recursion in rounds (3m). Draw the rounds on the board as you type: anchor 1; round 1 reads 1, makes 2; ... round 5 reads 5, the stop condition fails, nothing comes out, done. "Each round sees only the rows the previous round made." 
    WITH RECURSIVE numbers AS (
    SELECT 1 AS n            -- anchor: the first row
    UNION ALL
    SELECT n + 1             -- recursive member: next row from the last one
    FROM numbers
    WHERE n < 5              -- stop condition
)
SELECT n FROM numbers;
    +------+
| n    |
+------+
|    1 |
|    2 |
|    3 |
|    4 |
|    5 |
+------+
  7. A calendar that fills the gaps (2m). "Nobody ordered in July, so GROUP BY over orders cannot show July. Generate the months first, then hang the orders on them with a LEFT JOIN." 
    WITH RECURSIVE months AS (
    SELECT DATE('2026-01-01') AS month_start
    UNION ALL
    SELECT month_start + INTERVAL 1 MONTH
    FROM months
    WHERE month_start < '2026-07-01'
)
SELECT DATE_FORMAT(m.month_start, '%Y-%m') AS month,
       COUNT(o.id)                         AS orders
FROM months m
LEFT JOIN orders o
       ON o.order_date >= m.month_start
      AND o.order_date <  m.month_start + INTERVAL 1 MONTH
GROUP BY m.month_start
ORDER BY m.month_start;
    +---------+--------+
| month   | orders |
+---------+--------+
| 2026-01 |      2 |
| 2026-02 |      3 |
| 2026-03 |      2 |
| 2026-04 |      2 |
| 2026-05 |      2 |
| 2026-06 |      1 |
| 2026-07 |      0 |
+---------+--------+
    COUNT(o.id) counts matched orders only, so July is 0. The date range in ON is the safe way to say "in this month".
  8. The org chart, top down (3m). "Anchor: the person with no manager. Each round: the people whose manager is someone from the round before. The data itself is the stop condition." Point at the CAST in the anchor: the anchor fixes every column's width. 
    WITH RECURSIVE org AS (
    SELECT id, first_name, last_name, department, 1 AS level,
           CAST(first_name AS CHAR(200)) AS path
    FROM employees
    WHERE manager_id IS NULL
    UNION ALL
    SELECT e.id, e.first_name, e.last_name, e.department, o.level + 1,
           CONCAT(o.path, ' > ', e.first_name)
    FROM employees e
    JOIN org o ON e.manager_id = o.id
)
SELECT CONCAT(REPEAT('    ', level - 1), first_name, ' ', last_name) AS org_chart,
       department
FROM org
ORDER BY path;
    +---------------------------+------------+
| org_chart                 | department |
+---------------------------+------------+
| Maria Santos              | management |
|     Daniel Reyes          | management |
|         Marcus Hill       | inventory  |
|             Sofia Alvarez | inventory  |
|         Priya Nair        | sales      |
|             Ben Carter    | sales      |
|             Lena Park     | sales      |
|             Tom Becker    | sales      |
|     James Okafor          | support    |
|         Nina Kowalski     | support    |
+---------------------------+------------+
  9. A chain of command, bottom up (1m). "Swap the join and it climbs: who is this person's manager?" 
    WITH RECURSIVE chain AS (
    SELECT id, first_name, last_name, manager_id, 0 AS steps_up
    FROM employees
    WHERE id = 7
    UNION ALL
    SELECT e.id, e.first_name, e.last_name, e.manager_id, c.steps_up + 1
    FROM employees e
    JOIN chain c ON e.id = c.manager_id
)
SELECT steps_up, CONCAT(first_name, ' ', last_name) AS person
FROM chain
ORDER BY steps_up;
    +----------+---------------+
| steps_up | person        |
+----------+---------------+
|        0 | Sofia Alvarez |
|        1 | Marcus Hill   |
|        2 | Daniel Reyes  |
|        3 | Maria Santos  |
+----------+---------------+
  10. Two errors on purpose (1m). "First I forget RECURSIVE, then I forget the stop condition." Read both numbers aloud. 
    WITH org AS (
    SELECT id, 1 AS level FROM employees WHERE manager_id IS NULL
    UNION ALL
    SELECT e.id, o.level + 1 FROM employees e JOIN org o ON e.manager_id = o.id
)
SELECT * FROM org;
WITH RECURSIVE numbers AS (
    SELECT 1 AS n
    UNION ALL
    SELECT n + 1 FROM numbers
)
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM numbers;
    ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'bookstore.org' doesn't exist
ERROR 3636 (HY000): Recursive query aborted after 1001 iterations. Try increasing @@cte_max_recursion_depth to a larger value.
    Raising cte_max_recursion_depth is the fix only for a series that really needs more than 1000 rounds; here the fix is a WHERE in the recursive member.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Expecting a CTE to persist. "I made monthly and now it is gone." Coach: "Which statement was it written in? A CTE is part of one statement; for something everyone reuses, that is a view (Session 9)."
  • Reading the stop condition as a row filter. Learners expect WHERE n < 5 to stop before 5. Coach with the rounds on the board: "Which row does the round read? 4 passes and makes 5; 5 fails and makes nothing." (Self-check Q2.)
  • An anchor that starts everywhere. No WHERE in the anchor: 30 rows for 10 employees, no error. Coach: "How many starting points did you give it? Compare COUNT(*) with COUNT(DISTINCT id)." (Self-check Q5, the lesson's Common Mistake 5.)
Guided lab

File: learners open labs/module-08/l5_1-ctes/staff_tree_starter.sql; your reference is labs/module-08/l5_1-ctes/staff_tree_solution.sql. Recursion on new questions: the twelve months of the year with zero months, then every employee's division (the level-2 manager they work under), carried down the tree.

  • SOURCE it as shipped (2m): SOURCE labs/module-08/l5_1-ctes/staff_tree_starter.sql. Everyone should see one month, a tree where every division says (owner), and a TODO line.
  • TODO 1 together (3m): the stop condition. Ask "which round makes month 12, and which round stops?" before anyone edits.
  • TODO 2 in pairs (7m): each round copies the division from the manager's row, but the owner has none, so nobody ever gets one. The fix is COALESCE(o.division, e.first_name): level-2 people start a division, everyone below inherits it. Circulate for edits to the anchor instead of the recursive member.
  • Expected: TODO 1 twelve rows, 5, 5, 3, 3, 3, 2, then six zeros; TODO 2 Daniel's division of 7 (Daniel, Marcus, Priya, Ben, Lena, Sofia, Tom) and James's of 2; TODO 3 (home or fast finishers) Daniel 7 people 310500.00, James 2 people 88500.00.
  • Debrief (1m): "The anchor decided where to start and how wide the division column is; the recursive member decided how to get from one round to the next. Nothing in the query says how deep the company is."
  • Fast finishers: TODO 3, then labs/module-08/l5_1-ctes/category_steps_starter.sql (non-recursive: two CTEs, and one CTE read three times).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is The Staff and Sales Briefing, org_report.sql: four commented queries in one script. In the room: Steps 1 and 3 (10 minutes), the two you just saw in the demo.

  • Step 1: create org_report.sql in the course folder with USE bookstore; and the indented org chart, with the level as a number.
  • Step 3: the chain of command for Nina (id 9); change the id to 7 and see Sofia's chain from the demo.
  • Done in the room = the file runs with SOURCE and prints the chart (10 people, 4 levels) and one chain.
  • Goes home: Step 2 (team size and payroll for every manager: an anchor that starts everywhere, on purpose), Step 4 (the sales calendar: a recursive CTE plus two ordinary ones), Step 5 (run the whole file).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Can the main statement read a CTE twice? Answer: yes: the demo's average step reads customer_totals, and the month comparison joins monthly to itself.
  • Which part of a recursive CTE runs exactly once? Answer: the anchor.
  • What stops the org chart's recursion? Answer: the data: in the last round nobody has Tom, Lena, Ben or Sofia as a manager, the join finds nothing, no rows come out.
  • When would you raise cte_max_recursion_depth? Answer: only for a series that really needs more than 1000 rounds, for your session only, and back to DEFAULT afterward; never to silence a missing stop condition.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6.

Answers: Q1 B) Only while that one statement runs; Q2 2,4,8,16,32 (one row, column series); Q3 A) ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'bookstore.org' doesn't exist; Q4 C) JOIN chain c ON e.id = c.manager_id; Q5 D) The anchor has no WHERE, so every employee starts a walk of their own; Q6 GROUP BY over orders can only show months that have orders; the recursive calendar has every month, and the LEFT JOIN keeps the empty ones (July shows 0). Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: draw each round as a row on paper before running anything; for the lab, TODO 1 only, then read the solution's TODO 2 line by line with a partner. For learners shaky on joins (W2 to W4 missed), the refresher lab first.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (the shape of the company) and the Bonus Challenge (a cycle on purpose inside a transaction, then a FIND_IN_SET guard). Ask them what MariaDB would do with the same runaway query.

5.2

Window Functions

70 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain how OVER() differs from GROUP BY, and split a window with PARTITION BY
  • Number and rank rows with ROW_NUMBER, RANK and DENSE_RANK, and pick the right one when there are ties
  • Build running totals and moving averages, and control them with a frame (ROWS BETWEEN ...)
  • Compare each row with the one before or after it using LAG and LEAD
  • Return the top N rows per group by filtering a window result in a CTE, and explain why WHERE cannot do it (error 3593)
Timing in the room
10mFrame: GROUP BY squashes, OVER keeps; partition, order, frame; windows run late
25mLive demo: OVER (), PARTITION BY, three ranks, running total and frame, LAG/LEAD, top N per group, 3593, 1064, the RANGE trap
20mGuided lab: leaderboards_starter.sql
15mStart the lesson project: sales_dashboard.sql Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

The two columns on the board: "GROUP BY answers 'what is the total?' by squashing each group into one row. A window function computes the same kind of number across a set of rows, the window, and hands it back to every row. Eleven books in, eleven books out, each next to the average."

Then the three parts of OVER, written one under the other: PARTITION BY (one window per group), ORDER BY (an order inside each window: needed for ranks, running totals, LAG), the frame (which neighbors count: ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW).

Last, the clause order as a vertical list: FROM/JOIN → WHERE → GROUP BY/HAVING → window functions → SELECT list → ORDER BY/LIMIT. "Windows are computed late. That is why WHERE cannot filter on them, and why the fix is one more step: a CTE."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

The lesson's own examples on the freshly loaded bookstore. Predictions first on steps 3 and 9.

  1. One number, or every row (2m). "AVG gives one number and the eleven books are gone. Add OVER (), and it hands the same average back to every row." 
    SELECT ROUND(AVG(price), 2) AS avg_price FROM books;
SELECT title,
       price,
       ROUND(AVG(price) OVER (), 2)         AS avg_price,
       ROUND(price - AVG(price) OVER (), 2) AS vs_average
FROM books
ORDER BY price DESC, title;
    +-----------+
| avg_price |
+-----------+
|     10.76 |
+-----------+
+------------------------+-------+-----------+------------+
| title                  | price | avg_price | vs_average |
+------------------------+-------+-----------+------------+
| Lord of the Rings      | 15.99 |     10.76 |       5.23 |
| Dune                   | 14.99 |     10.76 |       4.23 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird  | 11.99 |     10.76 |       1.23 |
| The Hobbit             | 10.99 |     10.76 |       0.23 |
| Fahrenheit 451         | 10.49 |     10.76 |      -0.27 |
| 1984                   |  9.99 |     10.76 |      -0.77 |
| Brave New World        |  9.49 |     10.76 |      -1.27 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |  8.99 |     10.76 |      -1.77 |
| The Great Gatsby       |  8.99 |     10.76 |      -1.77 |
| Animal Farm            |  8.49 |     10.76 |      -2.27 |
| Pride and Prejudice    |  7.99 |     10.76 |      -2.77 |
+------------------------+-------+-----------+------------+
  2. A window per department (3m). "PARTITION BY splits like GROUP BY, without collapsing. Every sales row sees the sales average." Ask why sales comes first: department is an ENUM, sorted in declared order. 
    SELECT first_name,
       department,
       salary,
       ROUND(AVG(salary) OVER (PARTITION BY department), 2) AS dept_avg,
       COUNT(*) OVER (PARTITION BY department)              AS dept_size
FROM employees
ORDER BY department, salary DESC;
    +------------+------------+----------+----------+-----------+
| first_name | department | salary   | dept_avg | dept_size |
+------------+------------+----------+----------+-----------+
| Priya      | sales      | 46000.00 | 39625.00 |         4 |
| Lena       | sales      | 39500.00 | 39625.00 |         4 |
| Tom        | sales      | 38000.00 | 39625.00 |         4 |
| Ben        | sales      | 35000.00 | 39625.00 |         4 |
| Marcus     | inventory  | 47000.00 | 42000.00 |         2 |
| Sofia      | inventory  | 37000.00 | 42000.00 |         2 |
| Maria      | management | 92000.00 | 80000.00 |         2 |
| Daniel     | management | 68000.00 | 80000.00 |         2 |
| James      | support    | 52000.00 | 44250.00 |         2 |
| Nina       | support    | 36500.00 | 44250.00 |         2 |
+------------+------------+----------+----------+-----------+
  3. Three ways to number (4m). Predict first: "Dune and The Hobbit both sold 3. What does each function give them, and the next book?" Then run. Write 1, 2, 3, 4 / 1, 2, 2, 4 / 1, 2, 2, 3 on the board. 
    WITH copies AS (
    SELECT b.title, SUM(oi.quantity) AS sold
    FROM books b
    JOIN order_items oi ON oi.book_id = b.id
    GROUP BY b.id, b.title
)
SELECT title,
       sold,
       ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY sold DESC, title) AS row_num,
       RANK()       OVER (ORDER BY sold DESC)        AS rank_num,
       DENSE_RANK() OVER (ORDER BY sold DESC)        AS dense_num
FROM copies
ORDER BY sold DESC, title;
    +-----------------------+------+---------+----------+-----------+
| title                 | sold | row_num | rank_num | dense_num |
+-----------------------+------+---------+----------+-----------+
| 1984                  |    4 |       1 |        1 |         1 |
| Dune                  |    3 |       2 |        2 |         2 |
| The Hobbit            |    3 |       3 |        2 |         2 |
| Animal Farm           |    2 |       4 |        4 |         3 |
| Fahrenheit 451        |    2 |       5 |        4 |         3 |
| Lord of the Rings     |    2 |       6 |        4 |         3 |
| Pride and Prejudice   |    2 |       7 |        4 |         3 |
| To Kill a Mockingbird |    2 |       8 |        4 |         3 |
| Brave New World       |    1 |       9 |        9 |         4 |
+-----------------------+------+---------+----------+-----------+
    The aliases end in _num because rank is a reserved word (step 9).
  4. Running total and moving average (4m). "ORDER BY inside OVER turns SUM into a running total. A frame, ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW, makes a three-month moving average." January's average is January alone. 
    WITH monthly AS (
    SELECT MONTH(o.order_date)                 AS month_num,
           SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time) AS revenue
    FROM orders o
    JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
    GROUP BY MONTH(o.order_date)
)
SELECT month_num,
       revenue,
       SUM(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY month_num) AS running_total,
       ROUND(AVG(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY month_num
                                ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW), 2) AS moving_avg_3
FROM monthly
ORDER BY month_num;
    +-----------+---------+---------------+--------------+
| month_num | revenue | running_total | moving_avg_3 |
+-----------+---------+---------------+--------------+
|         1 |   61.95 |         61.95 |        61.95 |
|         2 |   51.95 |        113.90 |        56.95 |
|         3 |   33.47 |        147.37 |        49.12 |
|         4 |   38.97 |        186.34 |        41.46 |
|         5 |   31.97 |        218.31 |        34.80 |
|         6 |   18.98 |        237.29 |        29.97 |
+-----------+---------+---------------+--------------+
  5. LAG and LEAD (3m). "Before the break this took a self-join. LAG reads the previous row, LEAD the next." Compare the change column with the demo's step 4: identical. 
    WITH monthly AS (
    SELECT MONTH(o.order_date)                 AS month_num,
           SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time) AS revenue
    FROM orders o
    JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
    GROUP BY MONTH(o.order_date)
)
SELECT month_num,
       revenue,
       LAG(revenue)  OVER (ORDER BY month_num)           AS prev_month,
       revenue - LAG(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY month_num)  AS change_amount,
       LEAD(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY month_num)           AS next_month
FROM monthly
ORDER BY month_num;
    +-----------+---------+------------+---------------+------------+
| month_num | revenue | prev_month | change_amount | next_month |
+-----------+---------+------------+---------------+------------+
|         1 |   61.95 |       NULL |          NULL |      51.95 |
|         2 |   51.95 |      61.95 |        -10.00 |      33.47 |
|         3 |   33.47 |      51.95 |        -18.48 |      38.97 |
|         4 |   38.97 |      33.47 |          5.50 |      31.97 |
|         5 |   31.97 |      38.97 |         -7.00 |      18.98 |
|         6 |   18.98 |      31.97 |        -12.99 |       NULL |
+-----------+---------+------------+---------------+------------+
  6. Top 1 per department (3m). "Number the rows inside each department in a CTE, keep number 1 outside." 
    WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT first_name,
           last_name,
           department,
           salary,
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY department ORDER BY salary DESC) AS pos
    FROM employees
)
SELECT department, first_name, last_name, salary
FROM ranked
WHERE pos = 1
ORDER BY salary DESC;
    +------------+------------+-----------+----------+
| department | first_name | last_name | salary   |
+------------+------------+-----------+----------+
| management | Maria      | Santos    | 92000.00 |
| support    | James      | Okafor    | 52000.00 |
| inventory  | Marcus     | Hill      | 47000.00 |
| sales      | Priya      | Nair      | 46000.00 |
+------------+------------+-----------+----------+
  7. Why not WHERE? (2m). "Windows are computed after WHERE, GROUP BY and HAVING. WHERE cannot see them." Point at the stray quote at the end of the message: MySQL's typo, not ours. 
    SELECT first_name, department, salary
FROM employees
WHERE ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY department ORDER BY salary DESC) = 1;
    ERROR 3593 (HY000): You cannot use the window function 'row_number' in this context.'
  8. A reserved word (1m). "Old tutorials name the column rank." 
    SELECT title, price,
       RANK() OVER (ORDER BY price DESC) AS rank
FROM books;
    ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'rank
FROM books' at line 2
    Fix: rank_num, or backticks. MariaDB still accepts AS rank, which is why the habit spreads.
  9. A running total that jumps (silent) (3m). "No error, but look at the two 8.99 books: same running total. The default frame is RANGE, which adds tied rows in one step. Say ROWS and make the order unique." 
    SELECT title, price,
       SUM(price) OVER (ORDER BY price) AS running_total
FROM books
ORDER BY price, title
LIMIT 5;
SELECT title, price,
       SUM(price) OVER (ORDER BY price, title
                        ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS running_total
FROM books
ORDER BY price, title
LIMIT 5;
    +------------------------+-------+---------------+
| title                  | price | running_total |
+------------------------+-------+---------------+
| Pride and Prejudice    |  7.99 |          7.99 |
| Animal Farm            |  8.49 |         16.48 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |  8.99 |         34.46 |
| The Great Gatsby       |  8.99 |         34.46 |
| Brave New World        |  9.49 |         43.95 |
+------------------------+-------+---------------+
+------------------------+-------+---------------+
| title                  | price | running_total |
+------------------------+-------+---------------+
| Pride and Prejudice    |  7.99 |          7.99 |
| Animal Farm            |  8.49 |         16.48 |
| The Catcher in the Rye |  8.99 |         25.47 |
| The Great Gatsby       |  8.99 |         34.46 |
| Brave New World        |  9.49 |         43.95 |
+------------------------+-------+---------------+
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Filtering a window in WHERE (3593, or 1054 with the alias). Coach with the clause list on the board: "Where in this list does WHERE run? Where do windows run?" Then the two-step CTE pattern.
  • Forgetting PARTITION BY. "Top 2 per category" returns two books in total. Coach: "Where does the numbering restart? Nowhere: tell it to restart per category." (The lab's TODO 2 is built on this.)
  • Trusting a running total with ties. No error, two rows with the same total. Coach: "Which frame did you get by default? RANGE. Say ROWS, and make the order unique."
Guided lab

File: labs/module-08/l5_2-window-functions/leaderboards_starter.sql; reference labs/module-08/l5_2-window-functions/leaderboards_solution.sql. New leaderboards on the bookstore: customers by copies bought (the data has ties), the two cheapest books per category, and each salary's share of its department's payroll.

  • TODO 1 with a prediction (6m): before adding RANK and DENSE_RANK, every pair writes what Dave and Frank (both 4 copies) and Bob and Grace (both 3) will get from each function.
  • Expected TODO 1: Dave 4 2 2 2 and Frank 4 3 2 2 (copies, row, rank, dense); Eve last with rank 6 and dense rank 4.
  • TODO 2 (7m): without PARTITION BY only two books come back in total; with it, two per category (ten rows). Ask why Pride and Prejudice appears twice (it is in Classic and Fiction).
  • TODO 3 (5m): OVER () gives the company payroll on every row; PARTITION BY department gives sales 158500.00 and inventory 84000.00, then the percentage.
  • Debrief (2m): "One query, three kinds of window: an order (ranks), a partition plus an order (top N per group), a partition alone (a group total on every row)."
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-08/l5_2-window-functions/order_trends_starter.sql (a running total over orders, LAG per customer, every customer's first order).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is The Sales Dashboard, sales_dashboard.sql. In the room: Steps 1 and 2 (15 minutes).

  • Step 1: revenue so far: the monthly CTE, a running total with an explicit ROWS frame, and pct_done (running total over SUM(revenue) OVER ()).
  • Step 2: month over month: LAG and a three-month moving average sharing one named window (WINDOW w AS (ORDER BY month_num), then OVER (w ROWS BETWEEN ...)).
  • Done in the room = both queries print, the running total ends at 237.29 and pct_done at 100.0.
  • Goes home: Step 3 (best-seller leaderboard with DENSE_RANK), Step 4 (top customer per city with ROW_NUMBER), Step 5 (run the whole file).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Does the ORDER BY inside OVER sort the result? Answer: no, it orders the rows for the function; always end with the query's own ORDER BY.
  • Why does January's three-month average equal January? Answer: there are no earlier rows in its frame.
  • Which function do you want for "every book at the top two sales levels"? Answer: DENSE_RANK (ties included, no gaps).
  • A WHERE that filters a plain column, like WHERE is_active: does it break a window? Answer: no: it runs first and decides which rows enter the windows (the lesson's Exercise 1).

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12.

Answers: Q7 C) 10; Q8 RANK 4, DENSE_RANK 3; Q9 B) Compute the rank in a CTE, then filter on it in the outer query; Q10 D) The default frame is RANGE: rows with the same ORDER BY value are added in one step; Q11 Two rows: order 3, 2026-03-10, next_order 2026-03-28; order 7, 2026-03-28, next_order NULL; Q12 A) ROW_NUMBER() with a tie-breaker, e.g. ORDER BY salary DESC, id. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: keep the three-function table (1, 2, 3, 4 / 1, 2, 2, 4 / 1, 2, 2, 3) on the board. For the project, Step 1 only, typed from the lesson page, then change one thing (the frame) and watch what moves.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (cheapest book per author with two windows) and the NTILE bonus challenge; ask them to rewrite the 5.1 self-join with LAG and compare the plans with EXPLAIN (a preview of Session 9).

Debug Clinic

2:35

Debug Clinic: Both Lessons

15 min
How it runs
  • Pairs (8m): SOURCE labs/module-08/l5_1-ctes/debug_ctes.sql and labs/module-08/l5_2-window-functions/debug_windows.sql. For each ERROR line: the number, the cause in one sentence, the fix in one line, written in the workbook.
  • Whole room (5m): one volunteer per error reads the line and the fix. Then everyone rewrites debug_windows.sql query A with the CTE pattern.
  • Close (2m): "Six errors, three rules: RECURSIVE when a CTE names itself; a stop condition that becomes false; windows are filtered one step later."
What the handouts print, and the fixes
ERROR 1146 (42S02): Table 'bookstore.countdown' doesn't exist
ERROR 3636 (HY000): Recursive query aborted after 1001 iterations. Try increasing @@cte_max_recursion_depth to a larger value.
ERROR 1406 (22001): Data too long for column 'path' at row 1
ERROR 3593 (HY000): You cannot use the window function 'row_number' in this context.'
ERROR 1064 (42000): You have an error in your SQL syntax; check the manual that corresponds to your MySQL server version for the right syntax to use near 'rank
FROM books
ORDER BY price DESC
LIMIT 3' at line 1
ERROR 6037 (HY000): 'QUALIFY clause' can be used only if the hypergraph optimizer is enabled.
  • A, 1146: WITH countdown refers to itself: add RECURSIVE.
  • B, 3636: no stop condition: WHERE d < '2026-12-31' in the recursive member (365 rows).
  • C, 1406: the anchor's 'Maria' made a five-character column: CAST('Maria' AS CHAR(200)) (or CAST(first_name AS CHAR(200))).
  • 3593: a window in WHERE: number the rows in a CTE, keep pos <= 3 outside.
  • 1064: rank is reserved: AS rank_num.
  • 6037: QUALIFY needs the hypergraph optimizer on MySQL 9.7; use the CTE pattern (it works on every server).

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Learners answer in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Sort into "got it" and "not yet" before Session 9: question 3's pile tells you whether to open Session 9's warm-up with the CTE-filter pattern.

  1. Write the skeleton of a recursive CTE that counts from 1 to 10, and label the anchor, the recursive member and the stop condition. Answer: WITH RECURSIVE s AS (SELECT 1 AS n /* anchor */ UNION ALL SELECT n + 1 FROM s /* recursive member */ WHERE n < 10 /* stop */) SELECT n FROM s;
  2. GROUP BY department versus PARTITION BY department: what is the difference in the rows you get back? Answer: GROUP BY returns one row per department; PARTITION BY keeps every employee row and adds the department's figure to each.
  3. Why can't WHERE filter on ROW_NUMBER(), and what do you do instead? Answer: window functions are computed after WHERE (error 3593); compute the number in a CTE and filter it in the outer query.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both lesson projects. org_report.sql Steps 2, 4 and 5; sales_dashboard.sql Steps 3 to 5. Each ends with SOURCE running the whole file without an error.
  • Lesson exercises. 5.1: Exercises 1 to 3 (above-average sellers, a price ladder, Daniel's team level by level); 4 is a stretch. 5.2: Exercises 1 to 3 (pay rank by department, each order's share, who comes back and when); 4 is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes on the lesson pages (answers in the Answer Key).
  • Journal: both entries (a chain in your own life as a recursive CTE; a ranking you know and its tie rule).
  • Labs not finished in class: labs/module-08/l5_1-ctes/category_steps_starter.sql, labs/module-08/l5_2-window-functions/order_trends_starter.sql, and the debug handouts if the clinic was cut.
  • Before the Next Lesson (from 5.2): run sales_dashboard.sql from a fresh session; rewrite one query with a named WINDOW.
Bring list for Session 9
  • A laptop that printed READY for Part 2, and bookstore.sql in the course folder: Lesson 6.1 reloads it and then generates a 100,000-row table (a few seconds; nothing to download).
  • The course folder with labs/ (Session 9's starters are in labs/module-09/), org_report.sql and sales_dashboard.sql.
  • The workbook and the journal.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: the environment check; 5.1 demo steps 6 to 8; the 5.2 ranking prediction and top N per group; the staff-tree lab through TODO 2; the leaderboards lab through TODO 2.
  • Cut first (saves 15m): the debug clinic: hand out both debug files with the README's expected errors as homework.
  • Cut second (saves 10m): the 5.1 project start: read Step 1's goal aloud and send all five steps home.
  • Cut third (saves 3m): 5.2 demo step 9 (the RANGE trap): self-check Q10 and the lesson's Common Mistake 4 cover it.
Running short: what to add

  • Rewrite the 5.1 month comparison with LAG and put both versions side by side.
  • The NTILE bonus: three price tiers, and which tier gets the extra books.
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 9: Indexes and Views

How to teach indexes and views in one session: measure a slow query on 100,000 rows, add the index the plan points at and measure again, learn the habits that hide a column from its index, then save the best queries as views, make some of them safe to update, and use one as a security boundary. A clinic ties them together: a view is exactly as fast as its query.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 6.1–6.2 Session 9 of 14 · Part 2

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm as every session: frame → live demo (you type, they follow) → guided lab → start the lesson's own project. Exercises, quizzes, journals and the rest of each project go home.
  • Every SQL block below was run on a freshly loaded bookstore on MySQL 9.7 to produce the output printed under it. EXPLAIN shows ESTIMATES (cost, rows) and EXPLAIN ANALYZE real times: learners' numbers differ; the plan's shape (Table scan, Index lookup, range scan) must not.
  • Keep lesson_14.html (6.1) and lesson_15.html (6.2) open for the plan-word table and the updatable-view rules. Labs live in labs/module-09/.
  • Reloads today. Lesson 6.1 starts from a fresh bookstore and adds sales_log; Lesson 6.2 starts by reloading bookstore.sql, which removes it. Say so at the break: everyone types SOURCE bookstore.sql before the 6.2 demo. The labs build and drop their own practice tables.
  • Teal blocks are hands-on, amber blocks are watch-outs. Lesson codes (6.1, 6.2) match the course site.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Files to hand out: labs/module-09/ without the *_solution.sql files; the workbook.
  • ☐ Run the kit check once: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-09 (10 files, a few seconds; two lines show a WARN for warning 1739, which is the point of that lab).
  • ☐ Projector machine: a freshly loaded bookstore, the client at 18 pt, lesson_14.html open at the sales_log setup (you paste the generator from there). Run the generator once beforehand to see it takes about a second on your machine.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: the loop "measure → index → measure again"; a three-level B-tree sketch (root, branches, leaf pages); the plan words "Table scan · Index lookup · Index range scan · Covering · Rows fetched before execution"; for 6.2 a window in a wall.
  • ☐ Print or share: the workbook; sticky notes for the exit ticket.
Known traps today
SymptomCauseFix
EXPLAIN prints a wide box instead of the treeThe statement ended with ; instead of \GEnd with \G; or EXPLAIN FORMAT=TRADITIONAL for the table.
ERROR 1064 near ANALYZE on MariaDBMariaDB has no EXPLAIN ANALYZE (and no FORMAT=TREE)Use ANALYZE SELECT ... there; read the traditional table.
Learners' costs and times differ from the projectorEstimates come from sampled statistics; times from the machineCompare the shape and the actual row counts, not the numbers.
The index exists, the plan still says Table scanA function or a type conversion around the column, a leading %, or a tiny tableThe lesson's "What stops an index" list, in order; then ANALYZE TABLE.
ERROR 1050 ... already exists when rerunning a view scriptCREATE VIEW instead of CREATE OR REPLACE VIEWCREATE OR REPLACE makes scripts rerunnable.
Views vanishSOURCE bookstore.sql drops the whole database, views includedKeep them in reporting_views.sql and rerun it after a reload.
On MariaDB ALTER INDEX ... INVISIBLE is a 1064MariaDB says IGNOREDALTER TABLE t ALTER INDEX i IGNORED; there.

Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-up: CTEs and Window Functions

10 min
Sequence
  • Retrieval (7m). Five questions on the workbook's Warm-up box, no notes, three minutes; then take them one by one. Run W1 and W2 on the projector after the answers.
  • Homework check (3m). Hands up: who ran org_report.sql and sales_dashboard.sql from a fresh session without an error? One volunteer reads the dashboard's leaderboard: 1984 alone in first place. Collect questions for the break.
Warm-up answers (W1 to W5)
  1. W1 What does this print? Answer: 1,3,5,7: the round that reads 7 fails n < 7 and stops.
  2. W2 Which price_level do The Catcher in the Rye and The Great Gatsby (both 8.99) get, and Animal Farm after them? Answer: Both 2, Animal Farm 3: DENSE_RANK ties without a gap (RANK would give Animal Farm 4).
  3. W3 Why can't WHERE filter on ROW_NUMBER(), and what do you write instead? Answer: Windows are computed after WHERE (3593): number the rows in a CTE, filter in the outer query.
  4. W4 In a recursive CTE over employees, which join walks UP the hierarchy? Answer: e.id = cte.manager_id ("who is this person's manager?"); e.manager_id = cte.id walks down.
  5. W5 AVG(salary) with GROUP BY department versus AVG(salary) OVER (PARTITION BY department): how many rows from the 10 employees? Answer: 4 versus 10: GROUP BY collapses, OVER keeps every row.

W1 and W2's real output is in the Answer Key. W3 misses mean the CTE-filter pattern did not stick: it comes back today as a view that filters a window (the 6.2 worked example).

Lesson Plans

6.1

Indexes & Query Optimization

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain what an index is, what it costs, and which indexes every bookstore table already has
  • Read a query plan from EXPLAIN and measure a query with EXPLAIN ANALYZE
  • Create single-column, composite and unique indexes, and choose the column order of a composite index
  • Spot and rewrite the patterns that stop an index from being used: functions on the column, leading wildcards, type mismatches
  • List, rename, hide and drop indexes, and fix the errors MySQL raises while you do it
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the textbook index; B-tree; costs; the measure → index → measure loop
25mLive demo: sales_log; before/after; composite; unique; two killers; invisible; a silent duplicate; 1553
15mGuided lab: plan_reading_starter.sql
10mStart the lesson project: tune_sales.sql Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Hold up any textbook: "To find 'foreign keys' you do not read the whole book. You look the term up in a sorted list at the back and jump to the pages. An index is that list: a sorted copy of one or more columns, each value with a pointer to its row. Without it, MySQL has one way to answer WHERE: read every row. That is a full table scan."

Point at the B-tree sketch: "Sorted pages in a shallow tree: three or four levels for a million values. The primary key is special: the rows themselves are stored in its order."

Then the price, ticked off on fingers: space, slower writes (every INSERT updates every index), more choices for the optimizer. "The goal is not 'index everything'. It is: measure the slow query, index what the plan points at, measure again."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

The lesson's own examples on a freshly loaded bookstore; learners generate the same sales_log and follow. EXPLAIN ANALYZE times are from the run that produced this guide.

  1. A table big enough to notice (3m). "Eleven books never make a query slow. This builds the lesson's 100,000-row sales log: a CTE and a cross join, every value computed from a running number, so everyone gets the same rows." Paste it from the lesson page; while it runs (about a second), explain ANALYZE TABLE: fresh statistics for the optimizer. 
    CREATE TABLE sales_log (
    id          INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    receipt_no  CHAR(7)     NOT NULL,
    customer_id INT         NOT NULL,
    book_id     INT         NOT NULL,
    sold_on     DATE        NOT NULL,
    quantity    INT         NOT NULL,
    channel     VARCHAR(10) NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO sales_log (receipt_no, customer_id, book_id, sold_on, quantity, channel)
WITH digits AS (
    SELECT 0 AS d UNION ALL SELECT 1 UNION ALL SELECT 2 UNION ALL SELECT 3 UNION ALL SELECT 4
    UNION ALL SELECT 5 UNION ALL SELECT 6 UNION ALL SELECT 7 UNION ALL SELECT 8 UNION ALL SELECT 9
),
seq AS (
    SELECT 1 + a.d + 10 * b.d + 100 * c.d + 1000 * e.d + 10000 * f.d AS n
    FROM digits a
    CROSS JOIN digits b CROSS JOIN digits c CROSS JOIN digits e CROSS JOIN digits f
)
SELECT
    LPAD(n, 7, '0'),                               -- receipt 0000001, 0000002, ...
    1 + MOD(n * 7, 8),                             -- customers 1 to 8
    1 + MOD(n * 13, 11),                           -- books 1 to 11
    DATE '2020-01-01' + INTERVAL MOD(n * 37, 2400) DAY,
    1 + MOD(n, 3),                                 -- 1 to 3 copies
    ELT(1 + MOD(n, 4), 'web', 'store', 'phone', 'web')
FROM seq
ORDER BY n;
ANALYZE TABLE sales_log;
    +---------------------+---------+----------+----------+
| Table               | Op      | Msg_type | Msg_text |
+---------------------+---------+----------+----------+
| bookstore.sales_log | analyze | status   | OK       |
+---------------------+---------+----------+----------+
  2. How big? (1m). 
    SELECT COUNT(*) AS sales, MIN(sold_on) AS first_sale, MAX(sold_on) AS last_sale
FROM sales_log;
    +--------+------------+------------+
| sales  | first_sale | last_sale  |
+--------+------------+------------+
| 100000 | 2020-01-01 | 2026-07-27 |
+--------+------------+------------+
  3. The indexes you already have (2m). "Three automatic sources: every primary key, every UNIQUE, every foreign key." Point at fk_orders_customer: that is why Lesson 4.3's joins were fast. 
    SELECT TABLE_NAME, INDEX_NAME, COLUMN_NAME, NON_UNIQUE
FROM information_schema.STATISTICS
WHERE TABLE_SCHEMA = 'bookstore'
  AND TABLE_NAME IN ('books', 'customers', 'orders', 'order_items')
ORDER BY TABLE_NAME, INDEX_NAME, SEQ_IN_INDEX;
    +-------------+--------------------+-------------+------------+
| TABLE_NAME  | INDEX_NAME         | COLUMN_NAME | NON_UNIQUE |
+-------------+--------------------+-------------+------------+
| books       | fk_books_author    | author_id   |          1 |
| books       | PRIMARY            | id          |          0 |
| customers   | email              | email       |          0 |
| customers   | PRIMARY            | id          |          0 |
| order_items | fk_items_book      | book_id     |          1 |
| order_items | fk_items_order     | order_id    |          1 |
| order_items | PRIMARY            | id          |          0 |
| orders      | fk_orders_customer | customer_id |          1 |
| orders      | PRIMARY            | id          |          0 |
+-------------+--------------------+-------------+------------+
  4. Measure (3m). "EXPLAIN ANALYZE runs the query and times every step. Read from the most indented line up: a table scan of 100,000 rows feeds a filter that keeps 42." Write the total time on the board. 
    EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM sales_log WHERE sold_on = '2025-03-15'\G
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Filter: (sales_log.sold_on = DATE'2025-03-15')  (cost=10078 rows=9990) (actual time=0.239..16.5 rows=42 loops=1)
    -> Table scan on sales_log  (cost=10078 rows=99900) (actual time=0.134..13.4 rows=100000 loops=1)
    On the run that produced this guide the total was 16.5 ms; yours will differ.
  5. Index, measure again (3m). "One line instead of two: an Index lookup straight to the 42 rows." Compare with the number on the board. 
    CREATE INDEX idx_sales_sold_on ON sales_log (sold_on);
EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM sales_log WHERE sold_on = '2025-03-15'\G
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Index lookup on sales_log using idx_sales_sold_on (sold_on = DATE'2025-03-15')  (cost=14.7 rows=42) (actual time=0.075..0.0779 rows=42 loops=1)
    Here 0.0779 ms. A drop of a hundred times or more is typical; the estimate (rows=42) is now exact.
  6. A composite index (3m). "Sorted by customer, then by date within each customer, like a phone book. Customer 3 in March is one slice." Covering: COUNT(*) needs nothing but the indexed columns. 
    CREATE INDEX idx_sales_customer_date ON sales_log (customer_id, sold_on);
EXPLAIN SELECT COUNT(*) FROM sales_log
WHERE customer_id = 3
  AND sold_on BETWEEN '2025-03-01' AND '2025-03-31'\G
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Aggregate: count(0)  (cost=73.1 rows=1)
    -> Filter: ((sales_log.customer_id = 3) and (sales_log.sold_on between '2025-03-01' and '2025-03-31'))  (cost=34.6 rows=167)
        -> Covering index range scan on sales_log using idx_sales_customer_date over (customer_id = 3 AND '2025-03-01' <= sold_on <= '2025-03-31')  (cost=34.6 rows=167)
  7. A unique index (2m). "An index plus a rule. A constant on a unique index is found while planning." Then the rule in action. 
    CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_sales_receipt ON sales_log (receipt_no);
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM sales_log WHERE receipt_no = '0004242'\G
INSERT INTO sales_log (receipt_no, customer_id, book_id, sold_on, quantity, channel)
VALUES ('0004242', 1, 1, '2026-07-28', 1, 'web');
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Rows fetched before execution  (cost=0..0 rows=1)
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry '0004242' for key 'sales_log.uq_sales_receipt'
  8. Killer 1: a function around the column (3m). "The index is sorted by sold_on, not by YEAR(sold_on)." Same question as a range on the bare column. 
    EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM sales_log
WHERE YEAR(sold_on) = 2025 AND MONTH(sold_on) = 3\G
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM sales_log
WHERE sold_on >= '2025-03-01' AND sold_on < '2025-04-01'\G
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Filter: ((year(sales_log.sold_on) = 2025) and (month(sales_log.sold_on) = 3))  (cost=10078 rows=99900)
    -> Table scan on sales_log  (cost=10078 rows=99900)
*************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Index range scan on sales_log using idx_sales_sold_on over ('2025-03-01' <= sold_on < '2025-04-01'), with index condition: ((sales_log.sold_on >= DATE'2025-03-01') and (sales_log.sold_on < DATE'2025-04-01'))  (cost=581 rows=1291)
  9. Killer 2: a number for a text column (1m). "receipt_no is CHAR(7). 4242 is a number, so every row is converted." Fix: compare with '0004242'. 
    EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM sales_log WHERE receipt_no = 4242\G
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Filter: (sales_log.receipt_no = 4242)  (cost=10078 rows=9990)
    -> Table scan on sales_log  (cost=10078 rows=99900)
  10. Test a drop safely (2m). "Before dropping an index on a busy table, hide it. The optimizer ignores it; writes still maintain it, so VISIBLE is instant." 
    ALTER TABLE sales_log ALTER INDEX idx_sales_sold_on INVISIBLE;
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM sales_log WHERE sold_on = '2025-03-15'\G
ALTER TABLE sales_log ALTER INDEX idx_sales_sold_on VISIBLE;
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Filter: (sales_log.sold_on = DATE'2025-03-15')  (cost=10078 rows=41.6)
    -> Table scan on sales_log  (cost=10078 rows=99900)
    Back to a table scan: this query does need the index.
  11. Silent: an index you did not need (1m). "No error, no warning, and pure overhead: the composite index already starts with customer_id." 
    CREATE INDEX idx_sales_customer ON sales_log (customer_id);
SHOW WARNINGS;
DROP INDEX idx_sales_customer ON sales_log;
    Empty set
    Then the one MySQL refuses: the foreign key's own index (next step).
  12. Refused: the foreign key's index (1m). 
    DROP INDEX fk_orders_customer ON orders;
    ERROR 1553 (HY000): Cannot drop index 'fk_orders_customer': needed in a foreign key constraint
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Reading the plan top down. Learners report "it filters first". Coach: "Which line is indented most? That one runs first and feeds the line above."
  • Composite index in the wrong order. (sold_on, customer_id) for "customer 3 in March". Coach: "Equality first, range last: which column do you compare with =?" (the lab's TODO 2).
  • Comparing numbers instead of shapes. "My cost is 10083, yours is 10078." Coach: "Same shape? Then same plan. Estimates come from sampled statistics."
Guided lab

File: labs/module-09/l6_1-indexes/plan_reading_starter.sql; reference labs/module-09/l6_1-indexes/plan_reading_solution.sql. A new 100,000-row visit_log (website visits), generated like the lesson's sales_log and dropped at the end of every run.

  • SOURCE it as shipped (2m): about a second to build; every plan says Table scan, and the index list shows only PRIMARY.
  • TODO 1 with a prediction (4m): before removing the --, each pair writes what the second plan will say. Then run.
  • TODO 2 in pairs (5m): the composite index. Circulate for (visited_on, customer_id): run it anyway, and ask why the plan is now an index scan of a different shape.
  • Expected: 200 visits on June 1 through an Index lookup; customer 3 in June: 700 visits through a Covering index range scan on idx_visits_customer_on; TODO 3 Rows fetched before execution; four indexes at the end.
  • Debrief (2m): "Which plan words did you see? Which line ran first?"
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-09/l6_1-indexes/index_killers_starter.sql: four slow queries to rewrite, same answers, better plans.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is Tune the Sales Reports, tune_sales.sql: three slow reports, measured, indexed and measured again, with notes as comments. In the room: Steps 1 to 3 (10 minutes).

  • Step 1: report A (the ten most recent web sales): already fast, through an index made for another query. Write that conclusion as a comment.
  • Steps 2 and 3: report B with QUARTER() and YEAR() is a table scan; rewrite the dates as a range and compare the two plans.
  • Done in the room = the file holds reports A and B, each with its before plan as a comment, and B's fixed plan shows an Index range scan.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (report C's composite index on customer, book, date), Step 5 (is the old composite index redundant?), Step 6 (the final index list).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud:

  • Why does nobody create an index on the primary key? Answer: the table is already stored in primary-key order (the clustered index).
  • What does Covering index mean? Answer: the index alone holds every column the query needs; the table is never read.
  • When is a table scan the right plan? Answer: small tables, or a condition that matches a big share of the rows.
  • Why INVISIBLE before DROP? Answer: hiding is instant to undo; rebuilding a dropped index on a big table can take a long time.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6.

Answers: Q1 B) The primary key: the rows themselves are stored in its order (the clustered index); Q2 D) WHERE sold_on BETWEEN '2025-03-01' AND '2025-03-31'; Q3 WHERE sold_on >= '2024-01-01' AND sold_on < '2025-01-01'; Q4 A) The text column is converted to a number on every row, so the index order no longer helps; Q5 C) It succeeds without an error or a warning, and the new index is pure overhead; Q6 1553: the foreign key from orders to customers needs an index on customer_id, and this is the only one. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: a printed copy of the lesson's plan-word table next to the keyboard; in the lab, TODO 1 only, then compare plans with a partner.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (a functional index on YEAR(sold_on)) and the FULLTEXT bonus challenge; ask them why MATCH ... AGAINST('the') returns nothing.

6.2

Views

65 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain what a view stores (a query, not data) and read the definition MySQL saves
  • Create, query, replace and drop views, including views over joins and aggregates
  • Predict whether a view is updatable, and protect it with WITH CHECK OPTION
  • Use a view to hide sensitive columns, and grant access to the view alone
  • Recognize and fix the errors views raise (1050, 1288, 1356, 1369, 1393)
Timing in the room
10mFrame: a window in a wall; stores a query, not rows; three jobs (reuse, updatable slices, security)
20mLive demo: first view, SHOW CREATE VIEW, join and aggregate views, update through a view, CHECK OPTION, security, frozen SELECT *
20mGuided lab: report_views_starter.sql
15mStart the lesson project: reporting_views.sql Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Point at the window drawn on the board: "A view is a saved SELECT with a name. It holds no rows. Every time you read it, MySQL runs the saved query against the real tables and hands you the result, like a window that shows part of the room behind it. When the room changes, the view changes."

Then three jobs, one line each: reuse (a five-table join written once, read in one line), a safe slice to edit (updatable views with WITH CHECK OPTION), a security boundary (an account may read the view and not the table).

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Everyone types SOURCE bookstore.sql first (it removes sales_log). The lesson's own examples follow.

  1. Your first view (2m). "A view is a named, saved SELECT. Nobody needs to remember WHERE city = 'Chicago' any more." 
    CREATE VIEW v_chicago_customers AS
SELECT id, first_name, last_name, email
FROM customers
WHERE city = 'Chicago';
SELECT * FROM v_chicago_customers ORDER BY id;
    +----+------------+-----------+-------------------+
| id | first_name | last_name | email             |
+----+------------+-----------+-------------------+
|  2 | Bob        | Smith     | bob@example.com   |
|  7 | Grace      | Wilson    | grace@example.com |
+----+------------+-----------+-------------------+
  2. What MySQL saved (2m). "A rewritten copy of the query, plus three settings it filled in: ALGORITHM, DEFINER, SQL SECURITY DEFINER. Remember the last one: it makes the security trick work." 
    SHOW CREATE VIEW v_chicago_customers\G
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
                View: v_chicago_customers
         Create View: CREATE ALGORITHM=UNDEFINED DEFINER=`root`@`localhost` SQL SECURITY DEFINER VIEW `v_chicago_customers` AS select `customers`.`id` AS `id`,`customers`.`first_name` AS `first_name`,`customers`.`last_name` AS `last_name`,`customers`.`email` AS `email` from `customers` where (`customers`.`city` = 'Chicago')
character_set_client: utf8mb4
collation_connection: utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci
  3. A five-table join, written once (3m). Paste the CREATE VIEW from the lesson; no output on success. Then a question that reads like English. 
    CREATE VIEW v_order_details AS
SELECT
    o.id                                   AS order_id,
    o.order_date,
    CONCAT(c.first_name, ' ', c.last_name) AS customer,
    b.title,
    a.name                                 AS author,
    oi.quantity,
    oi.price_at_time,
    oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time         AS line_total
FROM orders AS o
JOIN customers   AS c  ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
JOIN books       AS b  ON b.id = oi.book_id
JOIN authors     AS a  ON a.id = b.author_id;
SELECT author, SUM(quantity) AS copies, SUM(line_total) AS revenue
FROM v_order_details
GROUP BY author
ORDER BY revenue DESC, author;
    +----------------+--------+---------+
| author         | copies | revenue |
+----------------+--------+---------+
| J.R.R. Tolkien |      5 |   64.95 |
| George Orwell  |      6 |   56.94 |
| Frank Herbert  |      3 |   44.97 |
| Harper Lee     |      2 |   23.98 |
| Ray Bradbury   |      2 |   20.98 |
| Jane Austen    |      2 |   15.98 |
| Aldous Huxley  |      1 |    9.49 |
+----------------+--------+---------+
    Money uses price_at_time, the price actually paid, never today's books.price.
  4. A view with aggregates (2m). "LEFT JOIN, COUNT(DISTINCT), COALESCE: everything a report needs. Now a manager who never wrote a join asks one line." 
    CREATE VIEW v_customer_summary AS
SELECT
    c.id                                       AS customer_id,
    CONCAT(c.first_name, ' ', c.last_name)     AS customer,
    COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)                       AS orders,
    COALESCE(SUM(oi.quantity), 0)              AS items,
    COALESCE(SUM(oi.quantity * oi.price_at_time), 0) AS total_spent
FROM customers AS c
LEFT JOIN orders      AS o  ON o.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN order_items AS oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.first_name, c.last_name;
SELECT customer, total_spent
FROM v_customer_summary
WHERE total_spent > 40
ORDER BY total_spent DESC;
    +---------------+-------------+
| customer      | total_spent |
+---------------+-------------+
| Alice Johnson |       58.95 |
| Dave Brown    |       45.96 |
| Frank Miller  |       41.96 |
+---------------+-------------+
  5. Updating through a view (2m). "Each row of this view is one customer row, so MySQL knows which row to change. The change lands in customers." Inside a transaction, rolled back. 
    START TRANSACTION;
UPDATE v_chicago_customers
SET email = 'grace.wilson@example.com'
WHERE id = 7;
SELECT id, first_name, email FROM customers WHERE id = 7;
ROLLBACK;
    +----+------------+--------------------------+
| id | first_name | email                    |
+----+------------+--------------------------+
|  7 | Grace      | grace.wilson@example.com |
+----+------------+--------------------------+
  6. MySQL's verdict (1m). "Grouping makes a view read-only; a five-table join is still updatable, one table per statement." 
    SELECT TABLE_NAME, IS_UPDATABLE, CHECK_OPTION
FROM information_schema.VIEWS
WHERE TABLE_SCHEMA = 'bookstore'
ORDER BY TABLE_NAME;
    +---------------------+--------------+--------------+
| TABLE_NAME          | IS_UPDATABLE | CHECK_OPTION |
+---------------------+--------------+--------------+
| v_chicago_customers | YES          | NONE         |
| v_customer_summary  | NO           | NONE         |
| v_order_details     | YES          | NONE         |
+---------------------+--------------+--------------+
  7. WITH CHECK OPTION (3m). "Without it, an update could move Bob to Boston and he would vanish from the view that changed him. With it:" 
    CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW v_chicago_customers AS
SELECT id, first_name, last_name, email, city
FROM customers
WHERE city = 'Chicago'
WITH CHECK OPTION;
UPDATE v_chicago_customers SET city = 'Boston' WHERE id = 2;
    ERROR 1369 (HY000): CHECK OPTION failed 'bookstore.v_chicago_customers'
  8. A view as a security boundary (3m). "The support team needs a staff directory without salaries. The view hides columns and rows; the account gets SELECT on the view only." The account is dropped again right away; Lesson 7.4 teaches users properly. 
    CREATE VIEW v_staff_directory AS
SELECT first_name, last_name, email, department
FROM employees
WHERE is_active = TRUE;
CREATE USER 'support_demo'@'localhost' IDENTIFIED BY 'Replace-With-A-Strong-Passw0rd!';
GRANT SELECT ON bookstore.v_staff_directory TO 'support_demo'@'localhost';
SHOW GRANTS FOR 'support_demo'@'localhost';
DROP USER 'support_demo'@'localhost';
    +-------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Grants for support_demo@localhost                                             |
+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| GRANT USAGE ON *.* TO `support_demo`@`localhost`                              |
| GRANT SELECT ON `bookstore`.`v_staff_directory` TO `support_demo`@`localhost` |
+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
    Without a grant on employees, SELECT salary FROM employees would fail for that account with 1142.
  9. Silent: SELECT * is frozen (2m). "The table gains in_stock; the view does not, because * was expanded when it was created." 
    CREATE TABLE demo_products (id INT PRIMARY KEY, name VARCHAR(50), cost DECIMAL(8, 2));
INSERT INTO demo_products VALUES (1, 'Bookmark', 1.50), (2, 'Tote bag', 12.00);
CREATE VIEW v_demo_all AS SELECT * FROM demo_products;
ALTER TABLE demo_products ADD COLUMN in_stock BOOLEAN DEFAULT TRUE;
SELECT * FROM v_demo_all ORDER BY id;
DROP VIEW v_demo_all;
DROP TABLE demo_products;
    +----+----------+-------+
| id | name     | cost  |
+----+----------+-------+
|  1 | Bookmark |  1.50 |
|  2 | Tote bag | 12.00 |
+----+----------+-------+
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Expecting a view to store data. "Do I need to refresh it?" Coach: "Insert an order, then read the view." (Predict P3 shows it.)
  • Updating an aggregate view (1288). Coach: "Which customer row would MySQL change to make orders = 5?" There is none.
  • CREATE VIEW in a script that runs twice (1050). Coach: "What should a rerunnable script say?" CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW.
Guided lab

File: labs/module-09/l6_2-views/report_views_starter.sql; reference labs/module-09/l6_2-views/report_views_solution.sql. Two new views: sales per author (all ten authors, zero included) and every order with its customer's city.

  • TODO 1 together (6m): inner joins drop the authors with no sales. LEFT JOIN plus COALESCE. Ask first: "how many authors come back now, and why?" (seven).
  • TODO 2 in pairs (8m): create v_city_orders with no GROUP BY inside; the grouping happens in the query that reads it.
  • Expected: ten authors from J.R.R. Tolkien 64.95 down to three with 0.00 (F. Scott Fitzgerald, Isaac Asimov with 0 books, J.D. Salinger); New York 5 orders, Chicago 4, Houston 2, (no city) 1; v_author_sales NO, v_city_orders YES in IS_UPDATABLE.
  • Debrief (2m): "Why is one view updatable and the other not?" (grouping).
  • Fast finishers: TODO 3, then labs/module-09/l6_2-views/guarded_views_starter.sql (CHECK OPTION, a read-only window, an account that may read only that window).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is A Reporting Layer, reporting_views.sql: four views the manager reads with plain SELECTs, in a script that runs twice without an error. In the room: Steps 1 to 3 (15 minutes).

  • Step 1: v_book_catalog with GROUP_CONCAT for the categories.
  • Step 2: v_order_totals (why the bookstore stores no order total: a preview of Session 10).
  • Step 3: v_book_sales, with The Great Gatsby and The Catcher in the Rye at 0 copies.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (a view on a view), Step 5 (IS_UPDATABLE for the layer), Step 6 (run it twice).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud:

  • Does a view make a query faster? Answer: no; it runs its query. Indexes on the base tables make it fast.
  • Where does ORDER BY go? Answer: in the query that reads the view.
  • What does WITH CHECK OPTION protect against? Answer: writes through the view that would push a row out of it (1369).
  • What happens to your views when you reload bookstore.sql? Answer: they are dropped with the database; keep them in a script.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12.

Answers: Q7 A) Only the query: the rows are computed every time the view is read; Q8 ERROR 1369 (HY000): CHECK OPTION failed 'bookstore.v_ny'; Q9 C) SELECT id, title, price FROM books WHERE price < 10; Q10 D) The old columns only, without color; Q11 MySQL ignores a view's ORDER BY when the outer query has its own, and the SQL standard promises no order from a view: sort where you read; Q12 B) Read the directory through the view, but not the employees table itself. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: keep the table-versus-view comparison from the lesson on screen. In the lab, TODO 1 only, then read the solution's v_city_orders aloud with a partner.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (a safe editing view with a check option) and the "fake a materialized view" bonus; then the 6.2 worked example (a view that filters a window function).

Clinic

2:25

Clinic: A View's Plan, and the Debug Handouts

25 min
Timing
8mEXPLAIN on a merged view (demo step: v_order_details) and on a grouped view (v_customer_summary)
12mdebug_indexes.sql and debug_views.sql in pairs: eight errors, cause and fix in the workbook table
5mWhole room: one volunteer per error
A view is as fast as its query

Run both on the projector (6.2 demo state). The first view is MERGED into the query: no trace of the view, and the base tables' indexes do the work (fk_items_order, PRIMARY). The second groups rows, so MySQL computes it into a temporary table first (Materialize) and filters after all the grouping. Say: "Views do not have indexes. On big tables, filter in the base query, or keep a summary table."

EXPLAIN SELECT customer, title FROM v_order_details WHERE order_id = 3\G
*************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Nested loop inner join  (cost=1.05 rows=1)
    -> Nested loop inner join  (cost=0.7 rows=1)
        -> Index lookup on oi using fk_items_order (order_id = 3)  (cost=0.35 rows=1)
        -> Single-row index lookup on b using PRIMARY (id = oi.book_id)  (cost=0.35 rows=1)
    -> Single-row covering index lookup on a using PRIMARY (id = b.author_id)  (cost=0.35 rows=1)
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM v_customer_summary WHERE total_spent > 40\G
*************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Table scan on v_customer_summary  (cost=2.5..2.5 rows=0)
    -> Materialize  (cost=0..0 rows=0)
        -> Filter: (coalesce(sum(tmp_field),0) > 40)
            -> Group aggregate: sum(tmp_field), sum(order_items.quantity), count(distinct orders.id)
                -> Sort: c.id, c.first_name, c.last_name
                    -> Stream results  (cost=29.2 rows=288)
                        -> Left hash join (oi.order_id = o.id)  (cost=29.2 rows=288)
                            -> Nested loop left join  (cost=4.65 rows=16)
                                -> Table scan on c  (cost=1.05 rows=8)
                                -> Covering index lookup on o using fk_orders_customer (customer_id = c.id)  (cost=0.275 rows=2)
                            -> Hash
                                -> Table scan on oi  (cost=0.129 rows=18)
What the handouts print, and the fixes
ERROR 1061 (42000): Duplicate key name 'fk_books_author'
ERROR 1091 (42000): Can't DROP 'idx_books_price'; check that column/key exists
ERROR 1553 (HY000): Cannot drop index 'fk_orders_customer': needed in a foreign key constraint
ERROR 1062 (23000): Duplicate entry 'New York' for key 'customers.uq_customers_city'
+------------+--------------------+-------------+
| TABLE_NAME | INDEX_NAME         | COLUMN_NAME |
+------------+--------------------+-------------+
| books      | fk_books_author    | author_id   |
| books      | PRIMARY            | id          |
| customers  | email              | email       |
| customers  | PRIMARY            | id          |
| orders     | fk_orders_customer | customer_id |
| orders     | PRIMARY            | id          |
+------------+--------------------+-------------+
  • 1061: the name fk_books_author is taken (by the foreign key's index). Fix: idx_books_price.
  • 1091: there is no index of that name (A failed). Fix: look it up with SHOW INDEX FROM books first.
  • 1553: the foreign key needs it. Fix: leave it; create another index starting with customer_id first if you want to replace it.
  • 1062: two customers already share a city (New York, the first duplicate found). Fix: a city is not unique; clean data before a UNIQUE index, or drop the idea.
ERROR 1050 (42S01): Table 'v_dbg_cheap' already exists
ERROR 1288 (HY000): The target table v_dbg_counts of the UPDATE is not updatable
ERROR 1369 (HY000): CHECK OPTION failed 'bookstore.v_dbg_cheap'
ERROR 1356 (HY000): View 'bookstore.v_dbg_flyer_prices' references invalid table(s) or column(s) or function(s) or definer/invoker of view lack rights to use them
+----+-------+-------+
| id | title | price |
+----+-------+-------+
|  2 | 1984  |  9.99 |
+----+-------+-------+
  • 1050: the view exists. Fix: CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW.
  • 1288: a grouped view is not updatable. Fix: change the base table.
  • 1369: 12.99 would push 1984 out of the "under 10" view. Fix: change the price in books, if that is really meant.
  • 1356: the view still names cost. Fix: CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW with unit_cost.

The checker runs these in a scratch database, so its messages name that database instead of bookstore.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)
  1. A query filters WHERE customer_id = 5 AND book_id = 9 ORDER BY sold_on. Write the one index that serves the filter and the sort. Answer: CREATE INDEX idx_sales_customer_book_date ON sales_log (customer_id, book_id, sold_on); (equality columns first, the sort column last: the lesson project's Step 4).
  2. Name two habits that stop MySQL from using an index on a column. Answer: any two of: a function or arithmetic around the column; a leading % in LIKE; comparing a text column with a number; OR across different columns.
  3. Give one reason to create a view and one thing a view does NOT do for you. Answer: reasons: reuse a long query by name, give a safe updatable slice, hide columns and rows from an account. Does not: store rows, have indexes, make the query faster.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both lesson projects. tune_sales.sql Steps 4 to 6; reporting_views.sql Steps 4 to 6 (run it twice in a row).
  • Lesson exercises. 6.1: Exercises 1 to 3 (predict the plan, rewrite for the index, index the staff directory); 4 is a stretch. 6.2: Exercises 1 to 3 (books over $10, customers who never ordered, find the dependents); 4 is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes (answers in the Answer Key) and both journal entries.
  • Labs not finished in class: labs/module-09/l6_1-indexes/index_killers_starter.sql, labs/module-09/l6_2-views/guarded_views_starter.sql.
  • Before the Next Lesson (from 6.2): run reporting_views.sql on a freshly loaded bookstore; look at a spreadsheet you use and find a value that repeats on many rows: Lesson 6.3 starts there.
Bring list for Session 10
  • A laptop with a working bookstore (bookstore.sql in the course folder): Lesson 6.3 builds its own practice database (normalize_lab) and Lesson 7.1 reloads the bookstore.
  • The spreadsheet value from the homework (a sign-up sheet, an expense list): we normalize one in the warm-up.
