Lesson Plans

7.2

Transactions

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Group changes with START TRANSACTION, COMMIT and ROLLBACK, and explain autocommit and implicit commits
  • Undo part of a transaction with SAVEPOINT and ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT
  • Predict what a second connection sees and when it has to wait, under MySQL's default REPEATABLE READ isolation
  • Lock rows on purpose with SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, and read lock errors 1205 and 1213
  • Write a stored procedure whose error handler rolls back the whole transaction and passes the error on
Timing in the room
10mFrame: all or nothing; ACID; autocommit and implicit commits
25mLive demo: COMMIT, ROLLBACK, two silent traps, two windows, a procedure with a handler
15mGuided lab: loyalty_points_starter.sql in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: checkout.sql, setup and Step 1
Mental model to say

Start with the gift card: "Move 30 dollars of store credit from Alice to Bob: subtract from Alice, add to Bob. If the server stops between the two, 30 dollars vanish. A transaction makes the pair one unit: the database keeps both changes or neither."

Then the board line: START TRANSACTION → your statements → COMMIT (keep all) or ROLLBACK (discard all). "Every UPDATE you ran in this course was already a transaction: autocommit wraps each statement in its own and commits it at once. START TRANSACTION switches that off until you decide."

Then ACID in one breath, pointing at the bookstore: atomicity (an order never exists without its items), consistency (the CHECK holds after every commit), isolation (a report never counts a half-placed order), durability (after COMMIT it survives a power cut). And the warning list on the board: CREATE, ALTER, DROP, TRUNCATE and account statements commit by themselves.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

This is the lesson's own sequence, from a fresh bookstore.sql. Learners type along in their own window; for the two-window step they open a second terminal. Ask for a prediction before every statement that prints something.

  1. The accounts table (2m). "Gift-card balances, one row per customer, and a CHECK that makes a negative balance impossible. The lesson creates this table; it is not in bookstore.sql." 
    DROP TABLE IF EXISTS accounts;
CREATE TABLE accounts (
    id      INT PRIMARY KEY,
    name    VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
    balance DECIMAL(10, 2) NOT NULL,
    CONSTRAINT chk_balance CHECK (balance >= 0)
);
INSERT INTO accounts (id, name, balance) VALUES
    (1, 'Alice Johnson', 150.00),
    (2, 'Bob Smith', 50.00),
    (3, 'Carol Williams', 0.00);
SELECT * FROM accounts ORDER BY id;
    +----+----------------+---------+
| id | name           | balance |
+----+----------------+---------+
|  1 | Alice Johnson  |  150.00 |
|  2 | Bob Smith      |   50.00 |
|  3 | Carol Williams |    0.00 |
+----+----------------+---------+
    Learners who type along: this is the lesson's Setup block, run after reloading bookstore.sql.
  2. COMMIT: a transfer that sticks (3m). "Move 30 dollars from Alice to Bob. Two UPDATEs that only make sense together. Watch the Query OK lines: each UPDATE changed one row." Then, before COMMIT is typed, ask: "if the power went out right now, what would the server keep?" (Neither change.) 
    START TRANSACTION;
UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 30 WHERE id = 1;
UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + 30 WHERE id = 2;
COMMIT;
    Query OK, 0 rows affected
Query OK, 1 row affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 1  Warnings: 0
Query OK, 1 row affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 1  Warnings: 0
Query OK, 0 rows affected
  3. Check it (1m). "After COMMIT the change is permanent and visible to everyone." 
    SELECT * FROM accounts ORDER BY id;
    +----+----------------+---------+
| id | name           | balance |
+----+----------------+---------+
|  1 | Alice Johnson  |  120.00 |
|  2 | Bob Smith      |   80.00 |
|  3 | Carol Williams |    0.00 |
+----+----------------+---------+
  4. ROLLBACK: a rehearsal (3m). "The nightmare: someone deletes every order line. Inside a transaction it is a rehearsal." Predict both counts first. 
    START TRANSACTION;
DELETE FROM order_items;
SELECT COUNT(*) AS items_inside FROM order_items;
ROLLBACK;
SELECT COUNT(*) AS items_after_rollback FROM order_items;
    +--------------+
| items_inside |
+--------------+
|            0 |
+--------------+
+----------------------+
| items_after_rollback |
+----------------------+
|                   18 |
+----------------------+
    Say the habit aloud: before a risky UPDATE or DELETE by hand, type START TRANSACTION first, check with a SELECT, then decide.
  5. Silent trap 1: a schema change inside a transaction (3m). "I zero Bob's balance, create a little audit table, change my mind and roll back." Predict Bob's balance. 
    START TRANSACTION;
UPDATE accounts SET balance = 0 WHERE id = 2;
CREATE TABLE demo_audit (note VARCHAR(100));
ROLLBACK;
SELECT id, balance FROM accounts WHERE id = 2;
    +----+---------+
| id | balance |
+----+---------+
|  2 |    0.00 |
+----+---------+
    The CREATE TABLE committed the open transaction before it ran, so ROLLBACK had nothing to undo. Repair it (no output): UPDATE accounts SET balance = 80.00 WHERE id = 2; DROP TABLE demo_audit;
  6. Silent trap 2: COMMIT after an error (3m). "Carol has 0.00 and sends Bob 40 by hand." Run it as one block, so the COMMIT runs after the error. 
    START TRANSACTION;
UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + 40 WHERE id = 2;
UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 40 WHERE id = 3;
COMMIT;
SELECT * FROM accounts ORDER BY id;
    ERROR 3819 (HY000): Check constraint 'chk_balance' is violated.
+----+----------------+---------+
| id | name           | balance |
+----+----------------+---------+
|  1 | Alice Johnson  |  120.00 |
|  2 | Bob Smith      |  120.00 |
|  3 | Carol Williams |    0.00 |
+----+----------------+---------+
    In MySQL a failed statement undoes only itself; the transaction stays open with Bob's +40 in it, and COMMIT saved it: 40 dollars from nothing (PostgreSQL would refuse the COMMIT). Repair: UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 40 WHERE id = 2;. The fix in code is a handler; at the prompt, ROLLBACK after any error.
  7. Two windows (5m). "Real databases serve many connections at once." Open a second terminal on the projector, side by side, label them A and B on the board. The table continues from the steps above (Alice 120, Bob 80, Carol 0); it comes from two real client sessions:
    WindowStatementWhat it shows (real output)
    ASTART TRANSACTION; UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 20 WHERE id = 1; SELECT id, balance FROM accounts WHERE id = 1;100.00: A sees its own change
    BSELECT id, balance FROM accounts WHERE id = 1;120.00 at once: the committed value, no waiting
    BUPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + 1 WHERE id = 1;waits, then ERROR 1205 (HY000): Lock wait timeout exceeded; try restarting transaction (5 s on the test server; 50 s on a default server)
    ACOMMIT;releases the row lock
    BSELECT id, balance FROM accounts WHERE id = 1;100.00: now B sees it
    Say: "readers never wait and never see unfinished work; writers wait for writers." If you commit in A while B is still waiting, B's UPDATE simply finishes: show that once if the room is with you. FOR UPDATE (a read that locks) and deadlocks (1213) stay on the slides.
  8. A procedure that cleans up after itself (3m). "At the prompt you decide; in a procedure the code must decide. The handler is two lines: ROLLBACK, then RESIGNAL." Paste this one (it is the lesson's; do not type 28 lines live) and walk through the handler, the SIGNALs and FOR UPDATE. 
    DROP PROCEDURE IF EXISTS transfer;
DELIMITER //
CREATE PROCEDURE transfer(IN p_from INT, IN p_to INT, IN p_amount DECIMAL(10, 2))
BEGIN
    DECLARE v_balance DECIMAL(10, 2);
    DECLARE EXIT HANDLER FOR SQLEXCEPTION
    BEGIN
        ROLLBACK;   -- undo everything this call did
        RESIGNAL;   -- and pass the original error to the caller
    END;
    IF p_amount IS NULL OR p_amount <= 0 THEN
        SIGNAL SQLSTATE '45000' SET MESSAGE_TEXT = 'Amount must be positive';
    END IF;
    START TRANSACTION;
    SELECT balance INTO v_balance FROM accounts WHERE id = p_from FOR UPDATE;
    IF v_balance IS NULL THEN
        SIGNAL SQLSTATE '45000' SET MESSAGE_TEXT = 'No such source account';
    END IF;
    IF v_balance < p_amount THEN
        SIGNAL SQLSTATE '45000' SET MESSAGE_TEXT = 'Insufficient funds';
    END IF;
    UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - p_amount WHERE id = p_from;
    UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + p_amount WHERE id = p_to;
    IF ROW_COUNT() = 0 THEN
        SIGNAL SQLSTATE '45000' SET MESSAGE_TEXT = 'No such destination account';
    END IF;
    COMMIT;
END //
DELIMITER ;
  9. A good call, and the hardest failing one (2m). "Alice sends Carol 5. Then Alice sends 5 to account 99, which does not exist: the debit succeeds, the credit finds no row." 
    CALL transfer(1, 3, 5.00);
SELECT * FROM accounts ORDER BY id;
    +----+----------------+---------+
| id | name           | balance |
+----+----------------+---------+
|  1 | Alice Johnson  |   95.00 |
|  2 | Bob Smith      |   80.00 |
|  3 | Carol Williams |    5.00 |
+----+----------------+---------+
  10. The failing call (1m). Predict Alice's balance after it. 
    CALL transfer(1, 99, 5.00);
SELECT * FROM accounts ORDER BY id;
    ERROR 1644 (45000): No such destination account
+----+----------------+---------+
| id | name           | balance |
+----+----------------+---------+
|  1 | Alice Johnson  |   95.00 |
|  2 | Bob Smith      |   80.00 |
|  3 | Carol Williams |    5.00 |
+----+----------------+---------+
    Alice still has 95.00: the handler rolled the debit back before passing the error on.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Committing after an error. "I got 3819, then typed COMMIT, and Bob still got his 40." Coach: "In MySQL a failed statement undoes only itself. After any error inside a transaction, ROLLBACK, or let a handler do it." (Self-check Q3 and the lab's debug file.)
  • Expecting a lock to block reads. "Why could B read the row A was changing?" Coach with the two columns on the board: readers get the last committed version and never wait; only writers (and FOR UPDATE) wait for the lock.
  • A transaction left open in a forgotten window. Someone's UPDATE hangs for 50 seconds. Coach: "Which window has an open transaction? COMMIT or ROLLBACK it." Closing a window rolls back, which also releases the locks.
Guided lab

File: learners open labs/module-11/l7_2-transactions/loyalty_points_starter.sql; your reference is labs/module-11/l7_2-transactions/loyalty_points_solution.sql. A new loyalty table (points per customer, never below zero) and the real orders: each TODO practices one demo idea on new data.

  • SOURCE it as shipped (2m): SOURCE labs/module-11/l7_2-transactions/loyalty_points_starter.sql from a client started in the course folder with bookstore as the database. Learners should see unchanged points, 12 orders before and after, and a table of zeros (TODO 3's slip, committed).
  • TODO 1 together (3m), TODOs 2 to 4 in pairs (8m). Each is a small edit and a new SOURCE. TODO 4 is the implicit-commit trap again: learners must move a line, not add one.
  • Expected: TODO 1 Frank 15 / Grace 25; TODO 2 inside the rehearsal 7 orders and 10 lines (the cascade took 8 lines with 5 orders), after the ROLLBACK 12 and 18; TODO 3 1: 350, 2: 170, 4: 10, 6: 15, 7: 25; TODO 4 Bob back at 170 and log_rows 0 (as shipped: 10 and 1, because the CREATE TABLE ended the transaction and the INSERT autocommitted).
  • Debrief (2m): "Which TODO would have hurt in production?" (3: a committed slip; 4: a rollback that silently did nothing.)
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-11/l7_2-transactions/return_item_starter.sql (a procedure with a handler; the failing calls are typed at the prompt), then labs/module-11/l7_2-transactions/debug_lost_points.sql (three hand-typed transactions: errors 3819 and 1305 and one silent bug).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is An All-or-Nothing Checkout: checkout.sql, a procedure that places an order and charges store credit, all or nothing. In the room (10 minutes): the setup and Step 1.

  • Everyone runs the lesson's Setup block (the accounts table) if they did not type along in the demo.
  • Step 1: learners create checkout.sql in their course folder, paste or type the procedure from the lesson page and SOURCE it. Walk the room for DELIMITER problems (a missing DELIMITER ; at the end leaves the client waiting).
  • Done in the room = CALL checkout(1, 11, 1, @order_id); runs and Alice's credit drops by 8.49.
  • Goes home: Steps 2 to 6: the failing calls (out of stock, unknown book, not enough credit, no account), the integrity audit, the gap in the order ids, and a file that runs twice.
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • After START TRANSACTION; UPDATE ...; who can see the change? Answer: only your own session, until COMMIT.
  • Which of these ends a transaction by itself: SAVEPOINT, CREATE TABLE, SELECT? Answer: CREATE TABLE (an implicit commit).
  • B's plain SELECT did not wait for A's lock. Why did B's UPDATE wait? Answer: writers need the row lock; readers read the committed snapshot.
  • Why RESIGNAL after ROLLBACK in a handler? Answer: so the caller still learns the original error.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q1 B) The server rolls it back when the connection ends; Q2 Two rows: id 1 qty 4, id 2 qty 8; Q3 C) ALTER TABLE books ADD COLUMN rating INT;; Q4 D) Nothing yet: B waits for A's row lock, and fails with ERROR 1205 if A holds it past the timeout; Q5 A) So the caller still receives the original error after the half-done changes are undone; Q6 D) Run the whole transaction again from the start. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: pair learners who are unsure in the terminal with a confident typist for the two-window step (one laptop, two windows, two people). For the lab, TODO 1 is a two-number edit: start everyone there.

Stretch: return_item_starter.sql and debug_lost_points.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (a safe cancel_order procedure) and the Bonus Challenge (a real deadlock, 1213, in two windows, and SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS\G).

7.3

JSON Columns

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Add a JSON column and explain how MySQL validates and stores what you put in it
  • Read values with ->, ->> and JSON_EXTRACT, and filter rows on them
  • Change parts of a document with JSON_SET, JSON_INSERT, JSON_REMOVE and JSON_ARRAY_APPEND
  • Turn rows into JSON with JSON_OBJECT and JSON_ARRAYAGG, and JSON back into rows with JSON_TABLE
  • Index a JSON path through a generated column, and decide when data belongs in real columns instead
Timing in the room
10mFrame: columns for what every row has, JSON for the flexible leftovers; paths, -> and ->>
25mLive demo: the lesson's attributes column from setup to an indexed path, and two silent mistakes
15mGuided lab: reader_prefs_starter.sql in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: catalog_feed.sql, the setup and Step 1
Mental model to say

Use the two columns on the board: "Every book has a title, a price, an author: columns. Only some books have a series, some won awards, some are illustrated, and marketing invents a new detail every month. A column per detail would be mostly NULL; a JSON column holds one small document per row for the flexible leftovers."

Then the path: "You reach inside a document with a path. $ is the whole document, $.format a key, $.awards[0] the first element." And the two arrows, written large: -> gives JSON (a string keeps its quotes), ->> gives plain text for people, comparisons and sorting.

Then the rule that decides everything: "If every row has it, you join on it, sum it or protect it with a constraint, it is a column. JSON is for optional, varied, mostly-for-display details. Design the tables first; JSON gets the leftovers."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

The lesson's own sequence, from a fresh bookstore.sql (learners reloaded it at the break). Ask for a prediction before each step that prints something.

  1. Add the column and fill it (3m). "Details that only some books have: a format, a series, awards. One JSON document per row, next to the ordinary columns." Learners reloaded bookstore.sql at the break; these are the lesson's four statements. 
    ALTER TABLE books ADD COLUMN attributes JSON;
UPDATE books SET attributes = '{"format": "paperback", "series": "Dune Chronicles", "awards": ["Hugo", "Nebula"]}' WHERE id = 1;
UPDATE books SET attributes = '{"format": "hardcover", "series": "Middle-earth", "illustrated": true}' WHERE id IN (6, 9);
UPDATE books SET attributes = '{"format": "paperback", "awards": []}' WHERE attributes IS NULL;
    Query OK, 0 rows affected
Records: 0  Duplicates: 0  Warnings: 0
Query OK, 1 row affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 1  Warnings: 0
Query OK, 2 rows affected
Rows matched: 2  Changed: 2  Warnings: 0
Query OK, 8 rows affected
Rows matched: 8  Changed: 8  Warnings: 0
  2. What was stored (2m). Point at Dune: "I typed format, series, awards. MySQL shows awards first: it parsed the text and sorted the keys. Never rely on key order." 
    SELECT id, title, attributes
FROM books
WHERE id IN (1, 2, 6)
ORDER BY id;
    +----+------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| id | title      | attributes                                                                         |
+----+------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|  1 | Dune       | {"awards": ["Hugo", "Nebula"], "format": "paperback", "series": "Dune Chronicles"} |
|  2 | 1984       | {"awards": [], "format": "paperback"}                                              |
|  6 | The Hobbit | {"format": "hardcover", "series": "Middle-earth", "illustrated": true}             |
+----+------------+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
  3. MySQL checks every document (1m). "JavaScript lets you write a bare key. JSON does not." 
    UPDATE books SET attributes = '{format: "ebook"}' WHERE id = 2;
    ERROR 3140 (22032): Invalid JSON text: "Missing a name for object member." at position 1 in value for column 'books.attributes'.
    Fix: double quotes around every key, or let JSON_OBJECT('format', 'ebook') write it for you.
  4. Two arrows (3m). "Every path starts at $. The single arrow gives JSON, quotes and all; the double arrow gives plain text." Predict which column has quotes. 
    SELECT
    title,
    JSON_EXTRACT(attributes, '$.format') AS extracted,
    attributes->'$.format'               AS arrow,
    attributes->>'$.format'              AS double_arrow
FROM books
WHERE id IN (1, 6)
ORDER BY id;
    +------------+-------------+-------------+--------------+
| title      | extracted   | arrow       | double_arrow |
+------------+-------------+-------------+--------------+
| Dune       | "paperback" | "paperback" | paperback    |
| The Hobbit | "hardcover" | "hardcover" | hardcover    |
+------------+-------------+-------------+--------------+
  5. Arrays and missing keys (2m). "[0] is the first element. 1984 has an empty array; the Hobbit has no awards key at all. A missing key is NULL, not an error." 
    SELECT
    title,
    attributes->>'$.awards[0]'          AS first_award,
    JSON_LENGTH(attributes->'$.awards') AS award_count,
    attributes->>'$.series'             AS series
FROM books
WHERE id IN (1, 2, 6)
ORDER BY id;
    +------------+-------------+-------------+-----------------+
| title      | first_award | award_count | series          |
+------------+-------------+-------------+-----------------+
| Dune       | Hugo        |           2 | Dune Chronicles |
| 1984       | NULL        |           0 | NULL            |
| The Hobbit | NULL        |        NULL | Middle-earth    |
+------------+-------------+-------------+-----------------+
  6. Is it in the array? (1m). "MEMBER OF asks: is this value one of the array's elements?" 
    SELECT title
FROM books
WHERE 'Hugo' MEMBER OF (attributes->'$.awards');
    +-------+
| title |
+-------+
| Dune  |
+-------+
  7. Change part of a document (2m). "JSON_SET returns a changed copy; UPDATE stores it." Mention the family: SET adds or replaces, INSERT only adds, REPLACE only replaces, REMOVE, ARRAY_APPEND. 
    UPDATE books
SET attributes = JSON_SET(attributes, '$.edition', '75th anniversary')
WHERE id = 6;
SELECT attributes->>'$.edition' AS edition, attributes->>'$.format' AS format
FROM books WHERE id = 6;
    Query OK, 1 row affected
Rows matched: 1  Changed: 1  Warnings: 0
+------------------+-----------+
| edition          | format    |
+------------------+-----------+
| 75th anniversary | hardcover |
+------------------+-----------+
  8. JSON from rows (2m). "An app wants JSON? MySQL can build it." Point out that numbers stay numbers and the keys come out sorted again. 
    SELECT JSON_OBJECT('id', id, 'title', title, 'price', price) AS item
FROM books
WHERE id <= 3
ORDER BY id;
    +-------------------------------------------------------------+
| item                                                        |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
| {"id": 1, "price": 14.99, "title": "Dune"}                  |
| {"id": 2, "price": 9.99, "title": "1984"}                   |
| {"id": 3, "price": 11.99, "title": "To Kill a Mockingbird"} |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
  9. JSON back to rows (3m). "Arrays are awkward to report on. JSON_TABLE makes one row per element." Read it aloud: for every book, take $.awards, one row per element, call the element award. 
    SELECT b.title, aw.award
FROM books AS b,
     JSON_TABLE(
         b.attributes,
         '$.awards[*]'
         COLUMNS (award VARCHAR(40) PATH '$')
     ) AS aw
ORDER BY b.title, aw.award;
    +-------+--------+
| title | award  |
+-------+--------+
| Dune  | Hugo   |
| Dune  | Nebula |
+-------+--------+
  10. Index a path (3m). "You cannot index the JSON column itself (error 3152), but you can index a generated column that pulls one value out." 
    ALTER TABLE books
    ADD COLUMN format VARCHAR(20)
        GENERATED ALWAYS AS (attributes->>'$.format') VIRTUAL,
    ADD INDEX idx_books_format (format);
SELECT title, format FROM books WHERE format = 'hardcover' ORDER BY title;
    +-------------------+-----------+
| title             | format    |
+-------------------+-----------+
| Lord of the Rings | hardcover |
| The Hobbit        | hardcover |
+-------------------+-----------+
  11. Ask the optimizer (1m). "MySQL even uses that index for the original JSON expression." 
    EXPLAIN SELECT title FROM books WHERE attributes->>'$.format' = 'hardcover'\G
    *************************** 1. row ***************************
EXPLAIN: -> Index lookup on books using idx_books_format (format = 'hardcover')  (cost=0.7 rows=2)
  12. Two silent mistakes (2m). "No error, wrong answer." A capital F, then the single arrow inside CONCAT. 
    SELECT title
FROM books
WHERE attributes->>'$.Format' = 'hardcover';
SELECT CONCAT(title, ' (', attributes->'$.format', ')') AS label
FROM books
WHERE id = 1;
    Empty set
+--------------------+
| label              |
+--------------------+
| Dune ("paperback") |
+--------------------+
    Keys are case-sensitive and a missing key is NULL, so the typo finds nothing and warns nobody. The quotes would end up on the web page: use ->> whenever a value leaves JSON.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • JSON written like JavaScript ({format: "ebook"}, single quotes inside): error 3140. Coach: "keys and text in double quotes; the whole document inside SQL's single quotes", or JSON_OBJECT writes it for you.
  • The single arrow where text was meant: quotes in labels, comparisons with '"paperback"'. Coach: "Is the value leaving JSON (display, CONCAT, WHERE with text)? Then two arrows."
  • A typo in a key, and no error: $.Format returns NULL everywhere. Coach: copy key names from the data (SELECT attributes FROM books LIMIT 1), keep one spelling, and protect important keys with a CHECK or a generated column.
Guided lab

File: labs/module-11/l7_3-json-columns/reader_prefs_starter.sql; reference labs/module-11/l7_3-json-columns/reader_prefs_solution.sql. Customers get a prefs column (newsletter, favorite genres, a nested contact object); the same ideas as the demo on new data. The file adds the column and removes it again at the end, so it can be SOURCEd after every edit.

  • TODOs 1 and 2 together (3m), 3 to 6 in pairs (10m). TODO 1 is the classic: a JSON true read with ->> is the text 'true'.
  • Expected: newsletter Alice, Frank, Grace; Classic Alice, Grace; TODO 3 true only for Alice; TODO 4 has_genres 0 for Dave (prefs without the key) but NULL for Carol, Eve and Hank (no prefs at all); TODO 5 Grace {"genres": ["Fantasy", "Classic", "Science Fiction"], "newsletter": true}; TODO 6 NULL 3, false 1, true 4.
  • Circulate for: = true instead of = 'true' (no rows: a JSON boolean is not the number 1), MEMBER OF (prefs->>'$.genres') with two arrows (an error: MEMBER OF needs a JSON array), and edits that leave the file half-run (ERROR 1060 next time: reload bookstore.sql).
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-11/l7_3-json-columns/order_documents_starter.sql (rows to JSON with JSON_OBJECT and JSON_OBJECTAGG, a supplier's JSON to rows with JSON_TABLE), then labs/module-11/l7_3-json-columns/debug_bad_json.sql (three errors, 3140, 3143 and 3152, and two silent bugs).
  • Debrief (2m): "Which of the six TODOs would a real column have caught with an error?" (TODO 4's missing key, and the debug file's misspelled key: a real column would raise 1054.)
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is The Catalog Feed: catalog_feed.sql, a rerunnable script that tags books, lists award winners and prints a JSON feed. In the room (10 minutes): the setup and Step 1.

  • The project builds on the lesson's own setup: the attributes column and its three UPDATEs, the generated format column and the chk_books_format CHECK. Learners who followed the demo have the first two; everyone runs the CHECK from the lesson page.
  • Step 1: start catalog_feed.sql with USE bookstore;, make Brave New World and Fahrenheit 451 ebooks with JSON_SET, run it.
  • Done in the room = both books show "format": "ebook" and a file_mb key.
  • Goes home: Steps 2 to 6 (titles per format, awards with JSON_TABLE, the feed, the series index, and a file that reloads the database and runs twice).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • attributes->'$.format' or attributes->>'$.format' in a WHERE with 'hardcover'? Answer: ->>: the text, without quotes.
  • What does a path to a key that is not there return? Answer: NULL, no error.
  • How do you make WHERE attributes->>'$.format' = 'hardcover' fast? Answer: a generated column for the path, with an index.
  • Price in JSON or in a column? Answer: a column: every book has one and every total uses it.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q7 C) Use ->> instead of ->; Q8 gift and 3; Q9 A) The document unchanged; Q10 B) A generated column for $.format, with an index on that column; Q11 {"id": 1, "price": 14.99, "title": "Dune"}; Q12 D) An optional gift-wrap note some orders have, with a color and a message. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: keep the two arrows on the board for the whole lab; for learners lost in the nesting, write the TODO 5 UPDATE as two separate UPDATEs first (JSON_ARRAY_APPEND, then JSON_REMOVE), then nest them.

Stretch: order_documents_starter.sql and debug_bad_json.sql; the lesson's Exercise 4 (price an incoming web order with JSON_TABLE, never trusting its prices) and the Bonus Challenge (a multi-valued index on the awards array).

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Put the three questions on the slide. Learners answer in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Sort them before Session 12: question 1's "not yet" pile gets the first minute of the Session 12 warm-up.

  1. Inside a transaction, an UPDATE fails with error 3819 and you then type COMMIT. What is saved? Answer: every change made before the failed statement: a failed statement undoes only itself. ROLLBACK after an error.
  2. Write the handler that makes a procedure undo its transaction on any error and still report the error. Answer: DECLARE EXIT HANDLER FOR SQLEXCEPTION BEGIN ROLLBACK; RESIGNAL; END;
  3. Name one bookstore fact that belongs in JSON and one that must stay a column, with a reason each. Answer (example): a book's series (only some books have one, display only) in JSON; its price in a column (every book has one, totals use it).
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both lesson projects. checkout.sql Steps 2 to 6 (the failing calls, the integrity audit, the id gap, a file that runs twice) and catalog_feed.sql Steps 2 to 6 (the feed and a file that reloads and runs twice).
  • Lesson exercises. 7.2: Exercises 1 to 3 (Exercise 3 needs two windows); Exercise 4 is a stretch. 7.3: Exercises 1 to 3; Exercise 4 is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes on the lesson pages (answers in the Answer Key).
  • Journal: both entries. The 7.2 prompt asks learners to explain the two-window experiment as if to a friend.
  • Labs not reached in class: return_item_starter.sql, debug_lost_points.sql, order_documents_starter.sql, debug_bad_json.sql.
  • Checkpoint 2: anyone who did not finish a part writes it at home and brings it; it is reviewed in the Session 12 warm-up.
  • Before the Next Lesson (from 7.3): reload bookstore.sql: Lesson 7.4 starts from the standard database, without the attributes column.
Bring list for Session 12
  • A laptop with MySQL 9.7, bookstore freshly reloaded, and the root password: Lesson 7.4 creates accounts as root and logs in as them.
  • A terminal you can open twice (two windows) and use outside the mysql client: Lesson 8.1's mysqldump runs at the shell prompt, not at mysql>.
  • checkout.sql and catalog_feed.sql (finished), and the Checkpoint 2 page.
  • The workbook and the journal. Session 12 ends with the final-project brief: bring a calendar for the two studio sessions.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: the full checkpoint; the two-window step; the handler; the two arrows.
  • Cut first (saves 5m): 7.3 demo steps 8 and 9 (JSON from rows and back): the fast-finisher lab covers them.
  • Cut second (saves 3m): the 7.2 rehearsal step; the lab's TODO 2 does the same.
  • Cut third (saves 10m): the 7.3 project start: learners run the lesson's setup and Step 1 at home.
Running short: what to add
  • FOR UPDATE live in two windows (the lesson's two cashiers): B's locking read waits, A commits, B sees the new balance.
  • Run debug_lost_points.sql for everyone and fix it together, one transaction per volunteer.
  • The 7.3 Bonus Challenge: a multi-valued index on the awards array and its EXPLAIN.
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 12: Users, Permissions and Backups

How to teach the twelfth session of MySQL Foundations: user accounts and least privilege (grants at three levels, logging in as each account, roles and the forgotten SET DEFAULT ROLE), then moving data in and out (a mysqldump backup proved by a test restore, the 9.7 GTID trap, a CSV export, a messy file through a staging table), and the hand-out of the final-project brief.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 7.4–8.1 Session 12 of 14 · Part 2 Final-project brief

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame → live demo → guided lab → start the lesson's own project. The lesson exercises, quiz and journal are homework.
  • Session 12 is the last teaching session of Part 2: it ends with a 20-minute final-project brief before the wrap-up, so each lesson gets 65 minutes in the room (the pages estimate 105 + 105 alone).
  • This session has two windows of a different kind: for 7.4 a second mysql client logged in as another account, for 8.1 a plain terminal for mysqldump. Every output below is real: the logins AS shop_reports, support_desk and tom were made with the mysql client on the kit's MySQL 9.7 test server; the shell commands ran there with the same options (shown as learners type them, -u root -p).
  • Keep lesson_19.html (7.4), lesson_20.html (8.1) and lesson_final_project.html (8.2, for the brief) open in browser tabs. Labs live in labs/module-12/; _solution.sql files are yours.
  • Safety. Never change root's password or privileges in class, and never let learners practice on a server other people depend on. Every lab account is a kit_ demo account that its own file drops again.
The #1 risk of Session 12: someone locks themselves out

Lesson 7.4 is the first lesson where a typo can lock a learner out of their own server: ALTER USER CURRENT_USER() IDENTIFIED BY ..., or a REVOKE aimed at root. Before: say it once at the start of 7.4 and write it on the board: "we only ever change kit_ and demo accounts". In the room: if someone does lose root anyway, do not fix it live; pair them up and help at the break (recovery means restarting the server with special options, Lesson 7.4's ground rules). After: the labs and the checker refuse root and SET GLOBAL, so homework is safe.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Bring list you can assume (from Session 11): a laptop with MySQL 9.7, bookstore freshly reloaded, the root password; a terminal that can be opened twice; checkout.sql and catalog_feed.sql finished; the Checkpoint 2 page; the workbook and the journal.
  • ☐ Checkpoint 2 marked with the rubric (Session 11's Answer Key), one sentence of advice per learner: you return it in the warm-up.
  • ☐ Files: copy labs/module-12/ without the *_solution.sql files into the shared folder (new_titles.csv included). For the brief: the link to lesson_final_project.html, or printouts of its Brief, What you hand in, Milestones and How Your Work Is Assessed sections. Keep the kit's final-assessment.html (the final-project rubric with instructor notes) for yourself.
  • ☐ Run the kit check once: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-12 from this kit's folder: 10 files, every line PASS.
  • ☐ Projector machine: MySQL 9.7 with a fresh bookstore; a root mysql window and a second terminal at 18 pt, side by side; the lesson's new_books.csv and price_update.csv saved in your server's secure_file_priv folder and in the course folder; a third terminal (plain shell) in the course folder for mysqldump. Find your folder now: SHOW VARIABLES LIKE 'secure_file_priv';.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: "GRANT what ON where TO whom"; the error ladder "1045 password · 1044 database · 1142 table · 1143 column · 3118 locked"; and "mysqldump = terminal · INTO OUTFILE / LOAD DATA = mysql>".
Known environment traps
SymptomCauseFix
ERROR 1819 ... does not satisfy the current policy requirementsThe validate_password component (some installers enable it)A longer password with mixed case, digits and a symbol; never uninstall the component in class.
Logging in as the demo account says ERROR 1045Typo in the password, or the account was created for another hostRetype; SELECT user, host FROM mysql.user; and use the exact pair.
mysqldump: command not found / not recognizedThe client tools' folder is not on the PATH (Session 1)Same PATH fix as for mysql; Docker: docker exec mysql97 mysqldump ....
ERROR 3546 ... GTID_PURGED cannot be changed on restoreA 9.7 dump without --set-gtid-purged=OFFDump again with the option (the demo shows it on purpose).
Windows: the restored file is gibberish or ERROR ... ASCII '\0'PowerShell's > wrote UTF-16--result-file=... for the dump; --commands -e "SOURCE file" for the restore.
ERROR 1290 for every INTO OUTFILENot the server's folderSHOW VARIABLES LIKE 'secure_file_priv'; and use that folder; NULL means file statements are disabled (the client-side methods still work).
ERROR 3948 or 2068 with LOAD DATA LOCALlocal_infile OFF on the server, or the client started without --local-infile=1The labs need neither; for the demo file, use the server-side LOAD DATA INFILE.
